# Johann Reuchlin
# Capnion <note>original: "Capnion" — This is the Greek translation of Reuchlin's name, meaning "little smoke," which he used as a scholarly pseudonym.</note>
### Or
### On the Miracle-Working Word. <note>original: "De Verbo Mirifico" — A reference to the "Wonder-Working Word" or the Tetragrammaton, the four-letter name of God in Hebrew tradition.</note>

<meta>This is the opening preface of Reuchlin's work. The previous page (not provided) likely contained the title page or a blank flyleaf. This page establishes the dedication and the intellectual motivation for the book.</meta>

<insert>
<unclear>3 G 35</unclear> <note>Shelf mark or library inventory number</note>
<unclear>From the Convent of the Scala</unclear> <note>original: "Del Convento della Scala"; likely referring to the church of Santa Maria della Scala in Rome</note>
</insert>

# THE WORK OF JOHANN REUCHLIN OF PFORZHEIM, DOCTOR OF LAWS: CAPNION, OR, ON THE MIRACULOUS WORD.

<margin>
By
</margin>

Certain curious explorers of secret matters, Dalberg, most sacred Bishop of Worms <note>Johann von Dalberg (1455–1503), a leading German humanist and Bishop of Worms (Vangionum)</note>, who are troubled by the hidden powers of words, the concealed energies of speech, and the characters of divine secret names, are found in our age (as far as I can rightly see) to have strayed not a little from the ancient footsteps of the leading philosophers. They often wander far astray regarding the operations of mysteries full of wonderful effects, chiefly for this reason: that either through the fading obscurity of the figures or the perverse and faulty corruption of the scribes, those symbols of that sacred philosophy and the venerable signs of supernatural virtues can no longer be understood, nor even read. Therefore, many, affected by weariness and useless, sluggish exhaustion, either flee from such a laudable kind of contemplation in horror, or abandon what they had long ago begun through neglect.

Wherefore, led by an incredible desire to please, and indeed by a singular love toward that eminent pillar of every good art, Johann Lapidanus <note>Jean Heynlin (c. 1425–1496), also known as Lapidanus, a scholar who helped establish the first printing press in Paris</note>, Doctor of Theology—distinguished both for his monastic life among the Carthusians and for his first-rate publication of books, under whose rod I first submitted my hands to be instructed in the humanities. I shall add also that man most learned in both laws and most adorned with polished letters, my friend of long standing, Sebastian Brant of Strasbourg <note>Author of "The Ship of Fools"</note>. And likewise Johann Amerbach <note>A famous printer in Basel</note>, outstanding in genius, noble in the marvelous craft of type-founding, and learned in various disciplines; both are most famous in the literary republic of learned men, so that you would not know whether they are more illustrious for the fame of their deeds or for their city. For they are held in no small praise among the highest citizens of Basel.

Moved, therefore, by the great bond of friendship with such great men, I have dared to enter such great darkness and the obscured hiding places of sacred—indeed, secret—words. I have dared to bring forth to our age, as if from the inner shrines of oracles and the innermost chambers of the most ancient philosophy, nearly all the names which in a former age wise men endowed with miraculous powers used in sacred rites. These may be the sacraments of the Pythagoreans and the more ancient philosophers, or the foreign <note>original: "barbara"; used here in the classical sense to mean non-Greek/non-Latin, specifically Hebrew and Near Eastern</note> memorials of the Hebrews and Chaldeans, or the devoted supplications of the Christians, which may be seen drawn from their books and languages in this work.

I have dedicated this to your name because sacred things are owed to sacred people, and especially priestly matters to the princes of priests, in which dignity you stand before others. This is not only because of the greatness of your dominion and power, in which you are most abundantly wealthy, but also because of your inexpressible skill in various literatures, to which your library—filled with Latin, Greek, and Hebrew volumes—bears witness. It is the one treasure of our Germany, which I have always been accustomed to use according to my heart's desire. Receive, therefore, this disputation of three philosophers concerning the Miraculous Word, whom I have represented as arguing among themselves (as the controversy of their sects required), so that the hidden property of the sacred names might shine forth more clearly. Concerning these, and concerning so many and such great—

<margin>
Argument of the work.
</margin>

He cites his friendships with notable humanists like Sebastian Brant and Johann Amerbach as inspiration for the project.</summary>

<meta>This page introduces the dialogue's participants and the central premise of the work: the search for the "Wonder-Working Word." It establishes the setting in Pforzheim and the lineage of the character Sidonius.</meta>

# ON THE WONDER-WORKING WORD
<note>original: "DE VERBO MIRIFICO"</note>

...at last, a easier opportunity might be offered to us for choosing the one thing that is truly supreme, miraculous, and beatific. And so that you may understand the whole matter through a brief summary: Sidonius—who was first thought to belong to the school of Epicurus <note>A Greek philosopher who emphasized pleasure and atomism; here implying Sidonius was once a materialist</note>, but was later found to have sworn his oath to no single master—is a philosopher of every variety. Driven by a desire for learning, he traveled abroad. After many travels, he entered Swabia <note>original: "Sueuiam," a region in southwestern Germany</note> and met two philosophers in the town of Pforzheim <note>original: "Phorce"</note>: Baruchia the Hebrew and Capnion the Christian <note>Capnion is the Greek-style pseudonym for the author, Johannes Reuchlin</note>. With them, he discussed various disciplines and, soon after, the very knowledge of things divine and human: concerning opinion, faith, miracles, the powers of words and figures, and the secret operations and mysteries of seals. In this manner, an enumeration of symbols is made from all nations that possess any excellent philosophy or noble ceremonies. Sacred names and consecrated characters are brought into question, examined diligently by each man according to the rite of his own sect, until at last, in the third book, Capnion gathers from all sacred things the one name **ihsuh** <note>The Pentagrammaton, a Renaissance kabbalistic rendering of the name of Jesus (YHShVH)</note>, into which the virtue and power of all sacred things are referred, and which is blessed forever and above all things. But now, let us hear Sidonius himself.

->**SIDONIUS**<-

If there is anything anywhere in human nature, my friends Baruchia and you, Capnion—both of you the very goal and boundary of my incredible desire, and the refuge for my long-pursuing wonder (for it pleases me to speak fearlessly only when you alone are commanding and listening)—which might produce perpetual pleasure for us, it is this one thing and, as I believe, it consists of everything: to perceive almost nothing else than things that are new every day and foreign to our senses, or things perceived at another time or in a different way than they were felt long ago. This is what usually entices our mind, which is greedy for beauty, to a will for experiencing by some most recent and wonderful marvel, and impels it into a task however arduous. By this, I could easily believe that not only my ancestral race (which clearly was famous for many centuries and now stands out for its skill in all things) but even the whole of Sidon, from which I have both my name and origin, and indeed my entire fatherland, was incited to labors—sweet yet hard—by either delightful novelty or stupendous antiquity. For from ancient times, the Phoenicians have been accustomed to indulge, more than can be said, in the investigation of various regions and the study of new things. By their own diligence, they were the first to invent letters, the benefit of which they bestowed upon the Greeks, who were perhaps not ungrateful men. And after an exquisite reckoning of numbers, they measured the lands and the heavens. They were likewise most expert in the natural and martial arts. Finally, that nation always saw both the customs and the cities of many men, a fact which resulted in the greatest glory for them in most parts of the world and a great proclamation above all others. By your leave, I am perhaps praising my ancestors too much, but love of country wins out, and nevertheless, the truth itself reveals it. For even that ancient dogma concerning atoms was first the work of a certain former countryman of mine, a Sidonian by the name of Mochus <note>A legendary Phoenician philosopher said to have lived before the Trojan War</note>. And this was before the Trojan War, so that you may learn how much the Phoenicians excelled in industry, in genius, and in the investigation of many affairs. Indeed, this was not hidden even from Lucan, who was no less a historian than a poet, when he sang:

> "The Phoenicians first, if fame be believed, dared
> To mark the enduring voice with crude figures.
> Not yet had Memphis learned to weave the river-reeds..." <note>A quote from Lucan's Pharsalia regarding the invention of the alphabet and papyrus</note>

<summary>The narrator introduces a dialogue between Sidonius, Baruchia, and Capnion regarding the mystical power of words, culminating in the revelation of the name "ihsuh." Sidonius begins by praising the Phoenician heritage of discovery and the invention of the alphabet.</summary>
<keywords>Kabbalah, Pentagrammaton, Phoenicians, invention of writing, Pforzheim, Johannes Reuchlin, philosophy of language</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the introductory dialogue of Book One. The speaker is recounting his extensive travels in search of ancient wisdom, moving from the East toward Germany to meet the renowned scholars Capnion (Reuchlin) and his companion.</meta>

# BOOK ONE
<note>original: LIBER PRIMVS</note>

->a iii<-
<note>This is a signature mark used by early printers to keep the pages in order.</note>

...he knew the [Egyptian] books, and that they preserved their magical languages only through animals, birds, and wild beasts carved in stone <note>A reference to Egyptian hieroglyphs, which Renaissance scholars believed were a secret, magical language of symbols.</note>. I was eager to follow the monuments of their praise and the traces of their path, to which (as naturally happens) the flow of my paternal blood finally led me. For many years now, I have been occupied amidst many things by sea and land, seeking out all the best masters of speech and of subjects, so that I might hear what was said from one and see what ought to be done from another. Nor was I content only with my entire homeland or the neighboring Three Cities <note>likely referring to the Tripoli region or a similar confederation of cities</note>, which is rightly the mistress of diverse and indeed universal arts—though it has now begun to be twisted by barbarian customs. Instead, I departed, I went out, I escaped, I broke forth further, traveling through Lebanon and Syria even more than that famous Menelaus, who informed his guests Telemachus and Pisistratus about his wanderings with these verses:

*I have indeed suffered not a few things, and arriving with many dangers*
*In my ships, the labor reached into the eighth year,*
*As far as Cyprus, then Phoenicia and the Egyptian fields,*
*Then through the Ethiopians and Sidonians and Erembi,*
*And Libya, where the horned lambs graze.*
<note>The author is quoting a Latin translation of Homer's Odyssey, Book 4.</note>

Thus, I did not lack labor in so long a journey through those same perils, until, having first left Britain, then Gaul, and then with the Rhine as my guide, my path was granted into Germany. I came so that I might see and prove both of you wise men, not so much with my eyes as with my ears. For I would adapt that saying of Plautus thus: *One witness who hears is worth more than ten who see*, especially in getting to know men who are philosophers, such as I have heard you are—most celebrated by fame everywhere. 

And let me speak first of you, **Capnion** <note>The Greek-style nickname for Johannes Reuchlin (1455–1522), a famous German humanist and scholar of Hebrew.</note>, whom I had learned was a native of **Pforzheim** <note>original: Phorcensem</note> from various inscriptions in your books and records. I was more often amazed by this than by anything else: that "wooded Swabia" <note>original: syluestris Sueuia</note> could produce such philosophy! At that time, there were certain companions on my journey, fellow students of the hidden arts, who responded that it should not be difficult to believe that such a rugged land could produce a certain excellent character. They reminded me of the former Bishop of Ratisbon <note>Regensburg</note>, who was called "Polyphemus" in the Greek tongue but was known by his family name as **Albert the Great** <note>Albertus Magnus (c. 1200–1280), a towering medieval philosopher and scientist.</note>. He seemed to surpass not only Aristotle but also the other primary followers of the Peripatetic philosophy <note>The school of philosophy founded by Aristotle.</note>, yet he was both born and raised in Swabia. I myself knew this to be true, for I had already read several remarkable books written in Latin by that man, which, because of their singular excellence, had been translated into the Greek language—an unusual honor. 

Finally, they brought forward no vain or fragile reasons for their belief: that your people easily migrate elsewhere, both because of the scarcity of food, the difficulty of tilling the fields, and the lack of money. I will add myself that the region cannot be accustomed to an abundance of trade goods. Therefore, I believe that among the migrating Swabians there will always be a wealth of the best teachers, since those who flee sterile lands must migrate to all the best places. Thus, the fact that you are powerful in learning, eloquence, and counsel is not because you were born in the forest—which they used to call the Hercynian or Bacenis, but now call the **Black Forest** <note>original: nigram</note>. Rather, as I hear from your friends, it is because you have surveyed not only ancient Paris <note>original: Lutetiam</note>, but also almost all the universities of Gaul and Germany, some in Italy, and Rome itself, the capital of studies. Nevertheless, I seem to see that the suitability of the Pforzheim territory contributes not a little to the talents of its natives, and that this is true is shown by the huge [number] of literate men...

<summary>The narrator describes his vast travels from the Levant to Germany in search of wisdom, eventually arriving in Swabia to meet the scholar Johannes Reuchlin (Capnion). He reflects on how a "rugged" region like the Black Forest could produce such great minds as Reuchlin and Albertus Magnus.</summary>
<keywords>Johannes Reuchlin, Albertus Magnus, Swabia, Pforzheim, travel, education, Black Forest, Aristotle, humanism</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>The previous page likely discussed the origins of names and the power of ancestral lineage. This page continues that theme by linking local geography to Trojan myth.</meta>

# ON THE WONDER-WORKING WORD
<meta>catchword: petus (seeks)</meta>

The number of ancestors thereafter creates the lineage. For the fatherland possesses a power of generative condition not much less than the father himself, if Porphyry <note>Porphyry (c. 234–305 AD) was a Neoplatonic philosopher who wrote on the origins of names and souls.</note> is to be believed. You have here a quite lengthy discourse from a newcomer and a stranger who attempts to discuss your own region. But so that you do not think me a sluggish investigator of that place—which for one man is a place of origin and for another a place of exile—it would certainly be fitting that I do not omit this very point which I discovered in Phoenicia from the most learned authors of ancient monuments.

For it is well established that in the time of Troy—not so much in the war as in the battle—Phorcys the younger was a leader of the Phrygian army, according to the testimony of Homer, who in the "Catalogue of Ships" says: *Phorcys was the leader of the Phrygians, along with Ascanius.* <note>original: "Phorcys ille Phrygū ductor fuit Ascaniusq́;" - a reference to the Iliad, Book 2.</note> Therefore, when Troy fell due to adverse fortune, those few survivors out of countless numbers who had preserved their lives were forced to seek other homes and hope for other kingdoms. Consequently, Aeneas, along with Ascanius and Phorcys—his companion in dangers—and many others, sought Italy. 

But while Ascanius was founding a town at the foot of the Alban Mount, and each man was designating a place for himself to stay in various locations, that Phorcys the younger—the grandson of the elder Phorcys (who was King of Sardinia and Corsica, said to have been defeated by Atlas and submerged in the sea) by Euryale—entered through Italy and the land of the Insubres <note>The Insubres were a Celtic people in Northern Italy (Milan area).</note> into Germany. Having crossed the great lake between the sources of the Danube <note>original: "Istri"</note> and the Rhine, he traveled through easier mountain passes and plains toward the setting sun. There he came upon a very clear river full of fish, and on its bank, a meager hut. 

Dragging his weary steps toward it, an old man, burdened by years and leaning entirely on a staff, caught sight of him. When the old man saw Phorcys marching through such a deserted forest with such a large household, he was terrified and thought he was nearly about to give up the ghost. When Phorcys asked what this stream was called, the old man replied that its name was **Aeneas**. 

Then Phorcys, astonished by that name and turning his face toward the river, soon added: *Are you not that Aeneas whom nurturing Venus bore to Dardanian Anchises by the wave of the Phrygian Simois?* <note>original: "Tu ne ille Aeneas quē Dardanio Anchisę Alma Venus Phrygii genuit Simoentis ad undā?"</note> Virgil <note>original: "Maro Virgilius"</note> later transferred these verses into his *Aeneid*, delighted by their ancient, foreign quality. 

Thus, he took this as an omen, and as a stranger, he decided he must stay there. He began to exhort his multitude not to despise the divine oracle he had discovered in the name of the Trojan priest. For to those by whom the original Aeneas had long ago been left behind at no small distance, another Aeneas now clearly revealed himself. Therefore, the Phrygian leader, with a royal spirit and custom, commanded everyone to strive as much as they could to clear the wilderness into level fields by uprooting the strength of the trees on all sides. In that place, he built a small town and a citadel, which he called **Phorcen** after his own name and that of his grandfather. So great was the devotion of that most grave, holy, and honorable man to Aeneas, that at the mere sound of the river being called Aeneas, he chose to settle there as if at a perpetual monument to the Trojan Aeneas. 

In the course of time, as the modest family became a populous people, and the narrowness of their first dwelling forced them to cut down neighboring forests, till the fields, build villages, and raise up cities, they say that besides these rural and urban works, he founded many other towns there. He marked each of these with the individual names of his mother and maternal aunts for the eternal memory of his lineage—names which devouring antiquity has partly abolished, and the influence of barbarians has partly changed...

<summary>The text recounts a legendary migration of the Trojan hero Phorcys to Germany, where he founds the city of Phorcen (Pforzheim) after discovering a river named Aeneas, which he takes as a divine omen.</summary>
<keywords>Phorcys, Aeneas, Troy, Pforzheim, foundation myths, Virgil, Homer, German geography, etymology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the introductory discourse of Book One, transitioning from the mythological origins of the town to the author's personal educational journey and his skeptical views on the nature of law and morality.</meta>

# BOOK ONE
<note>original: "LIBER PRIMVS"</note>

->a iiii<-
<note>This is a signature mark used by early printers to keep the pages in order.</note>

strives for modesty. For what was once called Euryale is now called Aurelium, and what was Stheno before is now Sthein; what was Scylla is called Scell, and what was once Medusa was changed in a more recent age into a new citadel. Thus the Gorgon daughters of Phorcys <note>In Greek mythology, these are the sisters of the Medusa; the author is playfully linking local German place names to classical myths</note> (whose fame is widespread and whose mention is most ancient) are preserved in the eternal memory of men through this fertile soil, this pleasant site of the town of **Capnion**—that is, in the furrow of your own origin. And so that a lasting monument would not be lacking for the descendants of Ascanius <note>The son of the Trojan hero Aeneas</note>, his returning companion and colleague, he began to build something similar in the image of that city which he himself had marked out with a furrow in a circle under the Alban Mount, which he called by the same name, **Longa Alba** <note>The legendary mother-city of Rome, Alba Longa; here the author likely refers to the town of Weissenburg</note>. That name remains today from Phorce within the second milestone.

Thus you have seen me paint a small map of your origin in the manner of Eudoxus <note>An ancient Greek astronomer and geographer</note>, like some chart for sailors, insofar as it leads us to the knowledge that it can be believed that such great men, most distinguished in every branch of learning—though of different sects—were nevertheless born from one sod of earth. I have long sought their conversation with a singular desire, more than gold, silver, pearls, and all precious things, to be found in these thickets and forests, in this very **Swabia**.

Now, pay attention to why I have come to this point. After the arts of speaking, oratory, and thinking, into which I was initiated under the schoolmaster's rod, and then after the disciplines of counting and measuring, which I drank in with a freer spirit, I was led by an admiration of things both on earth and in the heavens toward natural evidences, with which I had determined to complete the whole course of my life. For regarding **ethics** (which some philosophers consider a part of philosophy), I thought very little effort should be given to it. Nor do I judge it worthy of too much mental preoccupation, because moral practice itself consists in the observation of custom and the common social life, which threats and flatteries teach to boys, and punishment teaches to adults; even a native industry contributes not a little to the virtues themselves, which can neither be taught nor studied, but grow strong as the institution of each region demands.

Hence we see that what is called a virtue here is held to be a vice elsewhere, according to the opinions of the inhabitants. Thus I say that **Law** <note>original: "ius"</note> is nothing other than what men wish it to be. Therefore, it is my opinion that civil conduct and legal expertise are contained solely in the persuasion of the common people or the judges, whether you achieve this through oratorical eloquence or by gifts or flatteries, provided that the one who presides over the administration of the law is soothed.

In the writings of **Ulpian** <note>A famous Roman jurist</note>, a man of the greatest counsel without controversy, we read that a certain Barbarius Philippus, though he was a runaway slave, sought the praetorship at Rome; he was then designated praetor and finally performed the duties of the office. It was accepted that none of the acts he performed under the cover of such great dignity should be rejected. The same grave author left these writings to posterity concerning the status of men: that even a man regarding whom a sentence has been passed ought to be accepted as **free-born** <note>original: "Ingenuum"</note>, even if in reality he was a freedman, because a matter judged is accepted as the truth <note>A famous legal maxim: "res iudicata pro veritate accipitur"</note>. And **Paulus**, most skilled in the laws of the Romans, says: Today, because of the custom of the Emperors, if a candelabrum reported to be silver is made of silver, it belongs to the silver, not the furniture, because **error makes the law** <note>original: "error ius faciat"</note>. Therefore, what has pleased either the people or the prince or the king—that is, the judge—has the force of law, both through custom and through the constitutions of the law. For as **Julian** says: Since the laws themselves bind us for no other reason than that they have been received by the judgment of the people, and the people by law...

<summary>The author links local Swabian geography to classical mythology before arguing that morality and law are not absolute truths, but are instead based on local custom, social consensus, and even legal fictions or errors.</summary>
<keywords>Swabia, Capnion, Alba Longa, legal philosophy, Ulpian, Roman Law, ethics vs. nature, legal fictions, custom</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page continues a philosophical dialogue concerning the nature of law and true wisdom, likely part of Reuchlin's "De Verbo Mirifico" (On the Miracle-Working Word). The speaker is critiquing the vanity of civil law compared to higher spiritual pursuits.</meta>

# ON THE MIRACLE-WORKING WORD

Since the royal law conferred all command and power upon the king, it appears clearly that all jurisprudence <note>original: "iuris prudentia"</note> depends solely on the will of men. If this will supports the good and subjugates the evil, it is called a good thing, both public and private. If otherwise, it is the opposite. For on account of the injustices that occur with the people either approving or tolerating them, Hesiod—after writing much about kings, judges, and justice in his book *Works and Days*—concluded that the common people often pay for the sins of their kings (that is, their judges). Indeed, not only he, but also Homer and all of antiquity called all judges "kings," whose insignia of jurisdiction is the scepter they bear. A witness to this is that noble oath of Achilles against Agamemnon, explained in six or seven verses of the *Iliad*. 

I speak not before the ignorant, but as I believe, I speak in the presence of many highly learned and refined men. It should not escape them that this so celebrated and common practice of law—bid for at a great price by those seeking gain, and held as almost more than divine by consultants of controversies—is actually of no or only mediocre importance. It is more rightly judged as a kind of sedentary or menial trade <note>original: "artem aliquā sellulariam aut cerdonicā"; referring to low-status manual labor</note>, especially to a man who freely aims for high and most worthy things with the highest praise, rather than admiring fragile, falling, fleeting, and empty things—the trifles of glory, beauty, riches, and age. 

If you wish, a grave and holy witness and a very good author shall be cited for this discourse: Cicero. He dared to defend my opinion on this matter before the tribunals, even while Servius was listening and unwilling—Servius, who then thought himself someone learned in civil law. I pray you, Capnion <note>The Greek-style nickname for Johann Reuchlin, meaning "Little Smoke"</note>, you who are said to have sweated in the workshop of that same profession even unto the crown and the laurel. What ornament, what dignity, what grace, what admiration is located in that study? It depends on such a thin opinion of men, occupied with individual letters and the punctuation of words. It is cried out by everyone—not only the commoners but also the nobles—to be the authority of all factions, the strength of injuries, the fire of quarrels, and the fuel of iniquity. It sets as its goal only the empty names of magistracies, honors full of daily fear, mercenary gains, and riches that no wise man should seek. Therefore, it openly wins the enmity of many for its practitioners, before whose faces so many Antonys and Catilines <note>References to famous Roman villains/conspirators</note> hover, refusing to let a mind seeking quiet be at rest. 

Furthermore, it is not difficult to know what is observed by everyone; but that which is not observed in every village, they claim is not law. Why then shall I admire so greatly what any butcher, cook, sausage-maker, fisherman, fowler, or hunter might know as well as I? Does it seem a great thing to you to know the name of every law and apply it to every event? Why will you not value more highly the perfumer—whom they call an apothecary <note>original: "apothecariū"</note> because of his storage—who knows every little vessel in his shop: the boxes, jars, bowls, cups, vials, spices, pigments, and drugs <note>original: "pharmaca"</note>? Or the cobbler who remembers to fit any shape to any foot, and likewise does not forget his awls, needles, shears, and thimbles? Use, practice, necessity, and urgent want in affairs teach all these things; and sometimes hunger compels the brute beasts to do similar things. Who facilitated the parrot's "Greetings" <note>original: "χαῖρε" (chaire)</note> and taught magpies to attempt our words? "The belly, master of art and giver of genius, an artist at following denied voices," as that Satirist <note>Persius, in the prologue to his Satires</note> says. Therefore, that which one learns not for its own sake, but—

<meta>catchword: but</meta>

<summary>The speaker argues that civil law is a lowly, mercenary trade focused on trivialities and greed, comparing it unfavorably to manual crafts and even the survival instincts of animals.</summary>
<keywords>jurisprudence, civil law, Cicero, Hesiod, Johann Reuchlin (Capnion), critique of legal profession, Persius</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>The speaker continues a philosophical debate, likely following a discussion on the practicalities of life versus the pursuit of wisdom.</meta>

->BOOK ONE<-

...on account of the daily commerce of human livelihood, and on account of earthly desires foreign to the human mind—how on earth could I join such a thing to most sacred philosophy as if it were a limb, without which philosophy might seem crippled? Just as one who lacks a foot is called lame, and one who lacks a tongue is called mute. I will pass over these things, for I know not what storm or what wave has drifted me to this point. 

I return to where I digressed. For I have determined to complete the entire course of my life in the contemplation of natural things, from which alone arises that which we can truly call <term>science</term><note>original: "scientia"; in this context, it refers to certain, demonstrable knowledge of the natural world.</note>. For other things are either instruments of science, or disciplines of calculation, or the use of common opinion, or fictions of thoughts about things which people imagine exist without body or matter, and therefore call "simple." Although these things exist nowhere, there is nevertheless a race of men who are not satisfied to draw science from the things offering themselves to our senses—a science which can neither be deceived nor deceive—but instead they burden the mind with the thought of certain most thin vapors and "separated forms." Because these are not in nature, but are thought to be above nature, they therefore call them <term>metaphysics</term>. 

I do not dispute these things with the intention of refuting learned men, although they seem worthy of rebuke if we were to weigh on an even scale the rite of knowing: from what fountain it is drawn and by what path it proceeds. On this matter, you should allow your Aristotle to speak, most excellent <note>Capnion is the Greek-derived nickname for the famous humanist scholar Johannes Reuchlin (1455–1522).</note>Capnion; they say you have exercised yourself greatly in his wrestling-school and, having completed your time in the gymnastic contest, you carried away the noble tiara and golden rings. For Baruchias here is said (as I hear) to follow Pythagoras more closely, and any other ancient <term>Magus</term><note>A practitioner of ancient Persian or esoteric wisdom.</note> who might have lived. 

Therefore, that <note>Aristotle, who was born in the city of Stagira.</note>Stagirite, in the second book of the *Posterior Analytics*, toward the very end, set forth this opinion which, as I judge, fits this matter well. Listen, both of you, if by chance I can explain it well enough in Latin. For as you know, Aristotle is excessively thorny in Greek, and obscure, and even rough; he did not wish to be understood except by his own followers. His words are of this sort: 

> From sense-perception sprouts what we call **memory**; from memory, indeed, when a grasp of the same thing is frequently obtained, **experience** <note>original: "peritia"</note> is born. For many memories are a single experience. From experience, or from the universal resting entirely in the soul—the one thing taken from the many, which one thing is present in each of them—there arises the principle of art and of science.

You have heard Aristotle, to whom the poet Afranius once gave a not dissimilar account, both elegantly and wisely, and no less truly, in the book titled *The Chair*, concerning the source of the sciences in this verse: 

> *Practice fathered me, and Memory, my mother, gave me birth.*

Therefore, since those who would attain science must use the arts and the instruments of the senses (without which one happens to be ignorant of all things—for the blind man knows not colors, nor the deaf man the measures of verses or the melody of the lute), it will now be necessary that we can comprehend only bodily things. This is because that which is most thin flows from a body in a certain perpetual stream toward the openings of our senses; then, by a constant motion and with no leisure removed, **imagination** itself introduces that very thing into its chamber. In that chamber, the forms of things shine back into human intelligence, just as a ray reflected from standing water enters the pupil; all of these are bodies and the "flowings" of bodies. Whence Empedocles seemed to me to have spoken more correctly: *that we know like by like.* Nor is any delay or rest granted in this flowing. For we are perpetually perceiving, as Lucretius says, because indeed...

<summary>The speaker argues that true science is derived solely from sensory experience and natural contemplation, critiquing metaphysics as a fiction of "separated forms." He cites Aristotle, Afranius, and Empedocles to support the idea that knowledge begins with the senses and memory.</summary>
<keywords>Empiricism, Aristotle, Metaphysics, Johannes Reuchlin (Capnion), Memory, Sensory Perception, Natural Philosophy, Epistemology</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical dialogue regarding the nature of knowledge and the "Wonder-Working Word." The speaker here adopts an Epicurean or materialist stance, questioning metaphysical abstractions in favor of sensory experience.</meta>

# ON THE WONDER-WORKING WORD

Various senses exist within living creatures, each of which perceives its own proper object within itself. Why "properly"? Because all other things either deceive or are deceived—those things which a person thinks they know without the aid of any senses. But by Hercules! Fearing that I might suggest things too foreign to you, that shrewd interpreter of Epicurus <note>Likely referring to the Roman poet Lucretius, who popularized Epicurean philosophy</note> attempts to draw me back. "Indeed," he says, "the greatest part of these things deceives us because of the opinions of the mind which we ourselves add to our visions, so that things not seen by the senses are treated as if they were." For nothing is more excellent than to discern clear facts. 

Those people do not consider this who attempt to climb above nature and leap into a narrow pit from which they cannot withdraw their feet; who, despite so many things born in the vastness of this universe, nevertheless exhaust themselves over fictions—over "bare beings" as they call them, and the "forms" of things, over divine immovable things, over "subsistences," over the primary "dignities" of reason <note>Technical terms in Scholastic logic and metaphysics</note>, over principles, and over the simplest, incorporeal substances of the gods dwelling outside the home of heaven and leading some sort of "best life" there. All of these things (on the authority of the poets) were discovered as delights for the ears and as comforts for our greed—always most desirous of various affairs—serving as a singular and beautiful art of fabrication. 

Therefore, let all such gods and those meditating on such divine things be gone; let them go to bed and sleep the whole night through. According to Homer's judgment, sleep does not touch Jove <note>Jupiter/Zeus</note> alone, as if the other celestial beings were nothing. He alone might exist, about whom, however, we openly know nothing, but only seem to dream—a point which that same poet insinuates quite prudently, it seems to me, when he describes the dream sent to Agamemnon as being a messenger from Jove. For whatever we know of Jove is most similar to a dream. 

But what should we think of "dignities" and "principles"? Regarding these, the not-unskilled Peripatetics <note>Followers of Aristotle</note>—whom now almost all of Germany follows exclusively, despising all other most noble authors—contend that there is no "science" <term>scientia: certain, demonstrable knowledge</term> of them, for the reason that they are better known than all science and demonstration; indeed, all science depends upon them, and there is no "beginning" to a beginning, since it exists before all things. Yet it is not fitting for that to be unknown by which all things shine forth, appear, and are known. 

From where, then, shall we know these principles? From the senses. Listen again to your Aristotle, Capnion <note>The Greek nickname for Johann Reuchlin, meaning "Smoky"</note>, in the first volume of the *Topics*, when he says: "If anyone wishes to doubt whether all snow is white or not, he has need of the senses." Therefore, the faith which we have from principles as knowledgeable people consists entirely in things subject to nature and the senses; wherefore nothing that is not connected to body and matter is rightly enclosed within the limits of science. 

If this is so, one single knowledge of things will suffice, which the Greeks call *Physics* and you are accustomed to call *Natural Philosophy*. This investigates and searches out the conditions of those things which truly exist and are perceived by us without a veil, as well as their causes, effects, times, places, modes, outcomes, entireties, and parts:

->*Whence comes the trembling of the earth, by what force the deep seas swell,*<-
->*And having burst their barriers, sink back again into themselves.*<-
->*The various eclipses of the sun and the labors of the moon.*<-
->*Whence came the race of men and beasts, and likewise rain and fire.*<-
->*Arcturus, the Pleiades, the Hyades, and the twin bears.*<-
->*Why the winter suns hasten so much to dip themselves in the ocean,*<-
->*Or what delay hinders the slow nights.*<- <note>A series of quotes from Virgil’s Georgics and Aeneid, summarizing the scope of natural science</note>

Physics, the observer of natures, embraces all these things, in which all the most learned men may delight themselves in a wonderful manner for their entire lives. For its sake—as you have heard from me before—how many places and [the variety] of places...

<summary>The speaker argues for an Epicurean and Aristotelian worldview, asserting that true knowledge comes only from the senses and natural philosophy (physics) rather than metaphysical abstractions or poetic myths.</summary>
<keywords>Empiricism, Epicureanism, Aristotle, Natural Philosophy, Physics vs Metaphysics, Johann Reuchlin (Capnion), Virgil</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical debate from the previous section, focusing on the instability of physical knowledge versus the certainty of first principles. The speaker is addressing a learned opponent and mentions "Capnion" (Johannes Reuchlin).</meta>

# BOOK ONE
<margin>b</margin>

->...how could a work of divinity be fashioned from that?<-

I do not deny that there is a certain kind of teaching, opinion, or judgment regarding such matters. We distinguish ice from water, and we judge the latter to be fluid and the former more lasting, even though we know the ice arose from the water just the other day, and the water is ready to be dissolved back into it tomorrow. But that a constant and perpetual <term>science</term><note>original: "scientia"; in this context, it refers to certain, demonstrable knowledge rather than just empirical observation</note> can exist concerning these things, I utterly deny. I do this not only under the leadership of Pythagoras—whose follower you wished me to be (an honor I gladly accept, since I understand from that greatest of men and "god of philosophers," Plato, that the disciples of Pythagoras were so highly esteemed that he did not hesitate to purchase the notebooks of the Pythagorean Philolaus for ten thousand denarii, despite being a poor man). 

But even Aristotle, whose patronage we successfully employ, agrees with us in this matter. For in the first volume of the *Posterior Analytics* <note>original: "posterioris resolutionis"; Aristotle's work on logic and scientific demonstration</note>, after discussing at length what kind of cause we must choose for knowing, and who truly knows versus who merely appears to be a "little scholar," he added: "Therefore, that of which there is science in the absolute sense, it is impossible for it to be otherwise." This is not foreign even to Plato, who, after dividing the world into the first "intelligible" <note>the realm of ideas/forms</note> and the second "sensible" <note>the physical world perceived by the senses</note>, added that the former is judged by the mind through reason, while the latter is judged through opinion, not without the help of the senses. 

See then which Epicurus you are following, building everything upon dust with shadowy speech! With such great patrons on my side, I shall easily win. And yet, it should not seem shameful for me to be conquered, refuted, or proved wrong by a man as great as you—surpassing others in genius, learning, and dignity—especially since I have often been forbidden by the decree of our elders from touching philosophical matters (even "with the tip of the finger," as they say). 

For this reason, Germany nurtures Hebrews who are indeed religious, yet insufficiently learned; they persuade students that they should shrink from philosophers rather than fall into errors through carelessness or, more likely, curiosity—believing philosophers to be the primary authors of such errors. But I have acted as the greedy often do: the more I was deterred, the more fervently I drank, according to the opportunity of circumstances, the time, and my own capacity. Thus, I confess that I have occasionally read Aristotle and looked into Plato, from whom that which I cited to you earlier emerged. 

Even without these authorities, the sheer diversity of all the greatest men writing about natural things (with immortal praise for themselves) would have made me believe—and does make me believe—this same point: that truth cannot consist in such diversity. First of all, to go from the streams to the fountainhead, I think neither you nor our friend **Capnion** <note>The Greek-derived nickname for Johannes Reuchlin (1455–1522), the famous German humanist and Hebraist</note> will deny that a whole thing is known in the same way that it is established to exist. For if it were otherwise, opinion would be deceptive, representing the thing to the mind's eye differently than it actually is. 

But the universe possesses its own principles, even according to your Epicurus and Lucretius, who said: *It is necessary that all things be separated from their principles,* and that the world arose from principles of every kind, the discord of which:
> Confused their intervals, paths, connections, weights, blows,
> Encounters, and motions, mingling them in battle.

Therefore, we know all things equally from the principles out of which they are made; the demonstration of the sciences depends on these as if by a ruler and square, just as the inner chambers depend on the doors. But when one wanders at the very doors, how could I believe that the retreat into the inner chambers will be done correctly? Yet those who are ignorant of the way proceed nonetheless. They are ignorant who rush here and there in crowds around the crossroads, not following a single path...

<summary>The speaker argues that true, certain knowledge (science) cannot be found in the changing physical world, citing Pythagoras, Plato, and Aristotle to support the idea that physical study often leads to conflicting opinions rather than truth. He also defends his own study of philosophy despite traditional prohibitions within the German Jewish community.</summary>
<keywords>Pythagoras, Plato, Aristotle, Johannes Reuchlin (Capnion), epistemology, principles of nature, Jewish education in Germany, Lucretius</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue regarding the origins of philosophy and the search for the fundamental principle of the universe, contrasting various ancient schools of thought.</meta>

# ON THE WONDER-WORKING WORD
<note>original: "DE VERBO MIRIFICO" — This is the title of Johannes Reuchlin's 1494 work exploring the power of sacred names.</note>

...traveling on a journey, so that they are as far from the One as they are from the truth. Shall I begin with those Phoenicians of yours, who were the first and greatest—as you rightly thought—to be endowed with the investigation of all nature? And even with Thales himself, who, although a citizen of Miletus, was nevertheless descended from the most noble Phoenicians, Cadmus and Agenor; he is recorded to have disputed concerning the nature of things before all others. And if Plato is to be believed, he was the first to be called a "Wise Man." <note>Thales of Miletus is traditionally regarded as the first philosopher in the Greek tradition.</note> What, finally, after his innumerable efforts and untiring studies—for which he cast aside both the republic and the highest magistracies—what, I say, did he find through his constant searching that was a worthy foundation for this illustrious machine of the world which we behold? Hear his famous discovery: **Water is the principle of all things.** For from water all things have come forth, and into water all things shall be resolved. <note>original: "Aqua est principium universorum"</note> No one doubts that if we were to entrust the building of this vast mass of the world to the judgment of Thales, we would always have to fear floods and surging stagnations; for nature (in his opinion) would be shown to have placed this liquid floor beneath her buildings, which would collapse at any movement of the water. I remain silent on how much he works against the art of Architecture, he who chooses a marshy region for the structure of a house. But Thales may have intended any river, or (as I think) an influence from elsewhere which could also have been the secondary origin of causes, which Homer seems to me to name "Ocean" where he sang: *Ocean, the father of the gods, and Tethys the mother.* <note>A reference to the Iliad, suggesting that ancient poetry and philosophy shared a common root in water as the source of life.</note>

Yet from him, his fellow countryman Anaximander—first a listener to his teaching, then his successor in the school—departed far by design. For he determined that all things sprout forth from **the Infinite.** <note>original: "ex infinito" — referring to the Apeiron, or the Boundless.</note> I will add also a third Milesian and your same kinsman, one Anaximenes, a disciple of that Anaximander, who, having deserted the precepts of his master, dreamed that all things arise from **Air**, and into air they also return. Heraclitus of Ephesus, a despiser of philosophers and a mocker of Homer, belched forth from his exhaling lungs and hot vitals a thing unheard of, left behind in obscure writings and not in common language: that a certain sterile element which we call **Fire** begets the universe, and to it all things are again referred. In vain, therefore, did the Romans place Vesta <note>The goddess of the hearth and sacred fire.</note> over celibacy.

There succeeded Anaximenes the illustrious Anaxagoras, for some time a citizen of Clazomenae, thereafter a very distinguished philosopher who, for the sake of philosophy, spurned the republic and all popular dignity; he attributed much to a "similar participation" <note>A reference to Anaxagoras's theory of Homeomery, where every substance contains seeds of all other things.</note> which he thought was the cause and generation of things. Archelaus the Athenian, son of Apollodorus (for there was also another certain Homeric soldier), he thought that **Infinite Air**, being dense, provided the cause for all things to exist, and for that reason he is called the "Physicist."

There followed a certain sun of all secret philosophy, from whom that craft first received its name: **Pythagoras of Samos**. I would truly say that he did not err, but merely disagreed with the others. For he himself fashions the universe out of **Numbers**. He learned that discipline from our people—that most ancient discipline of both Syria and the Chaldeans—which, upon returning from there, he thereafter illustrated with Greek words and sentiments. He provided such great admiration of himself to his people that they hesitated as to what name they should call him. I cease to praise this prince of philosophers, who saw no predecessor and has no second, and for whom equal praise [cannot] be found...

<summary>The narrator reviews the theories of the early Pre-Socratic philosophers (Thales, Anaximander, Heraclitus, etc.) regarding the primary element of the universe, ultimately introducing Pythagoras as the pinnacle of wisdom who derived his knowledge of numbers from Eastern sources.</summary>
<keywords>Thales, Anaximander, Anaximenes, Heraclitus, Anaxagoras, Archelaus, Pythagoras, elements, water, fire, air, infinite, numbers, Milesian school, Greek philosophy, Phoenician origins</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical debate from the previous section, moving from a general defense of the arts to a specific critique of ancient theories regarding the origins of the universe.</meta>

# BOOK ONE
<note>original: "LIBER PRIMVS"</note>

...from me. And at the same time, lest I seem to flatter myself too much by extolling those arts and the man whom you yourself suggested I follow. I leave the praising of Epicurus <note>Epicurus (341–270 BCE) was a Greek philosopher who taught that the world is made of atoms and that pleasure (the absence of pain) is the greatest good.</note> to you alone; I marvel that he was born in Athens and not rather in the countryside. He affirmed that the principles of things were indivisible little bodies <note>atoms</note> and empty space. But the famous Empedocles of Agrigentum enumerates four elements from which everything is born: fire, air, water, and earth. He held two principal powers, Strife and Friendship <note>original: "litem & amicitiam"</note>, the latter of which brings things together, while the former scatters them. Plato, the disciple of Socrates, deemed that there were three beginnings of things: God, the forms of things, and matter. From these, he believed that what we perceive first is the shape, and finally the rest. Most recently, Aristotle—who is more famous for the nobility of his art than for Alexander, the King of the Macedonians—maintained that all things grew together from matter, which was stirred toward a desire for form through "privation" <note>Aristotle’s concept of 'privation' refers to the absence of a quality that a thing is capable of possessing.</note>. Whoever attempted to discover anything according to these views established a certain motion in place of privation, as something more effective for bringing both together.

Do you presume, Sidonius, that in such a great diversity and contention among so many men—the most distinguished of their age and (to use the names common then) so many "wise men" and "lovers of wisdom" <note>A play on the Greek word 'philosophos' (philosopher), which literally means 'lover of wisdom.'</note>—a certain faith in the truth can be established? Especially when they fight with such a marvelous struggle concerning the first principles, so that you are forced to wonder to whom you should justly and holily award the palm of victory or the "herb" <note>original: "herbam dare" - a Roman idiom meaning to admit defeat or yield the prize.</note>. For as Firmianus <note>Lactantius (c. 250–325 CE), an early Christian author often called the "Christian Cicero."</note> says, no one has yet descended from heaven to pass judgment on the opinions of each individual. Certainly, you might find some who approve of the mind of Epicurus with you, just as I find those who do not reject the dogmas of Pythagoras with me. But the choice of our profession leads us away, so that suspicion removes the judges, and we are not admitted as unchallengeable arbiters.

What then? What shall we say are the principles of things? Certainly, there has always been a huge contest among the most famous philosophers, and the lawsuit is still before the judge <note>A legal metaphor meaning the matter is still undecided.</note>. Therefore, since the beginnings are not yet certain, I ask you: what true knowledge of things will you have, which are said to have arisen from such beginnings? For Aristotle believes that we know a thing only when we recognize its principles, its cause, and its elements. And not only that, but also why that is the cause of the thing and not something else.

Stop, therefore, seeking stability in fleeting things. Leave the acorns to the pigs, the hay to the oxen, and the sensible foretelling of the storm to the frogs and crows. Let the brutes perceive mortal things subject to destruction; let us strive for higher things and embrace with an eternal mind not those things which are fluid and fragile beneath the heavens, but those which are everlasting above the heavens. For I will not attempt to force into the ears of our people—which are not yet worn down by such talk—those things which even the most practiced can scarcely understand. For they would say that I am separating knowledge from universal things; indeed, they would speak the truth regarding things subject to the mobility of nature, since we look equally at the perpetuity of knowledge and that rushing, unstable river of nature, of which we touch nothing at any time except a single point.

Has our Preacher <note>original: "concionator" - referring to the author of Ecclesiastes.</note> become so foolish—whoever he was who wrote Ecclesiastes, whether Solomon or, as many say, Isaiah himself? "What is it," he says, "that has been? That which shall be." And a little before: "A generation passes away, and a generation arrives." Indeed, how will you be washed unless the wood is transformed? <note>Likely a reference to heating water for a bath.</note> How will you be nourished, unless the stomach digests and transforms the vital juices...

<summary>The author critiques various ancient Greek theories on the origins of matter (atoms, elements, forms) and argues that true knowledge cannot be found in the unstable, physical world, but only in eternal truths.</summary>
<keywords>Epicurus, Aristotle, Plato, Empedocles, natural philosophy, four elements, atoms, Ecclesiastes, skepticism, metaphysics</keywords>

<meta>This page continues a philosophical dialogue regarding the instability of the material world and the elusive nature of the human soul, likely part of Reuchlin's "On the Wonder-Working Word."</meta>

# ON THE WONDER-WORKING WORD
## ii d
<meta>catchword: li</meta>

...distributing food and drink into each of the limbs: cattle produce dung, dung becomes manure, manure becomes grass, grass becomes milk, and milk in turn becomes cattle. The age <note>original: "seculum," referring to the cycle of time or the world</note> rotates all things, and is said to be named from "following" <note>a common medieval etymology: *saeculum* from *sequendo*</note>, because it drives forward things born just as a wave drives a storm, a day drives an hour, and an hour drives a moment. It absorbs one thing so that it may bring forth another. Nature always fashions things so as not to be idle, just as if from wax she makes now a horse, now a lion, and once that is destroyed, a human. One survives the other and, always following, buries the predecessor. Lucilla truly after Lucilla. Secunda after Maximus, after Secunda. Antoninus after Faustina, after Antoninus. In this way, all things go. Celer after Hadrian, then Celer. Indeed, the entire court of Augustus: wife, daughter, descendants, ancestors, sister, Agrippa, kinsmen, servants, friends, Arius, Maecenas, priests, physicians. Death is the mistress of the entire court. Here you would establish for me a perpetual science, so that I might know if whoever wove a history of these things wrote truly. Hippocrates, while curing innumerable diseases, died. Heraclitus, filling the world with fires, is said to have been extinguished by dropsy <note>original: "intercute aqua," literally water under the skin</note>. Lice born from himself—a tiny crowd of almost-animals—killed Democritus. It is no different in other things lacking a soul. For Ebony, among the genus of trees, is now wood and now stone. And according to the book inscribed as the *Alkorran* <note>The Quran</note> among your people, it asks what was first wood: soon it is spirit, they say. Because the rod of Moses was now wood, now a serpent. I shall deal with you in few words, for you are not the sort of person into whose mouth I must insert chewed food, as nurses are accustomed to do for crying boys. Nothing written, painted, or fashioned is hidden from you; therefore, why should I hesitate when I consider that our talents do not supply the strength to investigate nature with absolute certainty, but rather a doubt <note>original: "scrupulus," literally a small sharp stone in one's shoe</note> is always cast into our thoughts of this kind. Nor is a thorn fixed in the heel ever pulled out by the roots. Shall we boast that we will judge without falsehood concerning every species forming any matter, we who do not yet perfectly know our own soul by which we live, breathe, feel, and understand—both what it is and how it is? Regarding this matter, there will be no need for more words with you; you yourself know how many variations exist concerning it, how many labyrinths are circulated. For Epicurus, whom you seem to imitate, says the soul is a species mixed from fire and air. From this comes heat, motion, and the blind power of the wind. From this air begins, and from this all things are set in motion. Xenophanes says it is from earth and water. Parmenides from earth and fire; Empedocles and Critias say blood; Anaximenes, air; Hipparchus, fire; Critolaus the Peripatetic says it consists of a "fifth essence" <note>the quintessence</note>. Democritus says it is a mobile spirit inserted into atoms. Zeno, a spirit condensed with the body. Heraclitus the physicist, a spark of stellar substance. Heraclitus of Pontus, light. Hippocrates, a thin breath diffused through the whole body. Posidonius, an "idea." Pythagoras and Philolaus, a harmony. Xenocrates, a self-moving number. Plato, a self-moving essence. Aristotle, the glorious one, invented a new name: *entelechy* <note>original: "entelechiā," meaning the realization or actuality of a thing</note>, that is, the perfection of form, like the goal of the craftsman; that his discourses on the soul ought to be refuted as if they were mortal, that man of greatest integrity and distinguished with the highest dignity among the "Galileans" <note>The Christians</note>, the great Gregory Nazianzen, is the authority in his book against the Eunomians. Therefore, we who are ignorant of ourselves, how do we hope to acquire knowledge of the rest? How great was the multitude of excellent writers, and yet even up to the times of Jerome and Augustine, nothing certain was established concerning the soul where one might fix one's foot...

<summary>The author reflects on the transience of life and the historical failure of philosophers to define the human soul. He lists various ancient theories of the soul's composition to demonstrate the limits of human reason.</summary>
<keywords>transience, death, soul (anima), Greek philosophy, Epicurus, Aristotle, entelechy, Gregory Nazianzen, uncertainty</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical dialogue between the characters, focusing on the limitations of human knowledge regarding the physical world. The speaker addresses "Capnion" (the scholar Johannes Reuchlin) and references the teachings of the "Nazarene" (Jesus Christ).</meta>

# BOOK ONE

<margin>Signature: b iii</margin>

...it had been left behind, which (as you are a curious explorer of every sect along with me) you were perhaps able to learn from the letters sent between us. For concerning you, Capnion <note>A Greek-style nickname for Johannes Reuchlin, meaning "smoky," a play on his German name</note>, there is no reason for me to doubt, since you follow the teaching of the Nazarene <note>Jesus Christ</note>, of whom these two men stood forth as most studious heralds, like two lights of the world. You have my opinion concerning corporeal things—or those things clinging to the body—which can in some way fall under the senses: that because of the swiftness of the moment in which they persist (which the rapid torrent of nature immediately seizes and alters), and because of the unskillfulness of first principles, the hidden lurking-places of causes, the "eyes of bats" <note>A reference to Aristotle’s idea that our intellect is to divine things as a bat's eyes are to the sun</note>, and the most difficult discovery of the immediate conditions which they call "passions," human knowledge of these things cannot be so suddenly acquired, nor firmly settled, nor faithfully remain. For before a thing can be grasped (as is the opinion of Heraclitus), the object changes before you can apply your thought to its circumstances. I see Empedocles making a rush into the flames of Etna, and I think I know that Empedocles is leaping into Etna; yet while this voice is still pressed between my teeth, that Empedocles is no more, and a story will be told of him as if of one who does not exist—and more credit is given to the speaker than is demonstrated to the listener. Even if you wished to pronounce in a single moment what you desire to be known—like Lycophron, the son of Periander and Melissa, who, lest he should say in a long stretch of words that "the man is white," took away the verb of substance and called him "white-man" in a momentary utterance—surely even so you have not escaped the flux of the world and the rapid torrent of nature. For at every point of time we cast away the present, and just like a swift-turning sphere, those things concluded within this vast world never remain in one state; the world's "ornament" (from which it takes its name in Greek <note>original: "cosmos," which means both "world" and "order/ornament"</note>) is indeed nothing but variety. Therefore, since it is always in motion, a thing is always otherwise than it was, and one property drives out another. Thus, neither can the ray of knowledge be reflected without shaking because of the whirlwind, nor can the light of our mind be directed when it is intercepted so many times by the force of alteration. 

Wherefore we shall rightly approve the opinion of Ecclesiastes when he says: *I understood that of all the works of God, man can find no reason for those things which are done under the sun. And the more he labors to seek, the less he may find; even if the wise man says he knows it, he will not be able to find it.* <note>Ecclesiastes 8:17</note> This is what you said Socrates believed; although he did not wish for there to be *no* knowledge of things (he who disputes about so many things in the works of Plato), but rather no *natural* knowledge, while holding much *divine* knowledge. Finally, he held that there are arts famous in human life by which mortals are kept busy lest they grow sluggish, and which are at the same time conducive to both public and private affairs: smithing, brass-working, domestic management, civil, military, and rural arts, and whatever similarly depends on the prudent will of man. For indeed, those things which are done by our own arrangement will be valid in their established place; in these things, the transgressor will err who does not obey the precepts devised by us—precepts which we have perhaps commanded, compelled by many experiments or sometimes by mere will alone. Socrates thought all these things were "seen" rather than "known." But whatever things were necessary, he judged those should be sought from the divinity alone. And so, he who is commonly reported to have said he knew nothing, seems to me to have known all things—not by nature, but by divine gift. Wherefore he was judged most wise by his own merit by Apollo—not a man, but a god (as you Sidonians <note>Likely a poetic reference to scholars or those of Phoenician/ancient learning</note> persuade us in verse). The histories of the ancients would be available to us, unless you are already weary, and those of our ancestors...

<summary>The text argues that true scientific certainty about the physical world is impossible because nature is in a constant state of flux (referencing Heraclitus and Socrates). It suggests that human knowledge is limited to practical arts, while true understanding must be sought from the divine.</summary>
<keywords>epistemology, Heraclitus, Socrates, flux of nature, Ecclesiastes, Johannes Reuchlin, limits of knowledge, divine gift</keywords>

<meta>The previous page likely discussed the divine origin of knowledge and the limitations of human reason. The catchword "ad" connects to the beginning of this page's first full sentence.</meta>

The annals of our ancestors concerning the most memorable deeds and things most worthy of reading, would support this same opinion in a marvelous way—which (if we remember correctly) inform us about a certain Hesiod <note>An early Greek poet, often paired with Homer.</note>. When he was a shepherd of flocks in Boeotia, and had driven his herd near a certain Mount Helicon at sunrise, he heard a harmony of the Muses, with a sweet melody (as was fitting) for the Muses indeed. Drawing closer, where he beheld those divine faces and the most graceful dance of the goddesses, he began to imitate the song to which they were dancing in rhythm. Then the benevolent Muses, not scorning the simplicity of a rustic man, offered little branches of laurel and several reeds to the shepherd of Ascra; having accepted these, he suddenly acquired the poetic art, not by nature but by God. It was no different with that Demodocus in Homer, and with Minos among the Cretans, whom they say learned all things from Jove <note>Jupiter/Zeus</note>, since he had frequent conversation with God on Mount Ida and indeed in a cave of that very mountain. The Athenians are accustomed to say that Melesagoras of Eleusis was taught by nymphs, and Epimenides the Cretan by a certain very long sleep sent from heaven. There was also in Proconnesus a philosopher of especially marvelous knowledge named Aristeas, of whom there was a rumor that his soul sometimes left his body, and in turn returned to the body after seeing distant spaces. Although he had known innumerable things, yet he was believed to have had no other teacher than divinity. So our Socrates, a man preeminently perfect, and by the testimony of Apollo (as we mentioned before) exceedingly wise, although he shrank from the study, care, and anxiety of natural things, yet nothing in nature had escaped him. The Stoics affirm that this happened to him not by human investigation but by God assisting him, whom he himself called a *daemonium* <note>original: "daemonium" - a divine inner voice or spirit, not a "demon" in the modern sense</note>, whether it was a voice that he heard, or a vision that he saw, or something else by which he sensed the divine in a human way.

But indeed, let us proceed from your examples to ours, which in my judgment are more faithful. Antiquity itself, whose authority was always held in the highest honor, has handed down to memory both by fame and by letters that the Hebrew nation and my ancestors—following with venerable worship a certain God whose name they concealed from the common people (lest it become cheapened by common use)—followed Him through a long journey and for more than forty years through desert places, through rough and thorny paths, through mountains, through valleys, through waters; He meanwhile accompanied them with paternal affection and looked upon them with merciful eyes, so long as they loved and observed Him holily. Therefore that God, by whatever religious name He may finally desire to be called, descended through a cloud to one man out of such a great multitude and vast crowd—one man, I say, Moses, in whom a frequent devotion of mind was pleasing—so that He might join seventy men to him, who might both act as magistrates of the republic with diverse wisdom, and explain by various arts what ought to be done according to the state of the cases. But since that could not be done by natural industry, for the reason we contended above—that the human condition is not capable in every respect of things so changeable and revolving in this world—therefore, from the spirit which He had previously infused into Moses, He also dispensed to the seventy elders. And when the spirit had rested upon them, they prophesied and did not cease thereafter. Concerning sedentary craftsmen also, sacred history records in the same way. For God sent His spirit to the most skillful men, Bezalel and Aholiab, so that they might perform all works wisely, intelligently, and knowingly, and

<summary>This page argues that supreme wisdom and artistic skill are divine gifts rather than products of human study, citing examples from Greek mythology (Hesiod, Minos), philosophy (Socrates), and Hebrew scripture (Moses and the craftsmen of the Tabernacle).</summary>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between the characters Sidonius, Baruchia, and Capnion (Reuchlin) regarding the origins of wisdom and the validity of the Kabbalah versus sensory philosophy.</meta>

->BOOK ONE<-

they might discover new things necessary for the holy sanctuary. Again, I am drawn to your own writers. For what is read in Plato’s *Ion* <note>A Socratic dialogue regarding poetic inspiration</note> concerning Tynnichus of Chalcis that is worthy of memory? From that source, it truly seems to me that this poem of Naso <note>The poet Ovid</note> arose when he said: *There is a god within us, and we have commerce with heaven; from ethereal seats that spirit comes.* <note>original: "Est deus in nobis, sunt & commercia cœli, Sedibus æthereis spiritus ille uenit."</note> Therefore, Sidonius, and you, Capnion <note>The Greek-style nickname for Johannes Reuchlin, meaning "smoky"</note>, I will grant to ingenious men caution, circumspection, and foresight in their actions. In their studies, I grant them sagacity and skill. In the consideration of all things, I grant them teachability, art, intelligence, and opinion. But truly, deny to man any constant, pure, and ineffable knowledge concerning any sensible things unless it has been received—not by human discipline, but by divine tradition—continually from one to another, which we Hebrews call **Kabbalah**, that is, "reception." <note>The author defines Kabbalah here as a chain of oral tradition</note> In this, our ancestors, to whom fame had added the name of wisdom, exercised themselves with prepared minds: such as Abraham (whoever he finally was), Simon son of Jochai <note>Simeon bar Yochai, the traditional author of the Zohar</note>, the second Abraham surnamed Alaphias <note>likely Abraham Abulafia</note>, the famous Rambon <note>Nachmanides</note>, Recanati <note>Menahem Recanati</note>, and the rest who were studious for many cycles of years concerning divine religion, institutes, ceremonies, rites, temples, observations, and sacred mysteries.

Then Sidonius said: "Hey there, Baruchia! How many stones—no, great rocks—do you roll along in this huge river of your speech? May those ancestors of yours perish who, as you say, tried to prohibit you from philosophical books by some edict! For what would have become of you if you had been permitted to penetrate the books of the philosophers? Since I have never heard anything more copious than this oration of yours, and even more combative, so that I am quite at a loss as to what battering ram I might use to break down that wall you have opposed to my empty words. Nor is it right for me to fight back, since you have blunted my sword with such heavy blows. I shall therefore be silent, if I may suggest this one thing: it is a heavy burden for men to be ignorant of what they see, what they hear, and what they touch. Like that Sosia in Plautus <note>A character in the play *Amphitruo* who is confused about his own identity</note> who moves the laughter of the crowd for the sole reason that, by touching, seeing, and hearing himself, he doubts his own existence. For who, looking at snow, would ever call it black? And who has not judged by sense that fire is hot? Therefore, if you listen to Epicurus, you will find that the knowledge of truth was created from the primary senses. But as for your contention that divine things can be known by men, I will not easily grant you that. For in that way, while the thing is grasped by the one knowing it, you would be forced to admit that the divine is joined with the human—two things which are as opposite as can be. For Divinity is said to lack both beginning and end, while all human things are enclosed by boundaries. Moreover, of that which is finite to the infinite (as the Peripatetics <note>Followers of Aristotle</note> attest), there is no proportion. Thus our Lucretius wisely says: *Indeed, to think that the mortal can be joined to the eternal, and stay as one, and perform mutual functions, is to be foolish.* <note>original: "Quippe etenim mortalem æterno iungere..." from De Rerum Natura</note> For what can be thought more diverse, or more disjointed and discordant among themselves, than that which is mortal joined to the immortal and perennial? Truly, even if it were possible, I would not pray for such trouble for the gods above that they should stand by us mortals as teachers at every moment; I would hardly dare ask that of a companion, let alone the gods."

But Baruchia said: "I suspect this 'Sardanapalus' <note>A king known for luxury; here used as an insult for the materialist Lucretius</note> of yours, whom others call Lucretius, has deserved poorly of both philosophy and the whole choir of divinity; and if you do not take it ill..."

<summary>Baruchia argues that true knowledge comes only through the Kabbalah (divine reception) rather than human reason, citing Jewish sages. Sidonius counters using Epicurean logic and Lucretius, arguing that the divine and mortal are too different to ever be joined or communicated.</summary>
