<meta>This page continues the dialogue "On the Wonder-Working Word" (De Verbo Mirifico), specifically a debate regarding the inconsistencies of the poet Lucretius and the nature of divine wisdom.</meta>

# ON THE WONDER-WORKING WORD

-><note>original: "DE VERBO MIRIFICO"</note><-

...here as in many other places he is at odds with himself. But let us touch upon the matter at hand. For when he was describing the sacred rites of the Great Mother Cybele <note>The ancient Phrygian mother goddess</note>, and affirmed that armed men follow her chariot for the reason that they believed the goddess foretold with what protection they should defend their fatherland—yet he added in these words that such things are vain to believe:
*“Which things, although they are set forth well and excellently, are nevertheless far removed from true reason. For it is necessary that the whole nature of the gods, of itself, enjoy immortal life with the highest peace, far removed and separated from our affairs. For it is exempt from all pain, exempt from dangers. Strong in its own resources, needing nothing from us, it is neither won over by good merits nor touched by anger.”* <note>A famous passage from Lucretius's "On the Nature of Things," arguing that gods are indifferent to human affairs.</note>
From this it appears that the gods above grant us nothing of their clemency, lest perhaps they be affected by the trouble of business. Yet later, in the third book, he speaks thus: *“It must be said he was a god, O illustrious Memmius <note>Gaius Memmius, the Roman politician to whom Lucretius dedicated his poem</note>, who first discovered that reasoned way of life which is now called Wisdom <note>original: "Sapientia"</note>, and who by his art rescued life from such great billows and such great darkness, and moored it in so calm and so clear a light.”* This speech can signify nothing except that philosophy is the breath <note>original: "spiraculum"</note> of God and a divine illumination. He finally confesses this, forced by the truth itself—truth which we have scarcely wrung from his hands, and which we have expressed without the threat of the little strings <note>term: "fidiularum" - a reference to cords used in torture to extract confessions</note>.

I pass over in silence how he asserts at one moment that the mind is mortal, and at another that it returns to a heavenly beginning. And when I proceed to say, for the sake of the word, that the soul is mortal, you may believe I am also speaking of the mind. In another poem he says: *“Finally, we are all sprung from heavenly seed; that same one is the father to all.”* And after a few words he concludes: *“Likewise, that which was from the earth before passes back into the earth, but what was sent from the shores of the ether, that the shining temples of heaven receive again.”* Does he not fear to be refuted as one thoughtless and impudent? But he hoped to deceive us with decorated words, or at least to render us doubtful, and just as he betrayed himself, to touch us as if with the "sweet honey of the Muses."

But I pray you, let him sing among the poets, not preside over the platform of the philosophers. For if I thought that you or anyone else were moved by his decrees, his writings, or even by that most impure faction of Epicurus <note>The Greek philosopher who taught that pleasure is the highest good and gods are indifferent</note>, I would cry out and contend as much as I possibly could that this sect is the most filthy nurse of all vices, planted by the fanatical dream of a certain Leucippus <note>Early Greek atomist philosopher</note>, and later watered by the authority of Democritus. I would rouse philosophers of every kind, although to extirpate this sacrilegious tribe there is no need for so many men, nor for a particularly sagacious or brilliant intellect.

Then **CAPNION** <note>The Greek nickname for Johannes Reuchlin, meaning "smoky"</note> said: "Cease the insults; let us consider the matter itself. Indeed, I seem to see myself, both of you being explorers of the highest things, endowed with the greatest talents and a singular abundance and faculty of speaking, having contributed many things splendidly, many things magnificently, and most things wisely to the matter being discussed, providing, as it were, a most noble tasting of the various wares of each. But when I reflect within myself what I might be able to bring to your forum worthy of such great litigants, I truly pity and weary of myself, who would not hesitate to press a too rustic and inelegant speech upon the ears of the most learned men—unless this one thing frees me from fault: that I have mingled myself in your company deliberately and with effort, so that by the comparison of my rudeness and lack of skill, the dignity and glory of the most lettered men might shine much more sharply, as a white thing is accustomed to be <insert>valued</insert> among many black things."

<summary>The narrator critiques the inconsistencies of the poet Lucretius regarding the soul and the gods, ultimately dismissing Epicureanism as a "filthy nurse of vices" before Capnion (Reuchlin) begins his humble address to the learned company.</summary>
<keywords>Lucretius, Epicureanism, immortality of the soul, Cybele, Capnion, Johannes Reuchlin, divine wisdom, philosophy vs poetry</keywords>

# BOOK ONE

->page 12<-

...things shine more preeminently, and anything appears more distinct by the presence of its opposite. "Therefore, come now," said Sidonius, "I beseech you—and I know this Baruchia <note>A Jewish interlocutor in the dialogue</note> agrees with me—spare this air even in an inopportune matter. For what need is there for the renunciation of praise, of which we judge you most worthy? We have known each other for a long time. Therefore, proceed, Capnion <note>The Greek-style pseudonym for Johannes Reuchlin, meaning 'smoky'</note>, and explain your opinion to us. For this very convenient place of philosophy contains solitude, and we shall not be prohibited from hearing, nor you from speaking, by any noise." "I will do so easily because you command it," said **CAPNION**, "provided you bear my follies with a mind not troubled. And if my memory does not fail me, by Hercules, having left all the number of enemies with their very camps, I think the war is being waged between you for two reasons. First, because no knowledge <term>scientia: certain, demonstrative knowledge</term> of things so celestial or so infernal can be acquired by human means, an occasion provided both by human weakness regarding divine matters, and by the temporary mutability and continuous alteration of natural things, which is thought to pertain to the foundation of perfect knowledge. A second obstacle also stands: that mortals may not know anything even by divine means, lest the gods be affected by intolerable annoyance through the daily appetites of men eager for learning. Furthermore, and most importantly, because to join divine operation with human operation—which would necessarily happen in such knowledge—would be no different than if you strove to connect the most diverse things with a harmonious bond. To explain all this as briefly as possible according to our capacity, the force of the words must be weighed, with which you have been fighting almost from dawn until this evening. Moreover, the treatment will not be from very hidden principles, nor (as I think) by reasons not clear for believing. For what else do the orbs of the eyes, enclosed in the concave holes of the forehead, desire than to see? Which is their one and proper solace, without which fruit of the spectacle they would certainly be in vain and idle gifts. If you wish, remember what sort of not-at-all-inelegant song Phryxille brought forth in Euripides: 'Whoever that man is,' he says, 'even if he were a slave, he nevertheless rejoices to see the light.' What of that cartilage, softer than bone and firmer than flesh, hanging and bending in the ears of men—does it not desire to drink in voices? Whence also the ears <note>Latin: *aures*</note> are thought to be named from 'drinking in' <note>Latin: *haurire*</note>, as Virgil is cited when he says: 'I drank in the voice with these ears.' And to embrace the other instruments of perceiving things: what else is the scented power of the nostrils than to smell? What does the tongue desire more than to taste? Therefore, the food on which the eye feeds is color. Thus the sound of the ears, the sweet smells of the nose, the flavor of the tongue, and all soft things become the fodder of touch. I ask next: if the eye did not exist, nor the ear, nor the nose, nor finally any tongue, no sense—what benefit do you think color, voice, odor, bitter, sweet, rough, or smooth would bring us? Would not all these things be in vain in the world, and considered empty and nothing? Certainly, put this out of your hands and grasp not the 'arms' of the senses, but their powers and faculties. For unless a sharp judgment is present in them, all things will become indistinct and confused; nor would you assign to one what is proper to the other: the eye would 'see' a voice, and the ear would 'hear' a color. I pray you therefore, Sidonius, according to your Lucretius: 'Will the ears be able to rebuke the eyes? Or touch the ears? Or further, will the flavor of the mouth refute this touch? Will the nostrils confute the eyes, or will they also overcome taste?' It is not so, as I think. For...

<summary>Capnion (Reuchlin) begins his discourse on the limitations of human knowledge, arguing that the senses are specialized instruments that require internal judgment to function. He uses classical references to Euripides, Virgil, and Lucretius to illustrate how each sense has its own specific "food" or object of perception.</summary>

<keywords>epistemology, five senses, human weakness, divine knowledge, Reuchlin, classical philosophy, perception, Lucretius</keywords>

--- Page 27 ---
<meta>The previous page likely introduced the speakers of the dialogue, including Baruchias the Hebrew, as they discuss the nature of perception and the soul.</meta>

-># ON THE WONDER-WORKING WORD<-

Each power has its own separate authority divided among them. Both Baruchias <note>A character in the dialogue representing Jewish mystical wisdom</note> here with you, and all the Academics and Peripatetics <note>Followers of Plato and Aristotle, respectively</note> will answer the same. But what is the occasion for this division? Let Epicurus speak: the eye, and likewise the other instruments of sense, do not know the actual nature and condition of things. What then? Hear the verse: *In the end, it is the reason of the mind that must discern this.* <note>original: "Hoc animi demum ratio discernere debet." A quote from Lucretius, De Rerum Natura.</note> The matter is settled. For just as a picture feeds the sight with an empty image, so the form of truth reflecting in the mind will undoubtedly be the food of reason, which distinguishes one thing from another through the mediation of thought, which he calls "estimation."

I will review the whole cause as briefly as I can. A sensible object casts off from itself on every side a very thin and incorporeal likeness, which is easily seized by the sense because of the friendly hospitality of the sensitive power. When it dwells in that place, it soon acquires a more eminent dignity; casting off the present substance of the thing, it becomes an "idol" <note>term: idol (eidolon) - a mental image or representation of an object</note>, while retaining the other circumstances with it. Then, therefore, a certain imagination takes up the likeness of the absent image. Finally, the phantasy itself judges and estimates the conditions of the idol through certain appearances, sometimes truly, sometimes deceptively.

This is why horses, by discerning poorly, tend to hate masks and flee from them as if they were future harms. It is no different for birds, who, suspecting evil from the fake idols <note>scarecrows</note> in gardens and fields, shudder at them; they often fear traps and sense ambushes. Later, although these wrappings are diligently refined, they are still sprinkled with animal-like attachments; a worthier virtue called **reason** sifts and weighs them, running from one property to another. By this clear discourse, in physical things, it affirms that *this* is crystal, but *that* is ice; and among gems, it disputes whether amber is sometimes mixed with glass, whereby it notices that this is a chrysolite, that is called resin, and the other is named glass.

In actions and business, however, it judges one thing to be shameful and another to be honorable. Therefore, reason always probes, and with diligent and untiring investigation, it scrutinizes sensible things—first stripped of their matter, now stripped of the presence of the object—adding also various intentions not received by any of the senses. Yet these cannot become known without sensible things. For example, when I look at this man Baruchias: he is of medium stature, with a long beard, a somewhat sad brow, an aquiline nose, wearing a cloak and a circular badge <note>The yellow badge (croceus circulus) required to be worn by Jews in many Renaissance European cities</note>. As the Satirist says: *With head bent down and eyes fixed on the ground, he mumbles to himself and gnaws on rabid silences, weighing his words with an outstretched lip.* <note>original: "Obstipo capite & figente lumina terrę..." A quote from Persius, Satire 3.</note>

From this, I divine that he is a Hebrew, for whom the yellow circle is the sign; a philosopher, because he is covered by a cloak; and most likely a Pythagorean or of another more divine sect, because Saturn, the author of secret contemplation, is said to produce such appearances of the face. Furthermore, according to the custom of his nation and profession, I judge him not to be very friendly, except insofar as letters <note>scholarship</note> now unite us; likewise, he is one who enjoys a roof and the shade, and many other signs of this kind which indicate either vice or merit in a man. Nor does the scrutiny of reason ever cease, but there is an innate desire in everyone to seek diligently, until the keen appetite for knowledge is not frustrated in its wish for the desired truth. Craftsmen investigate this in every art, and nature itself makes anyone who is deceived blush, because they lacked the judgment of right reason between the true and the false. We also naturally hate deceivers as such, and all those who carry one thing on their tongue and another in their heart. And nothing is so...

<summary>The text explores the cognitive process of perception, moving from physical sensation to the formation of mental images (idols) and finally to the analytical judgment of reason. It uses the physical appearance of Baruchias to demonstrate how reason interprets external signs to deduce internal character and identity.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical defense of human knowledge, specifically addressing the innate desire for truth and the distinction between stable knowledge and sensory opinion.</meta>

# BOOK ONE

Page 13.

Men consider it more shameful to be in error than to be ignorant, especially those endowed with good character and who excel in the virtue of the mind—whom I say are the only true "men" whenever we must discuss the sciences. Even infants, scarcely torn from the breast, inquire curiously about whatever thing they see or feel; thus it is permitted to confess, not without reason, that the love and capacity for finding the truth is inborn within us. Nor should we allow ourselves to be dragged, or even led, to such madness as to think that the nature of the universe—which, even by the testimony of Sardanapalus <note>A legendary Assyrian king, often cited in classical texts as a symbol of luxury, though here perhaps referenced regarding the basic cycles of life.</note>, creates, increases, and nourishes all things—has wandered astray here. While nature is seen to have done nothing wrongly elsewhere, we must not think she has impressed such a greed for truth upon us in vain. For just as you would admit that eyes were created fruitlessly if they were never permitted to behold the light, so too shall we think of holy and skillful Nature: she would never have implanted reason in the minds of mortals, nor inserted such a vigilant effort for inquiring after the truth, unless it were established beforehand that the human mind could apprehend the truth. She intended for this noble faculty, condition, virtue, and power to eventually break forth into operation, to be reduced to effect, to be propelled into act, and to be aimed at an object agreeable to it and, in turn, desiring of it. For truth is the food of reason, just as illuminated color is to the eye, a sharp scent to the nostrils, and a harmonious sound to the ears.

Therefore, it clearly appears in this structure of the world which we discern that a certain truth triumphs; it so often stirs, agitates, entangles, and unfolds our reason, and so often solicits it—restless and sleepless—in wondrous ways. This happens not by chance or by outside accidents, but by nature's own proper and original allurements. Since none of these things happen in vain, it follows that we may finally embrace the truth of those things that exist at some opportune time. Whoever, therefore, truly comprehends that which exists—whether by his own nature or aided by divine assistance—why should I not consider him to have obtained the knowledge <term>scientia: certain, demonstrative knowledge</term> of it? For we shall call the true apprehension of a thing "knowledge." This is what I said at the beginning: the force of the words over which you cross swords so much must be weighed. In this cause, it can certainly be easily understood that the name of "knowledge" signifies various things, just as the things we say are known are manifold.

However, concerning the things from which many philosophers—but especially the Platonists—affirm they gather knowledge, they have decreed there to be two natures. One which can be seen with the eyes and touched by the hand, changeable at almost every moment. Let pardon be granted (as the man from Madaura <note>Apuleius of Madaura, a 2nd-century philosopher and author.</note> says) to the novelty of words serving the obscurity of things. Since this [material nature] cannot persist as one and the same, it is nevertheless received into the hospitality of the mind by such a virtue, more rightly according to the way it *is* than the way it *is not*, just as the thing is in truth: that is, mutable. This power in us capable of receiving a mutable thing—which ought to be equally agreed upon between us hereafter—I call "opinable reason" <note>Reasoning based on opinion or sensory data rather than eternal truths.</note>. From this it proceeds, Sidonius, that you contend there is a "knowledge" of natural and sensible things. And I agree with you; certainly, as the things known change, so too will the knowledge change. It matters nothing whether you call this "knowledge" or "opinion," since the nature of the business is manifest. Do you wish me to say it in one word? Of all things dwelling beneath the canopy of heaven, there is nothing which we humanly call "knowledge" in the true sense, but rather "opinion." Because if we ever hear that knowledge is obtained concerning these things, nevertheless always...

<summary>The author argues that the human drive for truth is an innate biological and spiritual necessity, distinguishing between certain knowledge and the "opinable reason" used to understand the changing physical world.</summary>
<keywords>epistemology, nature of truth, Platonism, Apuleius, reason vs. opinion, sensory perception, philosophy of science</keywords>

--- Page 29 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue concerning the nature of knowledge and the divine word. The catchword "lius q̃ ip̃e" indicates the transition to the next page's first word, likely "melius."</meta>

# ON THE WONDER-WORKING WORD

The term itself designates an opinion. On the other hand, they assert that the second nature of substances is incorruptible, immutable, constant, and always the same; it is perceived not only by penetrating thought but is also seen by the sharp edge of the mind, consisting of those things we call divine. For many call the preceding things "demonic" <note>original: "dæmonica." In the Renaissance context, this refers to intermediary spirits or natural forces, not necessarily evil demons.</note>. Hence, most have nicknamed Plato "Divine" and Aristotle "Demonic" because of the excellence of both in writing on such matters.

Therefore, concerning these things—since they are super-celestial and nowhere meet any physical sense—who among mortal men (for I call upon you, most excellent men, Sidonius and Baruchia), who, I say, besides one divinely inspired and by no human power, could obtain even the smallest bit of knowledge? No one, by Hercules, no one! Therefore, truth is the object, the food, the nourishment, and the highest pleasure of the reason for lower things, and of the mind for higher things, each according to its own measure and capacity. 

Yet, in the vast space of the world, there is no constant, complete, and irrefutable science regarding substances except by divine revelation, insofar as science was defined by Aristotle; for divine things cannot be touched, and worldly things escape the senses at every moment. For in the latter, a sufficient knowledge of causes is lacking; in the former, the constancy of permanence is missing; and what hinders us most is our fragile industry, late-arriving wisdom, and swift death. 

Thus it happens that what we often think is wisdom or science is actually an error, whether it is supported by the observation of the heavens or relies on an eminent suspicion of natural causes that drag matter along with them. This is what that most noble prophet Isaiah, writing to the city of Babylon, reproaches the Chaldean philosophers for: *Your wisdom and your knowledge, it has deceived you.* <note>Isaiah 47:10</note> *Let the astrologers of the heavens save you* (which we perhaps translate better than "soothsayers of the sky," for in Hebrew they are astrologers who contemplate the stars and calculate the months) *from whom shall come to you whatever things are to be.* *You have fainted in the multitude of your counsels; they have wandered, each one in his own way.* <note>Isaiah 47:13-15</note> 

He has therefore encompassed both: those who scrutinize lower causes and those who care for celestial influences. One errs by calculating, the other by gathering counsels; both are ignorant of the equalized power of operations and the condition of all circumstances. 

Wherefore, whether we are wise regarding eternal things (from which the name "wisdom" <term>sapientia: derived from 'sapere', to taste or be wise</term> comes), or we know perpetual things (in which a small measure of matter exists), or we recognize momentary, flowing, and falling things which are subject to time and the sky—right faith is always superior to them. Through faith, the **active intellect** <note>The 'intellectus agens', a concept from Aristotelian and Scholastic philosophy referring to the power of the mind that makes potential knowledge actual.</note>, placed at the summit and peak of the human soul—pure, lucid, and clear—beholds the states of all things, both mortal and immortal, radiating in divine and super-celestial minds and shining back as if in a mirror of eternity. This faith is far more certain and more effective for both contemplating and acting than any logical arrangement of reason, discourse, investigation, opinion, or science—and indeed more than any potential, active, speculative, or acquired intellect—insofar as that intellect visits things lower than itself. 

For if it is permitted to compare lower things to higher: just as the soul, according to sense, becomes one in act with sensible things, and according to intellect becomes one with intelligible things, so also—and even more so—through faith, our mind becomes one in act with the higher intelligences and is joined with God. In Him, all causes of things shine back much more eminently, much more nobly...

<summary>The text argues that true knowledge of divine and eternal substances is impossible through human reason alone and requires divine revelation and faith. It contrasts the limitations of Aristotelian "demonic" science and Chaldean astrology with the certainty provided by the active intellect when joined to God through faith.</summary>
<keywords>Plato, Aristotle, Isaiah, active intellect, divine revelation, wisdom vs science, astrology, epistemology, Renaissance Neoplatonism</keywords>

--- Page 30 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical discourse on the hierarchy of being and the nature of God, following the previous discussion on causes and effects.</meta>

# BOOK ONE
<note>Latin: LIBER PRIMVS</note>

...others that are able to consist in their own effects. For the first cause is more intimate to things than secondary causes are. The first contains the secondary, just as a fountain contains the waters of the streams. Indeed, just as the immense sea draws in all waves at once, its embrace is most wide-reaching. This is what Agamemnon proclaims in Homer <note>Referring to the Iliad, where Agamemnon acknowledges the supreme power of Zeus</note>. For He holds the universe, which is why He is said to possess *the greatest power* <note>original Greek: μέγιστον κράτος (megiston kratos)</note>. According to the testimony of Orpheus, the beginning, the middle, and the end are the origin of every thing and the most powerful force of subsistence. Believe me, when I revolve these things in my mind, and when I gather and separate the natures of the universe, I seem to see clearly in what manner man conducts himself toward those things closest to him. For although heaven shares matter with fire, fire shares heat with air, air shares moisture with water, and water shares coldness with earth—and again, earth shares dryness with fire—so God shares immortality with the intelligence (which we judge by the idiom of the name to be a *demon* <note>term: dæmon — here used in the classical Greek sense of a guiding spirit or divine intermediary, not an evil entity</note>, as if it were *knowing* <note>original Greek: daimon</note>). The demon shares affection with man, man shares sense with the beast, and the beast shares with the plant a certain kind of soul that causes it to grow. 

Yet I find, finally, that the brute differs from man by reason, and man from the demon by innate wisdom. But man differs from man most of all by faith; so that just as the ignorant man is different from the wise man through the use of reason, so the wise man differs from the prophet through the use of faith. The prophet also differs from the demon by his mortal condition. Now, therefore, you see why God placed man in the middle of paradise. Paradise is a garden of delights. All adornments are delights. The world <note>Latin: mundus</note> took its name from "adornment," since in Greek it is called *kosmos* <note>original Greek: κόσμος — meaning both 'order' and 'ornament'</note>. Indeed, in civil law, a woman's "world" <note>Latin: mundus muliebris</note> refers to those things with which women are adorned. 

He therefore placed man in the middle of the universe, shared between the lower and the higher beings, so that he might bear faith toward the higher and reason toward the lower. Among mortals he is like a god through faith; among the heavenly beings he is like a man through reason; excelling between both through wisdom—surpassed only slightly by divinity, yet a victor over mere humanity. This we have learned from King David, for he speaks thus to the highest and best God concerning man: *You have made him a little lower than the elohim* <note>Hebrew: elohim — translated here as 'God' or 'divine beings'</note>, that is, than God. 

Thus you can remember what I feel regarding your prior contention: namely, that there is a certain eternal truth in eternal things, of which the mind itself becomes a receptacle; and in mutable things there is a mutable truth, which is content with the lodging-house of reason. The knowledge of the one may be called wisdom, and of the other, right opinion; yet all things are uncertain unless they are strengthened by divine faith. 

There now remains one obstacle of a certain weak argument (if I may say so with everyone's peace) dreamed up by Epicurus, that popular philosopher. I believe Sidonius recites this rather than affirms it, given his singular gravity and excellence of learning, in which he is remarkably gifted beyond others. He does this to make us wonder whether it happens that God is affected by weariness if he bestows benefits upon mortals, or whether he rather desires to be asked by constant prayers. Epicurus alone, impatient of labor, loved idleness so much that he placed the "highest good" in leisure, and thought the influence of divinity was a burden. As if we should believe the sun is exhausted by its light and finally fails because it has shone so long, or that heaven is fatigued when it offers merciful influences to the lower realms. If this long-lasting duty does not weary, wear down, or weigh upon physical bodies, what must be felt concerning the supreme Author of all? If He, along with Epicurus, Lucretius, and those other pleasure-seeking sycophants <note>Latin: gnatonibus — referring to 'Gnatho,' a parasite character from Roman comedy</note>, embraced laziness and inertia...

<summary>The author discusses the hierarchical chain of being, placing man as a central link between the divine and the earthly, while refuting Epicurus's idea that divine providence is a wearying labor.</summary>
<keywords>Great Chain of Being, Reason vs Faith, Etymology of Kosmos, Epicureanism, King David, Divine Providence</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical dialogue regarding the nature of the divine and the "Wonder-Working Word." The speaker is currently refuting Epicurean atomism in favor of a unified, providential Creator.</meta>

# ON THE WONDER-WORKING WORD
<note>original: "DE VERBO MIRIFICO" — This is the title of Johannes Reuchlin's 1494 treatise on the Kabbalah and the power of divine names.</note>

...and had he loved idleness, he certainly would not have poured forth from his own bowels this so great and so admirable adornment which we behold, nor would he have constructed this machine <note>The "machine of the world" (machina mundi) was a common Renaissance metaphor for the universe's complex but orderly structure.</note> which he might govern for all eternity. For although we have experienced that there are individual agents, deputies, tribunes, and governors for various affairs, nevertheless one always recurs to this one Prince of the universe; we must believe that without him, neither can the agents act, nor the tribunes support, nor the governors suffice. It is no different than a beam of light without the sun, or a secondary cause without the first cause. For the order of the universe requires both a "first thing" and a "unity," which it is shameful for a philosopher to be ignorant of, since even common soldiers know it. For thus Ulysses speaks to the assembly in the army in Homer: *We Achaeans <note>original: "Achiui" — the Greeks at Troy.</note> certainly cannot all be in command. For the rule of many is not good; let there be one prince, one king.*

Where now are your many atoms, Epicurus, governing so great a kingdom? And where are the innumerable architects of a single world? If these tiny little bodies <note>original: "corpufcula" — corpuscles or atoms.</note> rule by reason, they will therefore not be simple and indivisible, since they would consist of both reason and a body. If they rule without reason, it is necessary that all things be carried out imprudently. From this not only nature, but the very name of the "world" <note>original: "mundi" — Reuchlin plays on the Latin word *mundus*, which means both "the world/universe" and "clean/neat/ordered."</note> shrinks back; in such a state, nothing adorned, nothing "ordered" could manage to exist, while individual things would be thrown into confusion and one thing would pull against another.

Therefore, he who is supreme is one; he who is one is simple. For if he were otherwise composed, he would be two, not one; wherefore he never receives into himself any sorrow, weariness, confusion, or trouble. For he would become varied and manifold and changeable for the worse, which does not befit unity and supreme goodness. However, when he is said to be "like himself"—that is, clement, beneficent, merciful, and other things which set forth, suit, and adorn his goodness—then he is that very same thing, and then he will be at once one, clement, and God. He is "God" when he is invoked; he is "clement" when he grants abundance to our prayers.

But is anyone more insane than a madman who thinks it possible that the laborious rest and restful operation of the eternal divinity could be disturbed by any prayers of suppliants? His highest dignity is to be petitioned; his highest tranquility is to grant. He even invites men to ask, which the Son of God (who is also God) taught us. *No one,* he said, *comes to me unless my Father has drawn him.* It is a pleasure for God to look upon the faces of men gazing at him and admiring him—not a trouble, not a restlessness.

But your Lucretius, Sidonius (for out of respect I no longer name you as part of so sacrilegious a sect), and also your Epicurus dare to note that in God which it would not be right to think even of good men: that he would find the prayers of friends troublesome. For what else moves mortals to prayer toward the divinity except the hope that they are a friend to God? What administers faith to supplications except love alone? This Homer, who is most full of all philosophy, showed when he said: *Apollo himself heard Chryses praying, because he was very dear to him.*

It appears, I think, from these things which I have embraced in a few words (according to the meager faculty of speaking that is in me), what must be answered regarding that human union with divinity. For the question now is not whether one and the same can be God and man, whether born of God or sprung from men. Such a thing that deserter Julian <note>Julian the Apostate, the Roman Emperor who tried to revive paganism.</note> recorded in the book published *Against the Galileans* <note>Julian's polemic against Christians.</note>, whose words are almost these, as translated by my hand: *For Jupiter, among his own intelligible things, [produced] Aesculapius from himself...*

<summary>The author argues for a single, unified Creator against Epicurean atomism, asserting that God is not burdened by human prayer but rather invites it through love. He begins to address the union of divine and human natures, citing Homer and the Emperor Julian.</summary>
<keywords>Monotheism, Epicureanism, Divine Providence, Homer, Ulysses, Lucretius, Julian the Apostate, Aesculapius, Prayer, Unity of God</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical and mythological discussion from the previous section, likely focusing on the manifestation of the divine in the physical world and the nature of the soul's connection to God.</meta>

# BOOK ONE
->c ii<-

<meta>Library stamp at bottom: National Library of Rome, Vittorio Emanuele</meta>

begat, and destined for a life through the sun's prolific power upon the earth; he, having obtained by lot a progress from the heavens to the earth, appeared uniformly in human form around Epidaurus <note>The site of the sanctuary of Asclepius, the god of healing</note>. Thus, increased by successes there, he stretched out his life-giving right hand over the entire earth. He passed to Pergamum, then setting out into Ionia to Taranta, and later to Rome. From here he crossed into Cos, and finally to Aegae, and to other places by sea and land, so that he might restore ill-disposed souls and bodies that were not faring well. If Julian <note>Likely referring to the Emperor Julian "the Apostate," who championed traditional paganism</note> himself truly saw these things, we shall not strive in this place to unfold the controversy concerning the immortal gods—how they lived upon the earth and were seen by men, such as Apollo pursuing Daphne, or when he was established in Thessaly at the house of Admetus, or was recognized in Phrygia in the presence of Laomedonte, or how both the high gods feasted in the hut of the poor Philemon and Baucis, as Ovid wrote: *Hither came Jupiter in mortal form, and with his parent came the grandson of Atlas <note>original: "Atlantiades," referring to Mercury/Hermes</note>, the wand-bearer, with his wings set aside.* Indeed, almost that entire book of transformations <note>Ovid's *Metamorphoses*</note> is nothing other than the communion of gods with men, or—what is more ridiculous—with beasts, metals, stones, and other things lacking a soul; not only a communion, but even a transmutation. This must seem even more admirable to the Epicureans <note>Followers of Epicurus, who believed gods did not interfere in human affairs</note>: that the poets sing of the highest Jove being mixed with all things, even the most base. For they say he was transformed now into gold, now into a swan. Now he was a bull, now an eagle, now the stone which is called Abaddir <note>A sacred stone swallowed by Saturn</note>; he was always more changeable than Proteus himself. 

But we—whether it be the society of man with God, or the communication of God with man, insofar as it contributes to our discussion among ourselves—call it Love, which faith has fathered, and hope nourishes and preserves. God is love, man is hope, and the bond of both is faith. Divinity is joined to the mind, as the mind is to the intellect, the intellect to the intention, the intention to the image, the image to sensation, and sensation to the senses, and these finally to physical things; so it is always that the superior virtue flows through each inferior thing according to the capacity of any living being, down to the very last. For it often happens that I direct my eyes toward something, yet I do not remember having seen it because my imagination was bound elsewhere. But what it is that I suspect I see, the intellect—previously exercised regarding the intention of the phantasy <note>The faculty of forming mental images</note>—provides with reason. Unless the intellect is illuminated through the mind, it is not immune from error. The mind does not extend light to the intellect unless God is shining, who, since He has a most free will (otherwise He would be inferior to man), rains more or less light into the mind according to the measure of His will, by which the other powers of mortals are watered. Therefore, although there is no proportion of man toward God—for God is infinite and man is finite—nevertheless (I speak with your permission, Sidonius) these two are not "greatly diverse" as you used to say, and yet one is not the other. They can, however, be joined in an ineffable union, so that one and the same may be judged both a human God and a divine man. I deal with you now, most learned men, familiarly in the wrestling-ground <note>original: "palæstra," a metaphor for a place of intellectual debate</note> not of secret faith, but of common philosophy. For thus Unity, which has neither beginning nor end, and is therefore called an infinite essence in power and operation—even though it is not a number (for that is a heap of multitude)—nevertheless creates number and is joined with number. Its varied conversation in the company of numbers also varies their substance, and it governs the whole numerical kingdom like a supreme king. This...

<meta>This page continues the philosophical dialogue regarding the "Wonder-Working Word," focusing on the Pythagorean and Neoplatonic properties of numbers and the power of speech.</meta>

-># ON THE WONDER-WORKING WORD<-

He humbles one, and exalts another. And as Hesiod <note>Greek poet, c. 700 BCE</note> says, "This same one easily strengthens and easily brings down the high." For it flows from an internal immanence, and since it is infinite, it is the measure and boundary of all things, acting as the first triangle <note>The 'trias' or triad, often seen in Pythagorean thought as the first stable form</note> producing the sums of all, since it is mingled in the procreations of all things. Furthermore, in its second departure, it reaches the first odd, uncompounded number <note>The number three</note> which is more agreeable to its nature, belonging to the indivisible sphere; yet it presents a certain face of a composite nature, being constituted from the initial seeds of things, both even and odd. Then, in the third place, it casts its rays upon the solid body, penetrating sensible things through the circular number of "otherness" <note>Likely referring to the movement from the One to the Many</note>, and incessantly driving toward the most perfect foundation of the denary pyramid <note>The Tetractys, where 1+2+3+4=10, forming a triangular pyramid</note>. Finally, the divine unity inhabits the admirable center of every number, which it endeavors to lead back to itself by a wonderful reflection through a certain circle of individual oddness; for which reason it has marked that very thing with a common character shared with the superior second departure. For the architecture of the universe has arranged only this difference of order between the two, with the body of the world intervening in the middle, so that one may see that unity does not shrink from communication with individuals. By this we perceive that God is willing to dwell with men; the most eminent proof of this conversation is that we ourselves are the performers of wonderful works beyond human strength. At the same time, though established in nature, we rule over nature, and we—mortals—produce monsters, portents, miracles, and the signs of divinity with a single word, which I dared to explain to you long ago.

Then **Sidonius** said: "Proceed then, O **Capnion** <note>The Greek nickname for Johann Reuchlin, meaning 'smoky'</note>. For I truly desire to know where you are heading. As God loves me, I have never heard anything more pleasant: namely, that it is situated and hidden within us to accomplish so many things—so wonderful, so difficult, so sudden, so bold—by words alone, which we consider the easiest and thinnest of all human inventions. It is just as we read of **Medea**: *'And three times she spoke the words that bring peaceful sleep, which stay the troubled sea and the rushing rivers.'* For what can songs <note>original: "carmina," meaning both poems and magic incantations</note> not achieve? Indeed, the Sorceress seemed to me to boast much of these in the works of **Naso** <note>The Roman poet Ovid</note> when she says: *'I stay the shaken waters and shake the still seas with my song; I drive away the clouds and bring them back; I dispel the winds and summon them. I break the viper's jaws with words and song; I move the living rocks and the oaks torn from their earth, and the forests; I command the mountains to tremble and the ground to bellow, and the ghosts to go out from their tombs. You too, O Moon, I draw down.'* Therefore, we must entreat **Baruchias**, and even offer sacrifice to this **Capnion**, so that we may not remain ignorant of what this word is by which he asserts such things are done."

**Baruchias** replied: "I agree with you, and certainly it will be a matter worthy of philosophers to dispute among themselves about such great secrets. Although our race of men <note>The Jewish people</note> was once quite curious about that part of religion in their occult books, yet I will say what I feel: there is no Hebrew in this age whom that power of words wishes to accompany. We mutter the whole night in vain, just as the oracles ceased at **Delphi**, and the **Pythia** was accused by **Demosthenes** of 'Philippizing' <note>Taking bribes from Philip of Macedon to give favorable oracles</note>—unless some sacrilegious person is found who, for the sake of profit, promises a portent to those as foolish as they are easily deceived. I shall speak of other nations first, then I shall remember my own, and likewise others. For..."

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between the characters Capnion (Reuchlin) and Baruchias, focusing on the corruption of sacred things through greed and the nature of true miracles versus superstition.</meta>

# BOOK ONE

<note>Signature: c iii</note>

The Phrygian soothsayer will give answers to the rich, and for a tiny coin, the Jews sell whatever dreams you wish. <note>original: "ere minuto qualiacūq; uoles iudęi somnia uendunt" - a reference to Juvenal's Satires regarding the perceived superstition of the era.</note> So great has the cursed hunger for gold always been among wicked men, that they do not hesitate to contaminate both divine and human affairs with their outrages for the sake of avarice and the desire for profit. Regarding those priests of your time, **Capnion**, <note>Capnion is the Greek nickname for Johannes Reuchlin (1455–1522), the famous humanist and Hebraist.</note> who boast that they are the purest and, as if the grandsons of God, most full of divinity, I would dare to object to nothing, lest you believe that by a singular neglect of that sect I am testing our friendship, which more refined letters have won for us. For that excellent proverb of Terence stands out, and should be embraced with a mindful heart, which says: *Truth breeds hatred.* <note>original: "Veritas odiū parit" - a famous line from Terence's play Andria.</note> Were I not afraid of that, I would bring into the open how they now profane all sacred things for a price, from the highest to the lowest, so that all things are now for sale for a fee. While they are forbidden by the law of action to demand this before a judge, they have sought out a safe refuge by imploring the judge's office so that the ritual of handing over money might not perish—as if it were not a requested fee when it is requested because of "custom," though that which is contrived against God ought not even be called a custom.

But I am silent about what portents and what miracles they preach to the people so that the votive offerings may be burdened with gifts and their entire world may overflow with coins. Just as Accius says: *They enrich the ears of others with words, so that they may enrich their own homes with gold.* <note>Accius was a Roman tragic poet.</note> Not otherwise does this man bring magical chants, and that one Thessalian love-potions, and the Jews sell dreams. Therefore, if avarice were to cease, I believe there would be no one hereafter who would promise that he will perform miracles with words or figures—unless some insane sorceress, <term>saga</term> <note>A female practitioner of magic or a "wise woman," often used pejoratively in this period.</note> possessing divine power, should dwell in the countryside, who wishes to be believed by every dullard to be so skilled as to pull down the sky, suspend the earth, harden fountains, dissolve mountains, raise the spirits of the dead, lower the gods, extinguish the stars, and illuminate the underworld. Such grandmotherly madness has sometimes been caught wandering in the midst of its own outrages, and is punished with the ultimate penalty not only by the Romans, but also by the more ancient laws of our people (that is, the laws of the Hebrews).

At these words, **CAPNION**, touched as if by a certain annoyance, nevertheless fell into the conversation quite modestly and said: "Most dear Baruchias, <note>Baruchias is the Jewish interlocutor in Reuchlin's dialogue.</note> where is your speech leading you, that you dare to persuade us that, unless profit is present, stupendous and admirable things are not often done by men? Things not born of time but arising suddenly, not propagated but present and pressing in a brief moment, the occasion of which no one grasped before, no one hoped for, and which are therefore called miracles—whether you do this by command or perhaps by a vow. For it is so far from the truth that they are easily done for the sake of riches, that even to those devoted to avarice, that venerable liberality of divine gifts is denied. For although the givers of riches are called gods in Hesiod, yet the heavenly ones do not wish to be asked to obtain them, and the infernal ones ought not be—the former to avoid a most wicked fellowship, the latter to avoid the foulness of avarice. There exists the sound doctrine of the ancient philosophers on this matter, by the testimony of Theognis, who says: *I do not love riches and I do not seek them, but let it be mine only to live on modest means and to have no evil.* Your own Solomon, likewise, endowed with no less wisdom and antiquity, expressed the same sentiment with divinity, speaking in these words. I shall indeed speak it in Latin as far as I am able, for it would more justly be your part to interpret it. He says: *Give me neither poverty nor riches; let me take only my appointed bread.* <note>A translation of Proverbs 30:8.</note> As they commonly say,"

<summary>Baruchias critiques the commercialization of religion and superstition, while Capnion (Reuchlin) defends the possibility of true miracles, citing classical and biblical authorities against greed.</summary>
<keywords>Avarice, Miracles, Simony, Johannes Reuchlin, Baruchias, Solomon, Hesiod, Superstition, Jewish-Christian Dialogue</keywords>

--- Page 35 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue regarding the "Wonderful Word" (Verbum Mirificum), specifically debating the origins of ancient Greek philosophy and its debt to Hebrew wisdom.</meta>

-># ON THE WONDERFUL WORD<-

->## Book I, Signature m iij<-

"Give me neither poverty nor riches; feed me with food convenient for me." <note>A quote from Proverbs 30:8</note> But as for those wonderful words which you say have ceased to serve your Jewish rituals, believe me, the cause of their absence must be no less troubling to me. Indeed, it was among your people that this art was once most practiced, so much so that our ancestors, driven by a desire for that philosophy, were led away from the sweet conversation of friends and fellow citizens and from their ancestral hearths—not merely into long travel, but into what was almost a perpetual exile. 

I hear, however, that some are unwilling to agree that Thales, Pythagoras, Plato, and the rest ever reached the Jews, but claim they only went to the Egyptian and Memphite priests. Among their number, Lactantius Firmianus revealed himself to be one, in the fourth volume of the book he titled *Divine Institutes*, perhaps following no one but himself in this. But I am easily forced to a different opinion by the traces of Mosaic reading which I find in their letters and teachings; I would say they bypassed neither Syria, nor Judea, nor even the school of Chaldea <note>Chaldea: an ancient region in Mesopotamia, often associated with astronomers and magi</note>. 

For in its own time, the sacred scripture of the Hebrews began to be held in such honor and in such frequent veneration among the Babylonians that they took care to translate it from its own tongue into the Chaldean language, and this occurred nearly in the fifty-sixth Olympiad, which is to say, in the age of Thales. Therefore, since these men were inflamed with such a great desire for seeking the truth that they sought exile and left their homes so that the most ancient monuments of sacred things might not remain hidden from them, who would be so out of his mind as to doubt that those same men, while seeking Egypt, did not also visit Judea? Would they not especially do so to drink from the fountain itself after having tasted the streams, and to look into the Nabataean rites so they might be imbued with a purer divinity? 

In the delivery of this divinity, the Jews were more noble than all the nations of the world, by the testimony of the oracle itself. This was not unknown to that age because of its daily fame, but was sung throughout the crossroads and villages. I shall try now if I can pluck even two verses from there which harmonize with the Greeks. For thus says the oracle:

->*The Chaldeans alone are wise, and the Hebrews are wise,*<-
->*Who care for the King and God, begotten of himself.*<-

There is more that can confirm my opinion, since there is but a small distance between the Egyptians and the Jews. For this reason, you may often find mixed populations in the same place, consisting of Egyptians, Arabs, Hebrews, and Phoenicians—especially in Herod’s Sebaste (which is also called Samaria), in Philadelphia, in Jericho, and likewise in Galilee. I speak of a matter that is not at all obscure to our most skilled and dear friend Sidonius here, because he was born not far from there.

"You speak the truth indeed," said **SIDONIUS**. "For one sees the continuity of Judea, Egypt, Arabia, and the Babylonians. Therefore, it does not seem probable to me that those philosophers failed to reach Palestine and the wisdom of the Hebrews, especially since I have discovered that they transferred not only the ideas themselves, but almost the very words of that nation into their own books."

"But proceed, **Capnion** <note>Capnion: The Greek-style nickname for Johann Reuchlin, the author</note>."

"And you judge quite rightly. For regarding Plato it is certain; I am accustomed to reading him sometimes for the sake of breathing after a thousand business affairs and the tumults of the court. And O good God, how much I find he has fished out from there, for the most part word for word! If you wish to know these things more extensively, read Justin the Philosopher <note>Justin Martyr, a 2nd-century Christian apologist</note>—and I believe you have read him—though he was a martyr for my religion and a man of no small integrity, and for that reason perhaps his..."

<summary>The speaker (Capnion/Reuchlin) argues that ancient Greek philosophers like Plato and Pythagoras derived their greatest insights from Hebrew and Chaldean sources rather than just Egypt. He cites historical geography and ancient oracles to prove that Jewish wisdom was the "fountain" from which the Greeks drank.</summary>

<keywords>Plato, Pythagoras, Hebrew wisdom, Chaldean oracles, Johann Reuchlin, Justin Martyr, transmission of knowledge, ancient philosophy</keywords>

# BOOK ONE

->c iiii<-

<note>original: "uis" (force/power)</note>

...seen by you not merely in word, but that you might be compelled by the reality itself to agree with me. I return to the cause. That healing power of words which has deserted you, has chosen Us; it accompanies us, and is seen to obey our every nod. That virtue of perfect religion, given and offered not by human counsel but by divine gift, has changed its location—not by chance, but through its own diligence and industry. Because you have changed the legitimate sacred rites, you therefore murmur in vain; in vain do you call upon God, whom you do not worship as He himself wills. Instead, flattering your own inventions, you even hate us, the worshippers of God, with an undying envy—a thing which the Divinity rejects above all else, desiring as He does to dwell in peaceful human minds. Nor would I easily accept our friend Sidonius <note>Sidonius represents the Epicurean/pagan philosophical perspective in this dialogue.</note> unless he could be turned toward a sincere way of believing. For regarding yourself, I have little hope, born of that lineage which has always been stiff-necked, unless the extensive literature in your excellent mind, your many studies, your innumerable doctrines, and even your great love of knowing might effect something.

Then both said: We beseech you, Capnion <note>The Greek-style nickname for Johann Reuchlin, meaning "smoky."</note>, that you do not despair, nor desert us. For that alone is the power of words which surpasses the knowledge of all things. Therefore, we submit our hands to your rod <note>A metaphor for becoming a student under a master's discipline.</note>, in whatever branch of learning we may finally be initiated. I know, as does Baruchias here, <term>that</term> <note>Latin: quod</note> in learning sacred matters, one must walk a far different path <term>than</term> <note>Latin: quam</note> mathematicians and physicists are accustomed to. For those men first think it most convenient to butt heads and contend among themselves, and to fight on both sides of an issue. Then, through certain antecedents—whether you call them axioms or principles—which you cannot refute, they soon conclude by the artifice of a fixed combination, by which you yourself are compelled to stand firm.

But on the contrary, in divine matters, silence is desired, contention is rejected, and the syllogism is laughed at. For there is no "principle" of the Divinity; nothing precedes Him. Therefore, whatever must be concluded, one must immediately acquiesce to it with a condition much firmer <term>than</term> <note>Latin: quam</note> science. What that may be, we have heard from you before. Therefore, renew your discourse, excellent Capnion, and teach us by what words we might be able to perform certain wonders. For I promise on behalf of this companion of ours whatever must be provided. Is it not so, Baruchias?

Then he replied: It is so, Sidonius. For I know you both to be good and learned men—both he who asks and he who promises. Therefore, what I might fear as sacrilege from certain other dull-witted men, I do not fear at all now. Nor do I fear <term>that</term> <note>Latin: quod</note> an injury might be done to the supreme Divinity through some perverse worship. For you both know <term>how</term> <note>Latin: quam</note> wicked this would be, and unworthy of philosophers.

I indeed, said CAPNION, accept your promise. But I shall prescribe laws for you both, which you shall observe without rebellion. Let this be your repentance: You, Baruchias, withdraw from the Talmudists <note>original: "Thalmudim"</note>; and you, Sidonius, from Epicurus and Lucretius <note>Representing the materialist philosophy that denies divine intervention.</note>. Wash yourselves; be clean. Hold to one God, the maker of all things, and have the other powers as His ministers. Let there be vows and prayers to the First; let there be hymns to the lower ones. <term>But if</term> <note>Latin: Quod si</note> by chance a petition should proceed to the lower ones, let there be no such intention unless it is under the manner of an administration delegated by the First. Let the Angels, flying from us to God and from Him to us, be held in reverence and trembling. Toward them, according to their known proof, let there be a pleasant obedience. Let the sacred rites, the observations of which you have come to hear from me, be open to the worthy and hidden from the profane. These are the edicts and the prohibitions alike.

<summary>Capnion (Reuchlin) sets the moral and philosophical requirements for his students, Baruchias and Sidonius, demanding they abandon their previous sectarian biases (Talmudic and Epicurean) to study the divine power of words. He establishes a hierarchy of worship, placing the one Creator above the angelic ministers.</summary>
<keywords>Capnion, Reuchlin, Baruchias, Sidonius, sacred names, divine power, Talmud, Epicurus, Angels, religious discipline, Neoplatonism</keywords>

# ON THE WONDER-WORKING WORD
### aiii 3

I have judged that these matters should first be written down on a white tablet, so that you may weigh them carefully in your minds and decide whether they are to be accepted or rejected. For I have determined to admit you into this secret Academy of sacred names under no other condition than as a schoolmaster might, provided you first agree to such laws. Indeed, when you willingly enter into these things I have proposed, I hope you will very easily reach those things you desire.

Then **SIDONIUS** said: Thus far, I think you have ordained nothing intolerable, unless perhaps this Baruchias wishes to see the law itself as harsh regarding the *Talmudists* <note>original: "Thalmudim"; referring to the students or practitioners of the Talmud</note>, whom I once convinced myself—while reading—were arrogant factions and conspiracies of many in their own madness, not to be followed; especially wherever it pertains to divinity, since they twist sacred things into hatred, slaughter, and plunder of foreign nations rather than converting them to the salvation of the world. Yet I think he will easily let this pass if he recalls to mind that they were men like us, who committed their inventions and meditations to writing for the sake of ostentation, just as there are commentaries by certain scholastics which anyone may certainly abandon without penalty. In the same way, I have become a defector from Lucretius and Epicurus <note>Epicurus and Lucretius were ancient philosophers associated with atomism and the denial of divine intervention, often viewed skeptically by Renaissance theologians</note>, for I never swore an oath to their words or those of anyone else. The remaining decrees belong to his own ancestral profession. Therefore, by my counsel, he will not reject any of them.

As for myself, why should I not undergo what right philosophy once persuaded me of as a boy? For many centuries now, the entire sect of Sardanapalus <note>A reference to the decadent Assyrian king, used here to represent hedonistic philosophy</note>—not without its own authors Leucippus, Democritus, Epicurus, and Lucretius—has been frequently exploded <note>rejected or driven out</note> by good and most distinguished men. Why should I doubt your other laws, which I learned previously from many men of various kinds, and especially from those teaching the magic arts? For regarding the ritual washing, when I was in the assembly of the wisest men of India, whom they call Brahmans <note>original: "Brachmanas"</note>, I turned that custom of washing almost into a habit of my own, having been deeply initiated by the length of daily use. I was accustomed to wash daily with them in a spring not unlike a certain one in Boeotia called Dirce, having first stripped off my clothes and anointed my head with amber drops and scents fit for that purpose. Then, after they thought themselves sufficiently clean according to the rite, we had to go out around midday, as many as stood there for the sacred rites in the manner of a choir, dressed in white linen and a white miter <note>a ceremonial headdress</note>, wearing rings on our fingers and carrying staffs in our hands. This cleanliness of body and dress was believed, as they said, to aid the purity of the soul in no small measure. And these things were done under the priest Vardane among the Indians, the fourth from Hiarcha. For after Hiarcha came Sauchnes, after him Sefonchoris, then Bocchoris, so named after the ancient kings of the Egyptians.

I also accepted a similar practice when I stayed among the Gymnosophists <note>literally "naked philosophers," ancient Indian ascetics</note> for the sake of learning; and even more so, because it was a sacred rule to wash in cold water three times by day and twice by night before I could enter the inner sanctum of the sacred rites at those same moments. Likewise, there was a linen garment freshly washed every day. For they consider wool a profane clothing, since it is pulled from sheep as the excrement of a beast's filth. We read also in Hesiod that this kind of precept is especially suited to divine matters. For he says (as you know) in the book of *Works and Days* almost thus:

->*No one at dawn would dare to pour wine to Jove*<-
->*With unwashed hands, nor to any of the immortals.*<-
->*They do not wish to hear such a man, and they spurn his prayers.*<-

And in another poem thus:

->*When a wicked man crosses a river without washing his hands,*<-
->*The gods are angry with him, bringing sorrows thereafter.*<-

Telemachus took diligent care against this. For when praying to Pallas <note>Athena</note>...

<summary>Sidonius agrees to Capnion's rules for the Academy, critiquing the Talmudists and Epicureans while recounting his experiences with purification rituals among Indian Brahmans and Gymnosophists. He emphasizes that physical cleanliness is a prerequisite for spiritual purity, citing classical authorities like Hesiod.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical defense of specific "laws" or principles of worship, likely following a discussion of ritual purity and the nature of the divine. The author is synthesizing classical Greek poetry with theological arguments.</meta>

# BOOK ONE

...about to offer a plea, he washed his hands in the sea. Do you not see that this custom was not first devised by you, but has been commended for many centuries? That "all-seeing" blind man, our Homer, I believe was hardly ignorant of this when he depicted the mother of Achilles <note>Thetis</note> about to become a suppliant to Jupiter <note>original: "Ioui"; the Roman name for Zeus</note>, making her first cast off the waves of the sea. Although he may also seem to have explained another mystery greater than that, it is of no relevance to this discourse. 

But as for the third law you brought to us, the clearest dogmas of the most ancient authors prove its worth. Hear what Sophocles brings forth in this Iambic distich—which, though not translated by me with great elegance, is, I think, not unsuitably rendered—when he says: 

->**In truth, there is only one God,**<-
->**Who fashioned this heaven and the spacious earth.**<-

Euripides likewise teaches that he should be named Jupiter. By his head (as Orpheus sang) all things proceeded into the light; Orpheus praises him as the father of all things in a single word, both the beginning and the end, in the first hymn to that same god. 

As for the fact that the other powers should be considered ministers, I believe you learned this from the poems of Homer, in the eighth volume of the *Trojan Rhapsodies* <note>The Iliad</note>. There, in a splendid speech full of majesty, Jupiter harangues the assembly of gods, declaring himself the supreme dispenser of all things and these others to be inferiors; nothing is to be attempted except by his nod, and those who act otherwise shall be cast down into Tartarus, so that it may appear how much more powerful Jupiter is than both gods and men. The poem is well known to you; render it from Greek into Latin as you prefer. 

Indeed, the fourth law is derived skillfully from this, as if in the manner of the logicians. For if he is the first who dispenses every single thing, it is in vain that one flees to the others, whether by prayer or by vow. As Nausicaa, daughter of Alcinous, once persuaded her guest: 

->**Jupiter himself dispenses all things to men from above,**<-
->**Whether to the upright or the perverse, as he favors each case.**<-

Therefore, Achilles by no means sought out Thetis so that she might bring him safety against the king from whom he suffered unworthy violence; rather, with outstretched hands and weeping, he prayed in this manner: 

->**But you, if you are able, embrace the cause of your son,**<-
->**And implore Jupiter, ascending the threshold of Olympus.**<-

For this master and father of the universe, as Menander says in *Diphilus*, we shall deem worthy of this highest honor, as he alone is the discoverer and possessor of such great blessings. For nature stands before us in such a state that when a clear vein of a spring can be reached, we should abstain from the mere rivulets. 

What then? Shall we, contrary to the institutes of our ancestors, shun the gods above and seek the aid of Jupiter alone? And should we not rather follow the warning of Theognis: 

->**A man must be daring in hard circumstances,**<-
->**And ask the gods above to snatch him from them?**<-

But perhaps we should use their labor just as we use the help of friends, or the conveniences of neighbors, or the help of kinsmen and prudent men, insofar as they are able to benefit us with counsel and consolation. By their erudition, their warnings, and their always faithful impulse, we can without doubt conform our minds in any matter to the divine will. For they are more skilled in divinity <term>diuinitas: the study or nature of divine things</term> and know us better than we know ourselves, along with all the circumstances of our affairs. Therefore, they subordinate us and our affairs more certainly and much more aptly to the immutable decrees of the sovereign God—just as certain polished bodies reflect specific rays—so that from there, either it may go well for us, or evils may be turned away. This is why, whether the gods are benevolent or turned against us, we either escape this fate or endure that one. Whence there was an ancient fame, even in the memory of our fathers and ancestors, that such majesty belongs to the gods...

<summary>The author argues for the supremacy of a single Creator (Jupiter/Zeus) using classical Greek authorities like Homer, Sophocles, and Menander, while positioning lesser gods as intermediary ministers or "helpers" similar to friends or neighbors.</summary>

# ON THE WONDER-WORKING WORD
<note>original: "DE VERBO MIRIFICO" — This is Johannes Reuchlin's 1494 treatise on the Kabbalah and the power of sacred names.</note>

...which the fates imposed upon mortals. It is an unworthy sentiment for a little woman already being overcome by sleep, who addresses **Ulysses** in the nineteenth book <note>of the Odyssey</note> (if I remember correctly) with these words: *As many as the immortals have appointed, so is the fate for mortals dwelling upon the fruit-bearing earth.* <note>original: "Quotis quibuſque ſtatuerunt immortales fatum mortalibus frugiferam terram habitantibus."</note> Thus **Socrates** proclaimed himself guided by a divine warning. Thus **Aegeria** led **Numa**. <note>Numa Pompilius, the second king of Rome, was said to be instructed by the nymph Aegeria.</note> Thus no one was ever truly a **Pythagorean** who did not perceive the gods. Indeed, because they are good, honor and reverence are due to them as portions closest to the "First Good." For honor and praise follow "The Good" by its very nature, as **Aristotle’s** *Ethics* bear witness. Since honor is deferred to superiors, it is as if it were a retribution for virtue. Moreover, the gods are greater, and not merely as our companions, but even as our rulers, because by their own nature they flourish more in reason and intellect; for this reason, they are sometimes called "Intelligences." Furthermore, your following laws are proved by preceding decrees in the manner of the mathematicians. **Sophocles** also showed the ultimate [conclusion] when he says in *Orestes*: *The superior must be obeyed in all things.* And in *Ajax* he uses these verses: *For he in whom fear flourishes along with reverence, believe that man shall soon have safety.* Therefore, **Capnion**, <note>The Greek-style nickname for the author, Johannes Reuchlin.</note> it will be very easy for me to offer my consent to your commands, certainly in that cause which I have known to be accepted by learned and good men for many thousands of years.

Then **Baruchias** <note>The Jewish representative in the dialogue.</note> said: "Sidonius, even the age of our ancestors is sometimes found to have wandered. Do not think that everything from former times granted for learning must also be followed, unless reason first discusses whether those things are true. I would not wish to bring this up led by any rashness, as if I desired to resist these promulgated laws, or because I insidiously distrust your other arguments, which you bring forth abundantly into the midst of us on account of your outstanding elegance and your skill in various matters. For I myself will also undergo them, even if compelled by no ready reason, save for the sole authority of divine revelation. For I am in agreement with you regarding the assertion of **Cotta** the priest in **Cicero**, which he confidently showed to **Balbus**: that in religion, our ancestors ought to be believed without any reason being rendered. **Plato** thought them the sons of the gods, who knew their own parents best. And therefore, just as with domestic matters, when they affirm something, the highest faith must be offered. 

But the vanity of the common people has often mixed certain falsehoods with truths, which the philosophers themselves could scarcely escape without danger to their lives. Just as they accused **Diagoras** of a crime for writing many things about divine matters differently than seemed right to the common folk, with a legal warrant for his head being offered; but he avoided imminent death by swift flight. **Anaxagoras** was judged at Athens with the ultimate punishment, and **Socrates** was killed by poison. Our own wise men, whom we once called 'Seers' and now call 'Prophets,' often underwent the indignation of kings, sometimes of the people, and occasionally death itself for the sake of religion and divine things. You remember that **Jeroboam** raised his hand against the man of God, which was soon turned into a withered stump; **Isaiah** was cut into pieces; **Jeremiah** was overwhelmed by stones in Egypt; **Ezekiel** was slain by the command of a leader in Assyria; **Daniel** was condemned to the beasts; **Amos** was killed with a club; **Micah** was cast into a precipice; **Zechariah** was slaughtered at the altar. For no other cause did they all suffer than that they perceived things more holily than the unlearned commoners. Therefore, you had added quite justly: 'By learned and good men.' However, nothing weighs more heavily upon me, nothing more troubles me now..."

<summary>The speakers discuss the necessity of honoring divine authority and the historical danger of sharing profound religious truths with the unlearned public, citing the martyrdom of Greek philosophers and Hebrew prophets.</summary>
<keywords>authority of ancestors, divine revelation, martyrdom of prophets, Socrates, Isaiah, Jeremiah, esoteric knowledge vs. common people, Reuchlin, Kabbalah</keywords>

# BOOK ONE

Nothing weighs on me more, nor is there anything I shrink from more, than to lack—not just for a time but forever—such great glory, such splendor, and such good reputation among our most learned men (whom they call <term>Talmudists</term><note>original: "Thalmudim"; referring to scholars of the Talmud, the central text of Jewish civil and ceremonial law</note> for their learning). But since you wish it so, <insert>Capnion</insert><note>The Greek-style nickname for the author, Johannes Reuchlin, meaning "smoky"</note>, let your will be done; I shall cast aside even the Talmudists, in whom, from my tenderest years—as soon as I began to have any sense—my greatest pleasure always resided, making the rest of my life more pleasant. I hope likewise that I shall be doing something not ungrateful to God if, for His sake, I reject what was most dear to my soul. For it has now happened—and I know not if it ever happened otherwise—that I can easily do without all other things, provided that I do not miss out on that holy knowledge of wonderful names which you seemed to promise us, or, if I remember correctly, lest I be accused of wickedly carping at your words.

You said you remembered a certain word (I know not what) by which we mortals, though established in nature, might rule over nature, and perform wonders, portents, and miracles, which are the marks of divinity. Come then, Capnion, let us not remain ignorant of this word. For my fellow countrymen hide many words as if under a bushel and keep them among their secrets, which are judged by them to be miraculous; I myself have read much of them, but I have never felt their effects. This is perhaps because I either did not observe the proper method, or I believed fictions to be truths.

"You have hit the nail on the head," said **SIDONIUS**. "For here 'a snake lies hidden in the grass'<note>A quote from Virgil's Eclogues, meaning a hidden danger or deception</note>; this is something that contributes not a little to the matter. For I once had a similar passion for certain triflers writing on the magical art, men not even worthy of having paper supplied for their pens. Moved by the index of their books, I thought I would find there certain marvelous skills, such as the operations of Zoroaster, Oromasis, Zamolxis, Epimenides, Orpheus, Pythagoras, Hostanes, Empedocles, or even the Thessalian factions of the younger men: Carinondas, Damigerontis, Apollonius, and John Dardanus. But listen while I read what it really is. By Pollux! It is nothing other than that line of Horace: *The mountains are in labor, and a ridiculous mouse will be born.*

These scoundrels hang splendid titles before the doors of their volumes; at one moment they lie that this is the Book of Enoch (whom antiquity asserted was more divine than all others), at another that it is the Book of Solomon, easily creeping into unlearned ears by this notable conjecture which one might divine from the books of Josephus. For he says in the eighth book of his *Antiquities* that Solomon discovered an art against demons for the benefit and cure of men, and instituted incantations against ailments, and even a method of exorcisms. To this he adds an experiment which he himself saw. Nevertheless, it is readily available from the sacred histories of the Hebrews that Solomon, in his age, used to discourse on trees and herbs, from the cedar of Lebanon even unto the hyssop; likewise on beasts, birds, reptiles, and fish—all of which could at least display a 'natural magic.' 

But to one looking more sharply, the deceptive trickery of these charlatans openly betrays itself, and the volumes themselves profess to have been fabricated long ago in our own age. This is clear if one considers the custom of the words, the force of the sentences, and the very type of character used. For nothing will prevent it being clearly seen that all such books have recently gushed forth from the same wallow of conspiracy in our own time. Furthermore, even if they have inserted into their volumes things from the actions of the ancients that relate properly to the art, they are unable to preserve anything unbroken due to their lack of learning—neither the words themselves, nor...

<summary>The speaker agrees to set aside his Talmudic studies to learn a "miraculous word" from Capnion, while Sidonius warns that many magical texts attributed to ancient figures like Solomon or Enoch are modern forgeries.</summary>
<keywords>Talmudists, Capnion, Sidonius, magic, Solomon, Josephus, forgeries, Kabbalah, ancient philosophers</keywords>
