<meta>This page continues the dialogue between the three scholars—the Jew Baruchias, the Christian Capnion (Reuchlin), and the Epicurean Sidonius—regarding the nature of sacred languages and the necessity of faith in performing wonders.</meta>

# ON THE WONDER-WORKING WORD

-> Book II <-

...numbers nor figures. For they are ignorant that the Egyptians use a double character <note>original: "duplici charactere," referring to the distinction between hieratic/demotic and hieroglyphic scripts</note>, one for profane matters, the other for sacred. Finally, we write the language of the Chaldeans <note>Aramaic</note> and the Hebrews—which they attest is necessary for magical work—with a similar appearance of characters. Therefore, none of these men—neither Robert, nor Bacon <note>Roger Bacon, 13th-century English philosopher</note>, nor Abano <note>Peter of Abano, an Italian physician and astrologer</note>, nor Picatrix <note>A famous medieval grimoire of astrological magic</note> *and the council of masters*—were able to hold or teach these things precisely as they ought, chiefly due to their ignorance of languages. Even less could the hands of the librarians avoid errors when copying from double examples, less could the students learn, and less could the practitioners operate. I return, therefore, to the opinion of Baruchias: although I have read abundantly many secrets of this kind, yet I have nowhere perceived their effects, perhaps because a suitable master was also absent, upon whose labor and genius those things depended. Therefore, do not be loath to recite them again.

And **CAPNION** said: It is already evening, and that time is imminent in which such a great and divine business—plainly a priesthood, and most worthy of wise philosophers and the light itself—cannot be completed before night. For we shall not imitate the Brahmans <note>Ancient Indian philosophers</note> who contemplate by night, for whom black is the most cultivated color because the frequent heat of the sun blackens almost all their things. Likewise, this place is unsuitable for such arcane ceremonies, unequal to such a celestial gift, and far too profane for sacred rites; therefore, it seems it must be changed. If it is also your opinion, we should withdraw into a small chapel, as such most excellent sacraments might persuade us. Therefore, I judge it much better to defer the whole matter until tomorrow, when there is the opportunity to meet at my suburban house, which is set almost in a grove. Meanwhile, on this night, remember what we have decided while deliberating: that one thing above all must be felt, most constantly believed, and most openly proclaimed and praised. For **faith alone is the right door to miracles**, which if you neglect, you cannot penetrate, and you would be justly barred from the inner sanctuaries and the assemblies of the oracles.

Then **SIDONIUS** said: The decree, the time, and the place please me well. But take care that you do not doubt our faith. Led by your integrity as well as your singular learning, I believe whatever you shall command.

And **BARUCHIAS** said: I shall believe no differently than your opinion holds. Unless I did so, Sidonius would cite to me the poems of the most ancient Linus <note>A legendary figure in Greek mythology, often associated with the origins of song and poetry</note>, in which he instructs that all things must be believed. For nothing is incredible; all things are easy for God; nothing is impossible. The verses of Linus, word for word (as you know), suggest these things. But also Pallas <note>Athena</note> in Homer, whom I recall on this occasion, introduces Achilles, consoling him with such speech: *I have come to quiet your anger, if you will believe.* I would more gladly bring forward some of my own sources, namely the history of Daniel, which was written not in Hebrew but in Chaldean <note>Aramaic</note>. For by this alone—that the holy man believed in the Divinity—he escaped the dangers of the lions unharmed. For thus the truth of the speech holds: *And no injury was found in him, because he believed in his God.* Therefore, I too, along with Sidonius, will believe whatever you command.

Then **CAPNION**, soon bringing forth certain tablets, displayed them and said: The matter goes well. Each of you take these home, in which all things of the true faith are briefly described in the manner of a catalog, which I decree you must believe beforehand, so that we do not join our appointed assembly in vain. Farewell.

And then "Farewell happily, Capnion," each responded while departing.

<summary>The dialogue concludes for the evening with Capnion emphasizing that linguistic precision and absolute faith are the prerequisites for divine magic. He provides his companions with tablets outlining the "true faith" to study before they reconvene at his secluded villa.</summary>
<keywords>faith (fides), sacred languages, Egyptian characters, Roger Bacon, Peter of Abano, Picatrix, Linus, Daniel in the lions' den, miracles, Capnion (Reuchlin)</keywords>

# THE SECOND BOOK

# OF JOHANN REUCHLIN OF PFORZHEIM, DOCTOR OF LAWS: CAPNION, OR, ON THE MIRACULOUS WORD

The first day having been happily completed, O Holy Prelate Dalberg <note>Johann von Dalberg (1455–1503), Bishop of Worms and Reuchlin's patron</note>, I pray you listen patiently to how the three philosophers, having awakened after sleep, meet before dawn and spend the entire day debating the miraculous word. Baruchias presides over this day as the leader, just as Sidonius did yesterday. For today you will learn much more taken from the books of the Chaldeans and the Hebrews. To capture the beginning of these matters, Sidonius began thus:

We are present, Capnion <note>Reuchlin’s own Greek-style pseudonym, meaning "little smoke"</note>, and we almost outpace the dawn itself, persistent as we are. For I see this man Baruchias not merely walking but hurrying, lest he neglect any of your concerns. If you wish, I shall apply the spurs even to a running horse. "Hey there, Baruchias, into the chapel!" But he, with breath hardly recovered, said: "I am here with you, Sidonius, though a little later than intended, yet still early, I think, 'since the dawn has not yet moved the damp shadow from the sky' <note>A poetic reference to Virgil's Aeneid</note>. Certainly this place is worthy of a philosopher, and I marvel at it, Capnion—that contrary to the custom of your nation (or rather now ours, since you have made us your own), it contains no idols—which you call *signa* in Latin—nor any images, unless something lies hidden in the inner chambers or these very shrines. And behold, there is the cross itself."

Then Sidonius said: "Leave that for now, Baruchias. Do you not know what is on the agenda? Beware of causing us trouble by being the author of a longer delay. But come, Capnion, teach us about the miraculous word—or if it should be some plural name. For we do not press the matter like grammarians, provided it is that by which we, though situated in nature, might be able to work miracles above nature, as you were saying. Whether this happens through foreseeing, as in prophecies, or by effecting the things themselves, the natural cause of which is commonly unknown and therefore admired even by the most skilled men."

"For there are certain things so composed by nature that we marvel at them because they are seen rarely or never: such as the Hermaphrodites, formerly called Androgynes; or the Phoenix of which it is sung: 'Egypt assembles for the miracle of such a sight, and the shouting crowd greets the rare bird' <note>original: "Conuenit Aegyptus tanti ad miracula uisus, Et raram uolucrem turba salutat ouans."</note>. Herodotus says that although he waited a long time for it, he never saw it except in a painting. Likewise, the birth of five boys at once, and other monstrous things. And if we ourselves manufacture any wonders with the aid of nature—as is found in Democritus concerning the liver of a Chameleon burnt on the highest roof-tiles, which stirs up rain and thunder; or even the wooden dove of Archytas of Tarentum <note>A Greek mathematician and philosopher (4th century BCE) credited with building the first flying machine</note> which he had designed to fly, the like of which we ourselves have seen and know how to fashion. These and similar things we produce either by perceiving nature or by using nature as an aid."

"This physical reasoning easily proves itself to unlettered men, mechanics, and small minds. I do not strive to learn any of these things from you. For they are so common that they are read in the streets and crossroads and even in the daily lecture halls of the sophists. Nor do I seek those miraculous machines which the astrologers promise—whether in composing images, or engraving rings, or predicting events, or dispensing fortune and misfortune, and applying the altogether incomprehensible power of the heavens to the natures of lower things individually—which, because they are so deceptive and so..."

<summary>The second day of the dialogue begins before dawn in a chapel. Sidonius and Baruchias petition Capnion (Reuchlin) to move beyond natural wonders and mechanical tricks to discuss the truly supernatural "Miraculous Word."</summary>
<keywords>Baruchias, Sidonius, Capnion, Miraculous Word, natural magic, Archytas's dove, Phoenix, Hebrew wisdom, Chaldean books</keywords>

--- Page 43 ---
<meta>This page begins mid-sentence, continuing a critique of astrology and the occult arts. The speaker is addressing a Jewish interlocutor named Baruchia (Moyses).</meta>

# ON THE WONDER-WORKING WORD

...diverse things occur among them, so that even the authors of that school oppose one another. They exist with such insurmountable diligence and infinite labor that they cannot help but fall under an art that is not without falsehood. I would prefer to speak the truth in tranquility than to deceive everyone with the greatest labors, having declared that condition to be ridiculous which teaches one to lie with devoted effort. For the clearest argument for truth is harmony; on the contrary, the argument for a lie is discord. 

But what will you find that is constant, firm, and sincere in that art which they profess? I do not speak of the measurement of the stars—than which nothing is more certain, as both mathematicians and experience itself indicate—but of the judgment conceived from them <note>i.e., judicial astrology or fortune-telling</note>, whose entire path is deceptive and whose opinion is foolish, especially in those things which exceed the qualities of bodies, elemental dispositions, the moments of the year, and the signs of the seasons, as your Baruchia Moyses <note>Likely a reference to a contemporary or historical Jewish scholar known to the author</note> suggests. 

See how varied they are among themselves! For Albumasar <note>original: "Abumasar"; a famous 9th-century Persian astrologer</note>, whom they adore as the president and master of that faculty, says when he deals with judgments that some astrologers think the planets signify only universal things, such as the elements and the species of natural things. But concerning individuals and the parts and acts of individuals—namely, to be moved, to be at rest, and the like—they demonstrate nothing at all. Others, however, think the stars show only what is necessary, for instance that fire is indeed hot and water cold, but not those things which are contingent, such as whether a man speaks or remains silent. Finally, others are of the opinion that the planets portend only the changes of the seasons and the extent to which they affect bodies. 

The later astrologers follow the ancients, yet none of the sayings of the ancients contribute to the intention of judging, since they say it would be impossible even for wise astronomers to find anything out regarding the science of judgments. Nevertheless, they dare to introduce a discipline by which they strive to persuade us that they know even the smallest points of human thought, and can pervert and reverse the entire nature of things. 

The admonition of Ptolemy <note>Claudius Ptolemy, the influential 2nd-century astronomer</note> does not terrify them at all, who asserts that such prediction deceives us for the most part. Nothing of the slippery reasoning of their principles turns them away—reasoning very similar to a vain fiction, which the ancients understood one way, Ptolemy another, Haimon another, and Hermes <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary father of Hermeticism</note> another. Nor does the infinite assembly of secondary causes which happen to a single thing deter them. Nor does the great distance between us and our stars, nor the innumerable mediums between us. Nor does that uncertain heap of stars, that incomprehensible multitude, to the point that there are some which have not yet been seen, and some arise for the first time which they confess they have never beheld—whose power must necessarily be admitted in the substances about to arise and be ruled. This is true even though a star as small as the one some call the Dog and others Sirius <note>The "Dog Star," associated with the sweltering "dog days" of summer</note> adds so much heat to the ruling planets in the month of August <note>original: "sextili mense"; the sixth month of the Roman calendar, later named August</note>, though it is scarcely seen. 

Nor do I demand much of you to explain to us in what ways it happens that miracles are performed through magical faction. For I have wasted no small amount of time in that illusion; although it was in vain, I do not yet repent of it, since by the highest reasons I can more easily turn other mortals away from such great destruction. For when you have consumed an entire lifetime in such sacrilegious business, you will at last extort from it nothing that is real, but only what by chance might seem to be, and this with the expectation of the greatest danger to life and limb. Thus I myself knew Robert the Englishman <note>original: "Roberthū Anglicū"</note>, who died miserably among the Swiss, and another whose name I withhold, who was more miserably captured and punished in reputation. Many others, even the most flourishing in that art, have ended most miserably...

<summary>The narrator critiques judicial astrology and magic, arguing that while astronomy is a valid science, predicting human fate or performing magic is a deceptive and dangerous vanity. He cites various authorities like Albumasar and Ptolemy to highlight the contradictions within the field.</summary>
<keywords>astrology, astronomy, Albumasar, Ptolemy, Hermes Trismegistus, Sirius, magic, Robert the Englishman, divination, critique of occultism</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue from Book One, transitioning into the core arguments of Book Two. The speaker, Sidonius, is concluding his critique of traditional Greco-Egyptian magic and astrology before yielding the floor to Capnion (Johannes Reuchlin).</meta>

# SECOND BOOK

-> d ii <-

I have heard from the most trustworthy men that they have departed from this world in such a state. The powers of darkness are accustomed to treat their followers with honors of this kind. But if there are any who think they practice magic not through the bindings or incantations of evil spirits, but by the quiet help of good spirits <note>original: "bonorum dæmonum"</note>—as perhaps it seemed to them—nevertheless, they have everything so entangled that it is first necessary to observe and mix together all the universal rules of the astrologers (which we refuted in few words above) along with the prescribed rites of their own sacred ceremonies to the letter <note>literally: "to the fingernail," meaning with perfect precision</note>. Indeed, this is too much for the human mind to endure, not to say impossible. For a man’s entire lifespan is scarcely sufficient just for reading the books of that faculty, of which **Mercury** <note>Refers to Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary founder of Hermeticism</note> alone is said to have written thirty-six thousand, five hundred and twenty-five volumes encompassing the universals of sacred rites. What do you think of the rest? 

Now finally, I grant that at last, through long vigils, great effort, and diligent observation, you have abundantly mastered the admirable secrets of arcane magic along with the inventions of the astrologers—by which the spirits of the dead <note>term: "manes" (the souls of deceased ancestors or underworld deities)</note> obey, the stars are disturbed, the divine powers are compelled, and the elements serve. Suppose you wish, with the unassailable power of that discipline and the blind violence of the compelled deities—or if you prefer, with a timely crowd of bright gods—to perform an operation free from all unskillfulness. Even then, you will be so astonished and stupefied by such a great mob of demons (who precede the deity you wish to use as a favorable influence in your sacred acts, like precursors and a sort of procession) that you will shrink back in horror and leave the work unfinished, resulting in harm to yourself and mockery from others. 

For it is known from the authors **Iamblichus** and **Porphyry** <note>Neoplatonist philosophers who wrote on theurgy and the soul</note>—though known to no one except those priests already experienced in the labor itself—how great a multitude of forces is usually stirred up when the gods descend and move. Truly, he who does not follow each one with its own specific honor, nor distributes to all what is fitting for each, nor conducts himself according to their custom with thanks, offerings, and gifts most similar to their condition, does not attain the presence of the deities or the desired effects; just as in a harmony, if one string is broken, the whole suddenly sounds discordant. These are the places, these the foundations of the discipline, and the very methods of the art. 

Therefore, **Capnion** <note>The Greek nickname for Johannes Reuchlin, meaning "Smoky"</note>, you have relieved us of a heavy annoyance and an unbearable burden by your promise—you who have come to free our study from so many stars, so many demons, so many precepts, so many rites and vain observations. For, to omit how many and how great the heavenly beings are (who are innumerable according to the infinite thoughts or "Ideas" of the First Mind), of whom they say that ancient **Orpheus** introduced three hundred and fifty gods by name; we shall consider only the earthly deities closest to us, of whom that most noble writer of equal antiquity, **Hesiod**, numbered thirty thousand in his book *Works and Days*. To sacrifice to each of these is beyond man and surpasses our strength. But now the sun is rising; begin, most adorned Capnion.

Then he said: "If nature had produced me a little more fluent in speaking and not so morose, certainly, as I reflect upon it, no small thanks would be owed to you, **Sidonius**, who have shown such a forest of miracles from which hewn wood might be most abundantly supplied to us for the construction of such a divine work, to be illustrated amply and magnificently. And if I were one from the school of **Chrysippus** <note>A Stoic philosopher known for rigorous logic</note>, I would greatly rejoice in these riches of most ornate eloquence proposed by you, from which my own speech could be made more copious. But I am not chosen for that..."

<summary>Sidonius concludes that traditional pagan magic and astrology are impossibly complex and dangerous due to the sheer number of deities and rituals required. He invites Capnion (Reuchlin) to present a simpler, more divine way to understand the spiritual world.</summary>
<keywords>Hermes Trismegistus, Iamblichus, Porphyry, Orpheus, Hesiod, Neoplatonism, theurgy, planetary magic, Johannes Reuchlin</keywords>

--- Page 45 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue of Reuchlin’s "On the Wonder-Working Word," where the speakers discuss the requirements for studying divine secrets.</meta>

# ON THE WONDER-WORKING WORD

I was neither born nor trained for such displays, nor does the subject at hand allow for it. On the contrary, that which is divine and sacred rejects an abundance of speech and ideas disguised with flowery words, especially in a doctrine dedicated to God alone, where every blemish must be avoided. For we are warned in the sacred texts that "in much speaking, sin will not be lacking" <note>original: "in multiloquio non deerit peccatum"; a reference to Proverbs 10:19</note>. In a similar way, the more refined letters command us, as Horace says: "Whatever you teach, be brief" <note>original: "Quicquid praecipies esto brevis"; from Horace's Ars Poetica</note>. This I intend to do both by design and necessity, so that I may complete everything shortly. 

Indeed, if I had not already begun to hold the highest familiarity with you, I would—after the manner of the **Pythagoreans** <note>Followers of Pythagoras, known for their strict silence and long periods of observation before being initiated into secrets</note>—first examine each of you deeply, perceiving each one's nature, way of life, interests, disposition, and age. I would require patience of the tongue and ears, and silence. For as **Gregory of Nazianzus** says, it is not granted to everyone, nor always, nor regarding all things, to philosophize about God; rather, there are specific people, times, and limits. 

However, the "yesterday's tablets" <note>likely referring to the previous day's discussion or notes</note> which you keep at home make you prepared and fair colleagues for this business. Nor do I demand more from you than what **Simo** in Terence's play sought from **Sosia**: "loyalty and silence." For you have reached more than twenty-five years of age; before this limit, as **Baruchias** <note>The Jewish interlocutor in this dialogue</note> here will testify is true, it is not permitted for any of the Hebrews to touch the scriptures of divine secrets. Thus, the same praiseworthy custom shall exist among us. 

Furthermore, this most sacred function of ours must not be betrayed to the profane multitude, but only to priests—a point about which **Plato** carefully warned **Dionysius of Syracuse**, and **Dionysius the Areopagite** warned **Theophilus**. Without controversy, we are indeed priests—even if no hands of Bishops have been laid upon us—provided that we hold divine wisdom alongside religious worship. For the opinion of **Lactantius Firmianus** is true, in those volumes he wrote to the Emperor Constantine: that the teachers of true wisdom are the same as the priests of God. Therefore, lest we be considered profane, both the handling of true philosophy and the reverence for the one God forbid it. Only humility makes us fit listeners for such a great matter, hope makes us attentive, and faith makes us teachable. But most excellent of all is a devoted love, which preserves us in our work and grants the results of our prayers.

Therefore, although every art of miracles—if any can be truly so named, as our friend **Sidonius** here has learnedly and diligently reviewed—is divided into three parts, each of which is a special and distinct faculty (namely **Physics** <note>Natural philosophy</note>, **Astrology**, and **Magic**, which contains within itself both **Goetia** <note>Low magic or sorcery</note> and **Theurgy** <note>High magic or "divine work"</note>), and although they are all so joined and related that he who promises Astrology without Physics achieves nothing, and he who dares to profess Magic without Astrology and Physics wanders far from true reason—yet you yourselves reject them all. You do so because of their vain outcomes and frustrated results after immense and laborious study. 

You have asked me again to enter a "fourth way" with you, which we may call **Soliloquies** <note>original: "soliloquia"; here referring to a direct, internal communion with the Divine</note>, where whatever is proposed succeeds according to the favorable prayers of those waiting. I will do this as far as is permitted and as much as lies within me; I will deny nothing of what you so diligently ask. But know this first: it was decreed long ago by the Pythagoreans and by Plato himself that the perception of divine things cannot be found by us through any [human] art. Rather, it is revealed from above—a point which **Plotinus** gave and **Proclus** confirmed, and to which not only your theologians but also ours attest. It will be fitting for us first to offer ourselves as purified and to commend...

<summary>The narrator discusses the spiritual and intellectual requirements for studying divine mysteries, arguing that true wisdom is a form of priesthood and that divine secrets are granted through revelation rather than mere human arts like astrology or magic.</summary>
<keywords>Pythagorean silence, divine revelation, priesthood of wisdom, magic vs. theurgy, Baruchias, Lactantius, Plato, spiritual preparation</keywords>

# BOOK TWO

<note>Signature: d iii</note>

to the deifying light; and then, with our senses closed and a quiet mind, we must wait for that rain of super-celestial water descending upon our heads, praising and at times adoring that sea of goodness. From this sea flow not only the streams of the wondrous Name and the sacred words, but indeed the currents of all miracles derive down to us, and the most divine rivers emanate. Therefore, as we first prostrate ourselves upon the ground, this hymn must be brought forth in the Ionic rhythm and with the sacred accent required for stirring the soul:

->**Generator and architect of all things. King of the heavens, light of the spirit, hope of men. A terror to the shadows of dark Phlegethon <note>A river in the underworld</note>. The unbelievable love of the heaven-dwellers. The invincible fear of the dwellers of Tartarus. The celebrated religion of those born of the earth. Lord, Our Lord, Our God <note>original: "Adonai Adonenu Elohenu"</note>. King, Almighty, First-born <note>original: "Basileus pantocrator protogenethlos"</note>. One God, the same God, nourishing God. Coming from above, glide into us.**<-

When this hymn had been spoken according to custom, Capnion <note>The scholarly nickname for Johannes Reuchlin</note> began again: Every miracle of man whose substance is found to be true and not imaginary—whether it be great, middling, or small—must always, if the prescribed order of sacred rites is observed, be referred back to the glorious God, whose name be blessed forever. For He alone is the one who—either by Himself, or through a delegate (not without Himself), or through a substitute acting from Him—performs such things as we marvel at, the adequate cause of which we cannot know, either why they happen or why they happen in this manner. This precept stands forth, drawn from the armory of sacred things. For thus the divine hymn contains: *Blessed be the Lord God of Israel, who alone does wondrous things.* If He alone, then not men; if He alone, then not demons; if He alone, then not angels. Therefore, God alone does them, either by Himself or through His creatures. For it is a settled point of law that what anyone can do through himself, he can do through another in his own name. And Aristophanes says immediately: "Jupiter rules through some of the gods," not because the lesser gods rule in the place of the Supreme God. For if that were so, God would be absent and they would fill His place. The philosopher—that Virgilian shepherd who sang among the flocks, "All things are full of Jove"—will not permit us to say that; rather, it is that the angels rule in the name of God, governing their offices and executing the gift delegated to them. It is lawful for them to take up commands, for God dwells in them, is carried by them, and walks upon them. It is no different for us mortals, as my friend Paul says: "We are co-workers with God." According to the testimony of Aratus in the *Phaenomena*, we are also His offspring, just like the angels in whom and through whom He acts as He wills. Therefore, it is not we who perform miracles, but God, the Best and Greatest, to whom you should give thanks, "who alone does great wonders, for His mercy endures forever." You have here who the worker is: the God of gods. You have the cause of the operation: because His mercy, piety, beneficence, and grace endure forever—not law, not rigor, not debt, nor any obligation. You have the end of these miraculous operations: because they lead to confession, magnificence, glory, honor, and worship, of which the God of gods and Lord of lords is worthy, to whom you should give thanks because He is good.

Therefore, some people should be ashamed—even those who think they have deserved well of philosophy—who, while professing many other things that walk the path of no small insanity and madness, strive most of all to persuade us that there is a certain art whose rules, by a kind of necessity, bind the wills of the gods, either by the constraints of words or the bonds of written characters; whereas they do nothing unless commanded, and He who commands is not...

<summary>Capnion argues that all true miracles proceed solely from God, often acting through angelic or human intermediaries, rather than through the "compulsory art" of magic or symbols. The text includes a syncretic hymn addressing God through Latin, Greek, and Hebrew titles.</summary>
<keywords>miracles, divine names, Capnion, angels, grace vs. law, Aristophanes, Virgil, St. Paul, Neoplatonism, Kabbalah</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue from Book One, transitioning into the core philosophical debate of Book Two regarding the nature of miracles and the "Wonder-Working Name."</meta>

# SECOND BOOK

-> i <-

does not obey the king but is accustomed to rule, for since he is the highest, he is by no means subject to inferior fabrications. Furthermore, there can be a doctrine of prestidigitation <note>original: "praestygiorum"; referring here to sleight-of-hand or illusions</note>, but of miracles truly there is none, unless it be a serving union with divinity. In this state, it is not we ourselves who act through God, but God who acts through us, gratifying the wishes of his friends to effect something that others might admire. Therefore, it was not with impunity that Moses in the desert of Zin and his brother <insert>Aaron</insert> <note>The text refers to his "German brother," likely meaning his close kinsman or brother-german, Aaron</note> persuaded the people that they could produce water from the hardest rock; nor did they attribute this to the Author, but rather presumed it for themselves to the extent that the fame of their own names might grow among the common people. This was indeed most foolish, for while they were careful lest their own gravity <note>authority or dignity</note> be diminished toward their subjects, they allowed the majesty of God to be harmed. For this reason, they were forbidden to enter the land flowing with milk and honey, so that younger men might learn that the operation of miracles ought to be referred to the one supreme and greatest God, who alone performs great wonders. Not unto us, surely, not unto us, but to the name of God must this glory be given. 

Therefore, as Sidonius says, neither do we perform miracles, nor can there be any artifice by which we might learn to perform them. For this reason, we have waited in vain, and only at long last, for you to speak to us about the wonder-working name. In vain has this desire for knowledge led me through an almost sleepless night—a matter for which, as you preach, there is no art, and of which we ourselves would not even be capable if such an art existed. But by Pollux! <note>original: "edepol"; a common classical oath</note> even this will seem a wonder of wonders to me, Capnion <note>The Greek-style nickname for Johann Reuchlin, meaning "smoke," a pun on his German name</note>, that you now deny what you previously so excellently affirmed. You said, if I remember correctly, that we ourselves are the effectors of miraculous works; now you tell another story, that neither the gods nor we ourselves act, but God alone. 

What is this great inconstancy? One moment you say yes, the next you deny it; although we are able to fight most fiercely against you using the deeds performed in the age of our ancestors and to conquer in battle. For let us set aside the poetic tales of the little woman who promises to release minds with her charms, to stop the waters in rivers, and to turn the stars backward; or the magical feats among the thousands in Lucan <note>The Roman poet Lucan, author of Pharsalia</note>: "That love, not sufficiently drawn, which flowed into hard hearts by the song of the Thessalian witches"; or of Pirithous, who is said to have been the first to draw the moon down from heaven; or the Homeric tales of Circe’s omnipotence, or the Maronic <note>referring to Publius Vergilius Maro, i.e., Virgil</note> tales of Proteus’s cunning. 

I prefer to contend with you using histories, which, although they are quite innumerable, you nevertheless dare not deny when we recount a few. For instance, that Tarquinius Priscus, according to the opinion of the augur Attius Navius (as found in Livy), cut a whetstone with a razor. Or that Pythagoras was in both Thurii and Metapontum at one and the same moment. Or that Apollonius was suddenly transported from Smyrna to Ephesus with a spoken word—he who is also said to have raised a girl dying on her wedding day in Rome by secretly pronounced words, as that noble writer Philostratus testifies. 

By this example and in that hope, we placed our trust in you, expecting to be instructed in a similar doctrine so that, having omitted the anxieties of the magi, the observations of the astrologers, and the scrutinies of the natural philosophers, we might be able to effect sudden things worthy of admiration and report by our mouth alone, which would prove that true philosophy exists within us. For what does it profit to learn many wonders daily if one never performs any? There is a great opinion of us among the common people regarding our philosophy and knowledge of all things. For thus they describe it: namely, as the knowledge of things divine and human. But I ask you, best of friends, what distinguished and illustrious satisfaction shall we provide to the people for our studies, while of this sort—

<meta>This page continues the dialogue from the previous section of "On the Miracle-Working Word," focusing on the necessity of performing wonders to prove philosophical or religious authority.</meta>

# ON THE MIRACLE-WORKING WORD

...works are lacking. It is a small thing for us to know letters, which even the lowliest people have learned. There is nothing elevating about being eloquent, a quality nature has granted even to simple women. Nor is there anything in our claim to either entangle or disentangle so many and such complex scholastic questions. For this reason, the common people judge us to be fools and insane rather than wise—so much so, certainly, that in our age the very name of "philosophy" has fallen into reproach. Even the rites of the heathens invite one to live religiously, and their customs compel it; therefore, the difference between us and the unlearned masses will be very slight unless wonderful works follow our admirable profession in equal measure.

Moved by this, Thespion, that prince of the Gymnosophists <note>A sect of ancient Indian philosophers known for their asceticism and "naked wisdom."</note>, showed an elm tree—the third tree in the row from them—to the philosopher Apollonius of Tyana <note>A famous Neopythagorean philosopher and miracle-worker of the 1st century AD.</note>, who had come from afar, lured by the fame of the Egyptian school to explore their discipline. Sitting among the disciples of wisdom in the garden for the sake of disputation, Thespion said: "O tree, speak to the wise Apollonius, so that he may recognize that it is not impossible for wisdom to be possessed by us as well." And so, according to the commands, the wood spoke to him in an articulate and feminine voice. They did not think themselves proven philosophers unless they displayed a prodigy that exceeded every sense of human judgment.

But because you attribute this to God alone and deny it to men, I do not know in what way you strive to diminish your own people, who are nevertheless celebrated with the highest praise for performing miracles. I remain silent regarding the Apostles, whose power in all the affairs of the entire world is said to have been immense. But what of Hermione, one of the four daughters of Philip—the man who instructed the treasurer of Queen Candace? <note>Referring to Philip the Evangelist from the Book of Acts.</note> While Trajan watched, she was beaten severely with rods but could not be harmed; after that Emperor died, she was flogged again by the order of Hadrian, yet she sang hymns. Then, placed in a cauldron heated by a great fire and filled with lead and boiling sulfur, she looked up to heaven, made the sign <note>The Sign of the Cross.</note>, and quicker than the word was spoken, the fire was extinguished and the lead leaped out. In the presence of everyone, she said, "O King, I call the God of heaven to witness that I feel no more heat than you yourself feel." For she appeared to everyone to be moving among dew and flowers. Again, when the cauldron was lit, she remained unharmed just as before.

I could cite the annals and deeds of many, and weave a longer history concerning a certain Peter in Atroa, but I have decided to restrain myself lest I seem to "teach Minerva," as they say <note>original: "sus Minervam" — a Latin proverb meaning a pig trying to teach the goddess of wisdom; i.e., a fool teaching an expert.</note>. Yet regarding this man, I will recall even this: that he is written to have crossed the river Halys with dry feet.

Then Baruchias said: "If you understand Capnion <note>The Hebrew-style nickname for Johannes Reuchlin, the author.</note>, Sidonius, as you most certainly ought to with an attentive and diligent mind, then unless I am mistaken, you have spoken much to us in vain. For he set forth the right path for both of us, though you have taken his words the wrong way. Remember what he introduced yesterday, immediately after discussing the inestimable conjunction, society, and union of the highest Divinity with human weakness: that we are able, as proof of such divine conversation, to perform wonderful prodigies with a single word. I shall judge it thus: as the common people are accustomed to attribute operations to those things from which we daily see them emanate with our raw sight. Such an example exists in the Odes of Horace: *'and the machines haul the dry hulls.'* <note>original: "trahuntque siccas machinae carinas" — Horace, Odes 1.4.</note> For if you penetrate the force of the speech and do not merely notice the custom of speaking, you will find no machine that hauls ships, but rather we..."

<summary>The speaker argues that true philosophy must be proven through miracles, citing examples from ancient wonder-workers like Apollonius and Christian martyrs like Hermione. Baruchias then redirects the conversation toward the power of the "miraculous word" discussed the previous day.</summary>
<keywords>Thespion, Apollonius of Tyana, Hermione, Trajan, Hadrian, Peter of Atroa, Capnion, Baruchias, Horace, miracles, Gymnosophists, divine union</keywords>

--- Page 49 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between the three characters: Sidonius (the Epicurean), Baruchias (the Hebrew scholar), and Capnion (the persona of Reuchlin himself). The discussion is shifting from the mechanics of human language and tools to the divine power of the "Mirific Word."</meta>

# ON THE MIRIFIC WORD
<meta>catchword: such</meta>

We ourselves ingeniously construct certain machines for our own use, by which we easily launch such a great mass into the sea. Thus it happens that we more correctly say *we* pull the hull with machines. But it fits the common manner of speaking better to say, as the poet does: *The machines haul the dry hulls.* <note>A reference to Horace, Odes 1.4.2: "trahuntque siccas machinae carinas."</note> So too we would be forced to accuse Homer of a similar fault when writing such things about Phereclus: both that he was the most skilled craftsman of all, and that he built for Alexander <note>Paris of Troy</note> the ships that were the authors of all evils, which were the destruction of all the Trojans and of Paris himself. Why should we blame the ships of Phereclus, which could do no harm without a pilot? Therefore Alexander caused the loss, not the ships. Here, Sidonius, you might rebuke the wisest and greatest of poets <note>Homer</note> for saying the ships were a harm to the Phrygians—those in which Helen was carried away to Troy—rather than the son of Priam, because as an adulterer he committed the abduction, or Ilium itself because it received Helen. But usage, as in many other things, ought to prevail in this case even among us; though we speak of divine matters in human speech, we say that men full of the spirit of God perform miracles, even though God himself performs them principally through the man. 

Indeed, I would have believed you were not of such dull taste that you did not perceive these things far better than I, and yet you wished to complain about the slowness of Capnion <note>The Greek-style nickname for Johann Reuchlin, meaning "smoke," a pun on his German name.</note>, who keeps us suspended with such a great desire for the "mirific" <note>mirifici: wonder-working or miraculous</note> name—perhaps to wear us out with delay to make us more eager, which as far as I am concerned is impossible. For there is nothing anywhere that I desire more. Or perhaps by this delay he thinks his neck will be snatched from the halter and thus he might escape his promises. But I think he attempts this the less because he values integrity so much that he would consider it a cause for blushing with incredible shame if he held his faith loosely. 

But there is a class of men who gladly bury their talent <note>A biblical reference to the Parable of the Talents.</note> and hide their knowledge, preferring to seem more ignorant so they do not have to teach, rather than producing rivals by teaching. I do not know if he is pretending to be of their number. For my part, in the fellowship of letters, I would wish every individual were like me and so profuse in publishing those things which we carry in our minds and which we accumulate for ourselves through erudition. For if by chance, deceived by self-love, I think I know something, I immediately seek a listener, whom I both pray and sometimes—only with difficulty—finally persuade to learn from me; so much am I delighted by the communication and liberality of studies. 

"Let us then put it to the test in Baruchias," Sidonius replied to Capnion, "to see whether he speaks the truth or whether he has decided to invent this discourse with himself so that he might more easily force me into a corner. Come, speak of the words which your fellow countrymen <note>The Jews</note> keep in secret, and hide almost 'under a bushel' as you were saying. For you were boasting that you had read them over many times." 

"Now I, with serious zeal, agree with you that the Hebrews have contributed not a little to our faculty; we even imitate them often more correctly than other nations. It is well known from the memory of our fathers that their religion possessed the greatest divinity and worshipped God rightly." 

And Baruchias said, "I shall do it without being forced, lest by fleeing I seem to seek glory by allowing myself to be asked by such great men, or lest I seem to say one thing and do another. For there are few things concerning the sacred names in which I have been instructed by the school of the Hebrews, and therefore I shall explain them in few words. But bar the door, lest any of the servants overhear, and lest sacred things become a laughingstock to the profane, and 'pearls be foully trampled by swine.' <note>A reference to Matthew 7:6.</note> I will also deliberately attempt to speak more obscurely, provided that you yourselves, by the goodness of your intellect, understand what I intend. The fellowship of divinity with the human mind causes God to use man as an instrument, not otherwise than the Peripatetics <note>Followers of Aristotle</note> say regarding the activity of the sensitive and vegetative [soul]..."

<summary>The dialogue transitions from a philosophical debate on agency and tools to a request for Baruchias to reveal the secret Hebrew names of God. Baruchias agrees to share his knowledge of the "Mirific Word," warning that such sacred mysteries must be kept from the "profane."</summary>
<keywords>agency, Homer, Phereclus, Capnion (Reuchlin), Baruchias, Hebrew secrets, sacred names, pearls before swine, divine instrument, Peripatetics</keywords>

--- Page 50 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical discussion from Book One regarding the relationship between the soul, the body, and the divine. The author uses the biological process of digestion as an analogy for spiritual transformation.</meta>

# BOOK TWO

Such things are taught by nature. For wherever the **vegetative** <note>The basic life force responsible for growth and nutrition</note> exists within the **sensitive** <note>The higher faculty of the soul responsible for sensation and movement</note>, the vegetative acts as an instrument, while the sensitive acts as the principal mover. When this mover begins to act, it does not move toward something inferior to itself, but through means suitable to both, it moves as much as possible toward its equal and like-minded counterpart, and toward that with which it can dwell. 

For the nourishing and nursing power in animals digests and transforms raw food by means of a moderate heat; yet this heat is merely the messenger of a certain **hidden and digestive property** <term>occulta proprietas: a hidden quality that cannot be explained by the four elements (hot, cold, moist, dry)</term>. For heat alone does not transform matter; otherwise, you would cook nutrients faster on a hearth than in the stomach, yet we clearly see the opposite. Rather, that heat intervenes like a herald, acting as a sort of character and image of a natural pact for digestion. Therefore, the food does not remain as it was, but migrates into another substance. The nourishing virtue does not transform it into grass or a plant—for that would make it more like a mere vegetable—but rather, it industriously converts it into flesh, which is most fit for the sense of touch, so that the sensitive mover itself may dwell within it through that touch.

In this same way, God, the most excellent of all, who (according to the testimony of **Hermes Trismegistus** <note>original: "Mercurio ter maximo"; a legendary Hellenistic figure associated with alchemy and philosophy</note>) fashioned two images after his own likeness—the **World** and **Man**—so that he might "play in the orb of the earth," as it says in the Parables, and "find his delights among the sons of men." 

In the World, He plays through wondrous operations, not only by stellar or elementary power, but also sometimes by a **hidden property**. Just as physicians prove regarding the wearing of Coral or other objects: it is not because this or that is "hot" or "cold," but because of a specific quality. As we said regarding animal nutrition, the transformation of digested things occurs not primarily through heat, but through such a hidden quality. And just as natural heat in the digestion of food is like a shadow holding the place of a hidden property, so too in the effects arising from the secret disposition of things—where one individual differs from another—it is not "Nature" itself as the common folk think, but something more obscure that precedes Nature that is the cause. The very word "Nature" in common speech carries the likeness and type of this cause.

You have now heard concerning the World. Let us likewise look at **Man**, who is called the **Microcosm** <note>original: "Microcosmus"; the "little world" or miniature universe</note> by the Greeks. God delights in his company even more than in the world’s, and He strives to transform man into Himself (as far as human capacity allows), just as sensitive nutrition transforms food. He does this toward us through the **heat of love** which we conceive, but toward Himself through a **hidden property** which we do not know—whether you call it a covenant, or an ineffable attraction, or by any other name. 

Furthermore, although God, being alone omnipotent, could do all these things without intermediate causes and with fewer observed boundaries of action, He nevertheless willed many participants of His goodness to concur. Some have called these "secondary causes," others "virtues," and some "gods," "angels," or "good spirits." Whatever mediates these effects holds an affinity to the Mover and, at the same time, is not at an enormous distance from the thing moved. Therefore, the Lord of Eternity, rejoicing in our society, holds the holy mind of man among His delights, as it is neighbor to His own nature and origin. When He sees that mind obedient with diligent worship and perpetual religion, He—like a most ample nourishing virtue—always endeavors (as much as human weakness permits) to transform it into Himself, digesting it not only by the heat of love but by a hidden property, so that **man may migrate into God and God may dwell in man.** But truly, just as that property by which transformation...

<meta>catchword: ...tation (transmutation)</meta>

<summary>The author argues that just as the body transforms food into flesh through a "hidden property" rather than simple heat, God transforms the human soul into His own likeness through the "heat of love" and divine grace.</summary>
<keywords>Microcosm, Occult Properties, Digestion, Hermes Trismegistus, Divine Love, Transformation, Secondary Causes, Natural Philosophy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical dialogue regarding the "Wonder-Working Word." The speaker is arguing for the superiority of ancient, "barbaric" (non-Greek/Latin) languages—specifically Hebrew—as the direct channel for divine communication.</meta>

# ON THE WONDER-WORKING WORD

<note>original: "DE VERBO MIRIFICO"</note>

...as we pass into God and exceed our human nature, he is secret and hidden to us; thus, by the highest right, God has also given to himself hidden and secret names. He has instilled in them certain covenants which, if observed, ensure that he himself will soon be present at their proper pronunciation according to our prayer. Furthermore, just as in natural digestion there is a power of heat which we recognize, and yet a hidden power of transformation <note>literally "transcorporation"</note> which we do not understand, so in this most divine passage into God there are certain words which we know, and certain ones we do not know. This bond of words is fitting for both parties: for God is spirit, the word is breath, and man is the one breathing. God is called *Logos* <note>Greek: "Word" or "Reason"</note>. The Word is named by that same title, and human reason is expressed by a similar term. God is conceived by our mind and brought to birth by the word; wherefore, just as he chose the imperceptible seat of the mind for himself, so he chose a perceptible dwelling place for words—not all words, nor those falling by chance, but those which the Divinity first established, not those which human invention devised. For God knows the thoughts of men, that they are vain. But the thought of God is preserving and health-giving, remaining, invincible, and certain; what he thinks shall be so, and what he treats in his mind shall thus come to pass. The Lord of the universe has decreed it, and who shall be able to weaken it?

Therefore, not all words possess divine power—if you have listened attentively to me speaking—but only those which the highest God of the world and of men, or the perceptive angels by the command and providence of God, have composed in a certain way. These words resonate with the harmony of eternity, they are tenacious of divinity, and they breathe with the joint-work of a beneficial dispensation. They are, as it were, the perceptible monuments of an imperceptible deification, which, relying on the immovable stability of letters and the everlasting firmness of the vocal members, allow at once both an unshaken alternation of modulation and an agility of pronunciation that is not sought after or feigned, but native, simple, and venerable. By these intermediaries, God strikes a covenant with man—not with just any man, but with one who shines with holiness, piety, religion, and most especially with the confidence of belief.

Therefore, if a certain decree of speaking ought to reconcile human mortality with the power of God, it is fitting that those most sacred names—or words, if you prefer—be primary, right, and ancient. This is so even though you sometimes call them "barbaric" on account of their uncultivated (that is, simple) antiquity. For that which is ancient approaches the age <note>Latin: *aevum*, meaning a span of time or an epoch</note>, the age is near to eternity, and eternity is close to God, who reigns above eternity. Hence he is called the "Ancient of Days" by the early theologians, because he presides over the kingdom of all ages. Therefore, "barbaric" things are more closely related to divinity. For this reason, not without cause, Zoroaster—the first theologian of the gentiles—forbids "barbaric words" to be changed. By "barbaric," they mean Hebrew or those derived most closely from it. For the little flowers of speech and the charm of elegancies were devised after Heber <note>The biblical ancestor of the Hebrews</note> and the distinction of languages by men who were curious rather than sincere. But the simple speech—pure, uncorrupted, holy, brief, and constant—belongs to the Hebrews. By this speech, God is said to have spoken with man, and men with angels, openly and not through an interpreter; face to face, not through the stream of Castalia, nor the cave of Trophonius, nor the tree of Dodona, nor the Tripod of the Delphians <note>References to famous Greek oracles and sites of prophecy</note>, but just as a friend is accustomed to speak with a friend. If you think this is said by me rather for the praise of my own people than because I feel it to be so, compare the languages, and you will find none purer, none more modest. As for antiquity and order, if you trust our historians less, then look to your own...

<summary>The text argues that Hebrew is the original, "barbaric" language of God, possessing a unique divine power and simplicity that allows for direct communication with the Creator, unlike the complex oracles of the Greeks.</summary>
<keywords>Hebrew language, divine names, Zoroaster, Logos, Ancient of Days, oracles, sacred linguistics, Reuchlin</keywords>

--- Page 52 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument regarding the antiquity of sacred wisdom, specifically focusing on the chronological priority of Moses over Greek authors.</meta>

->BOOK TWO<-

Learn from your own [writers] that no one among all authors is found to be more ancient than Moses. From his time until the fall of Troy <note>The legendary Trojan War, a primary chronological marker for ancient historians.</note> three hundred and ten years are counted. At that time, Abdon <note>original: "Abeſon"; a judge of Israel</note> presided over our people, Agamemnon over the Argives, Vafer over the Egyptians, and Teutamus over the Assyrians. Meanwhile, let someone produce for me another volume committed to the memory of letters before the destruction of Troy, besides the books of the Hebrews. For after one hundred and fifty years, finally the primary Greek poets, Homer and Hesiod, followed the Trojan War according to the testimony of Cicero; slightly before them was Orpheus with his sacred rites. But these are nothing compared to our author, who brought forth written laws a very long time before.

In former centuries, Eupolemus, a Greek historian of the kings of Judea, left word in the most noble monuments of his works that Moses was exceptionally wise and handed down the knowledge of grammar and letters to the Jews, who afterwards bestowed them upon the Phoenicians on account of their proximity. Then the Phoenicians distributed the system of characters to the children of the Greeks because of their mutual commerce. I believe Pliny was moved by this when he said he always considered the primordial letters to have been Assyrian, although others wish them to have been discovered among the Egyptians, and others among the Syrians; nevertheless, he says Cadmus brought sixteen in number into Greece from Phoenicia. The entire region being bordering on one another creates this variety [of opinions].

And perhaps this is also why certain writers, not being very diligent, called Moses an Egyptian priest, such as Strabo in the sixteenth book of his *Cosmography*. Whatever that may be, Moses, whose writing has reached even to us, stands out as more ancient; before him, no monuments of letters are read. Many of the Greeks confess this antiquity, though one in one way and another in another. Our own Philo, and our—or rather your—Fabius Josephus <note>Flavius Josephus, the Jewish historian whose works were highly valued by Christian scholars.</note>, while noting "Antiquity" in his title, writes nothing foreign to Moses. Likewise Polemon, in the first book of his Greek histories, [places] him as a leader in the times of Ogygus and Inachus. Apion, that learned man and [disciple] of Poseidonius, says he was the leader of the fugitives while Amosis was reigning in Egypt, both in his book against the Jews and also in the fourth of his histories. And Ptolemy of Mendes mentions him in his histories of the Egyptians.

Finally, those who are held as famous in recording the affairs of the Athenians—Hellanicus, Philochorus, Castor, Thallus, and Alexander surnamed Polyhistor—and the easily first of all historians, Diodorus, who himself diligently traversed almost the whole of Asia and Europe with his own eyes: all these have proclaimed the most ample signs of antiquity concerning that wonderful leader and most excellent author of divine matters. Through which it becomes manifest that he stands before all others both in age of time and antiquity of religion, and in the admirable power of secret operation and the discipline of divinity.

For you would seek in vain from the Greeks for words descending from heaven and names contained by the divine favor of syllables, because of their novice art of speaking and very recent variety of language. For the Greeks, as Iamblichus of Chalcis, the disciple of Porphyry, says, are by nature studious of new things, and are carried headlong everywhere like a ship lacking ballast, having no stability. They do not preserve what they have received from others, but quickly let even this go, and are accustomed to transform everything because of their instability and the expression of new invention. And therefore a certain Egyptian priest is rightly praised for having responded to Solon in Plato’s *Timaeus* when he said: "O Solon, Solon, you Greeks are always children, nor is there anyone from Greece who is an old man, because your soul is always young, in which there is no ancient [knowledge] from the commemoration of antiquity..."

<summary>The author argues for the historical primacy of Moses, asserting that he predates the oldest Greek poets and historians and was the original source of written language and divine wisdom.</summary>
<keywords>Moses, antiquity of letters, Greek historians, Phoenicians, Josephus, Diodorus Siculus, Plato, Solon, chronology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discourse on the origins of sacred wisdom, specifically addressing the superiority of Hebrew antiquity over Greek and Egyptian traditions.</meta>

# ON THE WONDER-WORKING WORD

opinion, nor any discipline gray with age. By this chance, that famous Ptolemy Philadelphus <note>Ptolemy II Philadelphus (309–246 BCE), known for his patronage of the Library of Alexandria.</note> was led to collect books of antiquity; being a man of the sharpest zeal, when he had filled the Library of Alexandria with nearly seven hundred thousand volumes, and yet lacked Moses—the pillar of antiquity and the first monument of the past—it is said he then sent royal ambassadors to Jerusalem. He prayed that six men from each individual tribe, most learned in Greek and most fit for translating the Pentateuch <note>The first five books of the Bible.</note>—or rather that volume which we call by the composite name "The Twenty-Four" <note>The Tanakh or Hebrew Bible, traditionally counted as 24 books.</note>—should migrate with them to Alexandria. Upon their arrival, the king built seventy-two small cells for them, not in the city itself, but at Pharos nearly seven stadia away, lest they be hindered by the tumult. Jerome, writing to Desiderius, would not have thought these cells a fable if he had more accurately weighed the advisory of Justin Martyr, who asserted that he himself had seen the remains of those cells still standing. Therefore, the Scripture of the Hebrews was translated into the Greek tongue for the Egyptian king, lest among so many volumes he should lack the first elements of this world, or lest he be forced at some time to admit (as Solon was by the compelling truth) that neither he nor any other Greek possessed any knowledge of antiquity. This warns us with the highest reason that for secret operations, no ancient names related to divine matters are to be sought from the Greeks, nor even from the discipline of the Egyptians, though they think it older. For as Socrates philosophizes with Hermogenes in Plato’s *Cratylus*: "Certainly all divine names were delivered either by the gods first, or by an antiquity whose beginning is not easily known, or by Barbarians." <note>original: "Omnia certe diuina nomina uel a diis primū uel ab antiquitate... uel a Barbaris prodita sunt." In this context, "Barbarians" refers to non-Greeks, specifically the Hebrews.</note> But indeed, the Egyptians fashioned their own gods with their own hands, by the testimony of their father Hermes Trismegistus; they erred concerning the nature of divinity and failed to observe the proper worship and religion of God. What divine thing, then, will you seek in a human fiction? What sacred things can you hope for from your own inventions, or what celestial aid from the works of your own hands? In what way will you learn the seals of the most excellent and super-celestial presence, or the pathways of the highest powers, from demons whom the ancestors of the Egyptians—sinning against divinity and wandering—created? For the other heavenly gods, whose maker is eternal, dwell in the highest places according to Egyptian philosophy (and are thus called "celestials"), and they do not mingle with our human allurements. Therefore, those others with whom their daily conversation in the temples took place, and who were content with human proximity, are not gods but demons, made by ancestors against the pleasure of God, born after the birth of men, and either so foolish that they do not speak but merely nod, or so shrewd that they flatter their Egyptian makers in all things, saying whatever might soothe their ears. Whence you can draw nothing stable, nothing firm, nothing constant, nothing pleasing to God, to whom even the generation of those beings was displeasing. You can understand these arguments from the more excellent philosophers of that region. But neither did the sacred names of divinity flow to this nation from primeval antiquity; although they boast of many years in Plato, when they claim their deeds are contained in writings of nine thousand years, I consider this entire number—as far as I am persuaded to believe concerning the beginning of the world—to be full of fables.

<summary>The author argues that true divine names and ancient wisdom originate with the Hebrews rather than the Greeks or Egyptians, citing the miraculous translation of the Septuagint and the fallibility of Egyptian "man-made" deities.</summary>
<keywords>Ptolemy Philadelphus, Library of Alexandria, Septuagint, Moses, Pentateuch, Justin Martyr, Hermes Trismegistus, Cratylus, divine names, demons, antiquity</keywords>

--- Page 54 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's argument regarding the antiquity of languages and the origins of sacred names, specifically comparing the claims of the Egyptians against those of the Hebrews and Babylonians.</meta>

# BOOK TWO

I judge it so. But if they do not think these things are false, I demand from them that they also freely admit what Marcus Tullius Cicero <note>The famous Roman orator and philosopher (106–43 BCE).</note> records concerning Babylon and the **Chaldæans**, who possess records spanning four hundred and seventy thousand years—as they themselves say—a number far greater than that of the **Egyptians**. Moreover, although they are also called **Assyrians**, it is established by the most ancient men of letters that the Babylonians were the same people once called **Syrians**, who made Babylon and Nineveh the capitals of their kingdom. The illustrious author **Strabo** and many other noble writers, seeking with great zeal to investigate antiquity, have handed this down to human posterity. 

Truly, the most noble territory of Syria is the region of **Damascus**, neighboring the Persians, from which the race of the **Jews** took its origin, according to the testimony of **Trogus** and **Justin**, and likewise several other historians not to be despised. Therefore, the Jews—who were also Syrians and Assyrians—would possess monuments of their language much more ancient than the Egyptians, even if we believe foreign authors. Thus it would follow that the Jews surpass them both in time and in their God. From this, the names of the **Hebrews** would be more sacred than those of the Egyptians, since they are both older and were handed down under the worship of the one and first God. 

I should also add this: because of their lack of refinement in ancient times, the Egyptians did not yet use invented alphabetic characters, but wrote using images of things themselves <note>A reference to hieroglyphs, which the author views as a more primitive stage of writing compared to the Hebrew alphabet.</note>. Indeed, they have observed this form of writing until now in their so-called "sacred rites," alongside more recently devised elements. It remains that if the Egyptian names were divine and truly sacred—useful in secret miracles—they were neither given by the gods (whom we have shown to be *demons* <note>original: "demones"</note> and inferior to men, according to the most excellent Egyptian authority, **Hermes** <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Hellenistic figure associated with the invention of writing and magic.</note>), nor handed down from the antiquity of their writings, in which those names ought to be contained, but which the books of the Hebrews surpass by far in every way. 

Let us now at least test this further: whether these names arose from themselves as if from "barbaric" men, which was the third part of the Socratic discourse. **Herodotus**, the father of history, teaches us in his second book that the Egyptians refuse to be called **Barbarians**, but rather apply that term to all other nations. But because they so abhor and disdain this word as if it were a disgrace to their region, they were therefore called "Barbarians" by their enemies, the Hebrews. It is just as when we sometimes say to a prostitute or a harlot in mockery, "O noble lady!" 

There were at that same time three noble courtesans in Rome: Cytheris, Origo, and Arbuscula. In this same sense, **Sallust** says: "He had an old habit of debauchery with Fulvia, a noble woman." You see that what is owed to virtue is instead cast as a reproach against obscene people. For a little later, the same Crispus Sallust asserts that much of the nobility seethed with envy against **Cicero**, a "new man" <note>term: "novus homo" (a man who was the first in his family to serve in the Roman Senate or be elected consul)</note>, by whom they believed the consulship was being polluted; in that case, the word "nobility" was used more correctly. 

Thus I understand that song: *When Israel went out of Egypt, and the house of Jacob from a barbaric people, Judah became his sanctuary.* <note>A translation of Psalm 114:1-2.</note> I judge that the Egyptians were certainly called "Barbarians" in contempt, because they did not wish to be called Barbarians. Yet the word *Loes* <note>original: "Loes"</note> here designates lack of refinement and dullness—the letter *n* being turned into *l*—although *Loes* is everywhere explained as "barbarian." Let the Egyptians therefore blush to be called barbarians and bring a charge of injury. And as for the sacred [rites] of **Zoroaster**...

<summary>The author argues that Hebrew and Babylonian records are far older than Egyptian ones, suggesting that Hebrew sacred names hold greater authority. He also discusses the term "Barbarian," arguing the Hebrews used it as a mocking insult against the Egyptians.</summary>
<keywords>Antiquity of Jews, Babylonian history, Egyptian hieroglyphs, Barbarians, Cicero, Sallust, sacred names, etymology of Loes</keywords>

--- Page 55 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue concerning the mystical power of words, specifically addressing why "barbarous" or foreign terms are held to be more potent in sacred rites than polished Greek or Latin.</meta>

-># ON THE WONDER-WORKING WORD<-

...of the prince <note>original: "principis," likely referring to a philosophical authority like Plato or Hermes Trismegistus</note> as they say, they will accept this decree of the priesthood when it forbids "barbarous words" from being changed during sacred rites. Therefore, the barbarous words which we use immutably and without contamination during sacred acts shall not be Egyptian, but rather Mosaic. For that very Moses confessed himself to be a "barbarian" before God—that is, thick-tongued and heavy of speech—when he said: *My Lord, I am not a man of eloquence, but of a heavy mouth and a heavy tongue.* <note>A reference to Exodus 4:10, where Moses describes his speech impediment.</note> Strabo the cosmographer <note>Strabo (c. 64 BC – 24 AD) was a Greek geographer and historian.</note> recorded in his fourteenth book that such men were called "barbarians" from the beginning; it was a coined name for those who spoke with difficulty, harshly, or roughly, just as we use the terms "lispers" or "stammerers," finding names that sound like the things they describe—such as *murmur*, *clangor*, *din*, *crash*, and other similar sounds. And if one wishes to search all histories with precision, we shall find that all the nations of the East take offense at the name of "barbarians," except for our Moses and his people, who did not deny openly that he was harsh-tongued and a barbarian. He even committed this to memory through his writings. Nor is it fitting for us, who imitate his footsteps and his speech, to deny that we are barbarians.

It is not that our method of speaking is entirely unskilled. For we have a language composed according to rules, and we practice the grammatical art. For young Hebrews, teachers like Moses Kimhi, the Master of the Ephod <note>original: "Magister Ephod," referring to Isaac ben Moses Stanow, known as Profiat Duran, a 14th-century Jewish scholar</note>—which we interpret in the French manner as "I, the perfect Duran"—and Hasra and other excellent schoolmasters, train both the tongue and the pen. Therefore, it is not that we are speechless or rustic, but that we are the first of men, whom all seekers of the most ancient things have asserted were always "barbarians." Not considering this accurately, some of our own people have dared to teach that "Barbarians" are simply those who do not speak the Holy Tongue <note>original: "lingua sancta," referring to Hebrew</note>, such as Solomon the scholar in his exposition of the hymn we mentioned regarding the interpretation of the word *Loes* <note>original: "Loes," likely referring to the Hebrew 'Lo'ez', meaning a foreign language or people</note>, and certain other beginners. But it happens to our people as it does to other writers: *Learned and unlearned alike, we write poems everywhere.*

Indeed, I would readily believe that the more illustrious priests of Egypt mixed into their own rites the names of the Hebrews, which they might use more effectively when they wished to bring some secret thing into the light. They learned these only through long and hidden study, just as you yourselves are able to learn. Unless these words had once been foreign to their native tongue, they could not have remained as hidden as we see in the histories. For although Plato and Eudoxus lived with them at Heliopolis for thirteen years, they were only just barely able to pluck a few small things from their secret words through long and constant conversation, which they might scatter throughout their own monuments.

Regarding the philosophers of the Indians, whom they call Brahmans <note>original: "Brachmanas"</note>, there is no need for much dispute. For the fact that they first took their beginnings from the Greeks is proven to us by infinite other things, as well as the many statues there which are both Greek and worshipped according to Greek ritual—such as those of Minerva, the Delian Apollo, Dionysus of Lemnos and Amyclae, and others which Philostratus diligently mentions in his *Life of Apollonius of Tyana*. The Indians also agree with the Greeks in their opinions and teachings. For Iarchas used to tell Apollonius a sentiment regarding the human soul, saying it was the same for them as Pythagoras had handed down to the Greeks and to the Egyptians themselves—as if the Brahmans were the teachers of the Egyptians. However, this indicates not so much the order of antiquity as a renewal of opinions, especially since the same Iarchas relates that his grandfather lived at a time when there was no great philosopher in India...

<meta>catchword: philosopher</meta>

<summary>The author argues that the "barbarous words" used in magic and liturgy are actually Hebrew in origin, stemming from Moses's own self-description as a "barbarian" or heavy-tongued speaker. He asserts that even the Egyptians and Greeks borrowed their most potent mystical secrets from the ancient Hebrew tradition.</summary>
