<meta>This page continues the philosophical dialogue from Book One, moving into Book Two. The speaker is discussing the superiority of the Hebrew language in mystical operations compared to other ancient traditions.</meta>

# SECOND BOOK
->e ii<-

philosopher, nor did any noble intellect sprout there. But among the Druids of the Gauls, one does not need to search through many generations of men, for they all philosophized in Greek <note>original: "gręce philosophati sunt"</note>, and for that reason they are known to have come later than the barbarians. With far too many words, most learned and kind men, I have dared to beat upon your ears out of my singular vice of talkativeness, while I attempt to some extent to affirm that the most ancient words of the barbarians must be selected for performing miraculous works—not those of the Phoenicians, nor the Greeks, nor the Egyptians, nor the Indians, nor the Druids. I would add: nor the Persians, were the vicinity of Damascus and Persia not so contiguous. But I speak only of the Jewish race and of that ancient Eber <note>The biblical ancestor from whom the term "Hebrew" is derived</note>, whose speech in the dispersion of languages reached all the way to Moses and after him to us, pure and holy. It equally reflects the first institution of speaking from the beginning of the world, which we necessarily judge the first-born humans received not from man, but from God alone—especially in the divine names, the positioning of which is above man. Regarding this, let us hear Socrates (so that I may use your own authors in this discussion rather than mine) speaking thus in Plato: "By Jove, Hermogenes, if we had any sense, we would establish with ourselves a primary rule of correctness: to confess that we know nothing of the gods, neither of them themselves nor of the names by which they call themselves. For it is certain that they call themselves by their true names." This is in Plato.

"Certainly you do not seem to me to speak obscurely," said Sidonius, "and you have threatened us with more than Baruchia <note>A character in the dialogue, likely representing a Jewish perspective</note>. For almost everything fits our philosophy: both that God joins himself to the human mind and rejoices in us when we have been transformed into him according to our capacity, and that he sometimes works through us just as a craftsman is accustomed to work through instruments, and sometimes, with no intervening causes, he does certain things suddenly. For he can do both; and if he acts through us with some medium concurring, it is fitting that this medium be suitable to both God and man. And you think that our words are such, as they reach toward God through the enclosed spirit, and toward man through the sensible air. This, it seems to me, that famous Epicurus (following Democritus) designated by embracing it in two words—not that I wish to follow those whom I have renounced, but because what is imbibed from tender years often offers itself to the memory against our will, and nonetheless, the harmony of opinions is a vestige of truth. For they say our voice is a *Flow of Reason* <note>original: "Reuma logon" (Greek: rheuma logon)</note>. For whenever reason drips onto the tongue, the air is broken, and a word is made. Thus reason is the fountain, whose stream will be the word; it meets with God in the fountain, and it meets with man in the stream and river. And just as there is nothing in the stream that was not first in the fountain, so according to the Stagirite Aristotle, there is nothing in the voice that was not first in the conception of the soul, just as letters are the signs of the voice. Concerning which Ammonius the Peripatetic wrote thus (forgive me if I do not translate elegantly or worthily for your ears): for he says that letters only announce, while things are only announced. But voices and understandings both announce and are announced. For voices are announced once, but they announce in two ways: namely, first the understandings, and second the things. Conversely, understandings are announced in two ways, but they announce only once. They are announced both by voice and by letters—by the former primarily, and by the latter secondarily. This variety, on the authority of both Aristotle and Ammonius, demonstrates to us the nature of our words, insofar as they communicate partly...

<summary>The text argues that the Hebrew language is the most ancient and divine medium for miraculous works, being the original language given by God. It uses Platonic and Aristotelian linguistic theory to explain how human speech connects the divine mind to the physical world.</summary>
<keywords>Hebrew language, Druids, divine names, Plato, Socrates, Aristotle, Ammonius, philosophy of language, mysticism, logos</keywords>

<meta>This page continues a dialogue concerning the power of sacred names and the "Wonder-Working Word." The speakers are debating whether divine power is restricted to Hebrew or if other ancient languages like Greek or Egyptian hold similar efficacy.</meta>

-># ON THE WONDER-WORKING WORD<-

...partly with spirit and partly with the body, so that I consider a certain use of words to be the most fitting medium for reconciling man with God. But as for your choice of "barbarous" <note>original: "barbara"; in this context, it refers to non-Greek/Latin languages, specifically ancient Near Eastern tongues</note> words as being more closely related to divinity, I do not much object. For now, as Iamblichus <note>A Neoplatonist philosopher who defended theurgy and the use of "meaningless" sacred names</note> says, the gods have approved the speech of the barbarians as being most consistent with sacred rites, especially because that mode of speaking was the most ancient and the first to exist. And since the first people received names from the divine and mixed them with their own language—as if tempering them with a voice familiar and agreeable to the gods—they handed them down to us to be preserved forever, and we must thereafter rightly maintain that very rule of tradition. 

By these precepts, Iamblichus himself leads me toward your opinion, except that you strive to persuade us that those things of which Zoroaster spoke—that "barbarous words should not be changed"—must specifically be Hebrew. And indeed, Baruchias has used such great genius, flow of speech, and so many historical testimonies that you easily move me toward agreement; nothing would now stand in the way of my believing you entirely, were it not for the singular experience of the ancients. They are said to have performed admirable feats of the magi not only with Egyptian and Assyrian words, but also with Greek ones, excelling greatly both in warding off dangers and in hastening benefits. 

For they report that the famous Orpheus—a follower of the Egyptian Mercury <note>Hermes Trismegistus</note>, a leader of the first priesthood, a theologian, and a prophet—while he was one of the Argonauts, turned away a savage power into which they had miserably fallen by means of his sacred rites. He was also the first to demonstrate augury, not only from birds but from other living creatures as well. Yet he used Greek invocations, as his hymns prove, which even now survive and are sung in Greek. Pythagoras and Plato likewise assert that they were taught all their sacred mysteries by the Egyptians, some by the Assyrians and Chaldeans, and others by various peoples; it is certainly incredible what miraculous works they displayed to men. Therefore, sacred names belong not only to the Hebrews, but also to other nations.

Then **BARUCHIAS** said: We sometimes call "sacred" <note>Latin: "sacrum"</note> the same thing as "holy," namely that which is defended and fortified against the injury of men. Sometimes, as Martianus replied in civil law, "sacred things are those which have been consecrated publicly, not privately." Or as the most learned jurist Ulpian wrote, "that is sacred which the Prince <note>The Emperor or sovereign authority</note> has dedicated, or for which he has given the power of dedicating." In this way, whatever we consecrate to God and withdraw from common use is called sacred, holy, and sometimes "sacrosanct," regardless of which nation or people it belongs to. 

Thus that priest in the *Timaeus* <note>A dialogue by Plato</note> once told Solon that the deeds of many men worthy of memory were preserved in the temples of the Egyptians and contained in their sacred writings. So too it is customary to say of the consecrated priests of every nation that they are devoted and hallowed to God, just as Virgil sang of Helenus, the priest of Apollo: "He implores peace from the gods," he says, "and unbinds the fillets of his hallowed head." And later, in the eighth book of the *Aeneid*: "But Caesar, entering the Roman walls in a triple triumph, was consecrating an immortal vow to the Italian gods." 

At other times, and more correctly, we call God—the Author of all things—"Sacred," as the principle and fountain of everything sacred; and thereafter, through participation in Him, all things are sacred in which God Himself dwells of His own accord with His presence and power. This differs from the previous definition: for in the former, we dedicate ourselves to God; in the latter, God dedicates Himself to us...

<summary>The text debates whether sacred power is exclusive to Hebrew or shared by Greek and Egyptian "barbarous" names, citing authorities like Iamblichus, Orpheus, and Roman jurists to define what makes a word or object truly "sacred."</summary>
<keywords>theurgy, sacred names, Iamblichus, Orpheus, Hebrew vs Greek, Baruchias, consecration, legal definitions of sacredness</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from Book One regarding the nature of sacred language and the distinction between divine miracles and demonic magic. The speaker is likely Reuchlin’s character Capnion or a fellow interlocutor in the dialogue.</meta>

# BOOK TWO

...speaks, and illuminates our affairs with his assistance. And for that reason, we rightly name those things "sacred" in which God truly dwells. Not angels, not demons, but God himself, the highest, best, and greatest. Because of this, we also call the angels themselves "sacred" or "gods," since God dwells in them as well, and they are often said to bear his name. On the other hand, we call "profane" those who are similarly execrable, from whom God is far removed, as Statius says: *The fraternal lines and alternating wars fought with profane hatreds.* <note>original: "Fraternas acies alternaq; bella prophanis Decertata odiis." From Statius's Thebaid, describing the unholy war between brothers.</note> For according to the primary meaning of the word, just as "sacred" things are dedicated by us to God, so "profane" things may be interpreted as being far from the temple <note>Latin: "procul a phano"</note> and from religion, living outside of sacred things. Indeed, that sacred quality arises so much from our own action that from the "making of sacred things" <note>Latin: "sacris faciendis"</note> are derived the titles of the "sacrificing king" <term>sacrificulus rex: a priest-king or official who performs rites</term> who performs the rites, the "sacrifice" <note>Latin: "sacrificium"</note> which is performed by him, and the very object itself is called "sacred." Ovid writes in the twelfth book of the *Metamorphoses*: *The festival day was present, on which Achilles, victor over Cycnus, appeased Pallas with the blood of a slaughtered cow. When he placed the entrails upon the warm altars, and the smell, acceptable to the gods, penetrated the ether, the sacred rites took their portion, and the rest was given to the tables.* <note>original: "Festa dies aderat..." Ovid, Metamorphoses 12.150-155.</note> For he means that the sacred sacrifices consumed their own portion. Therefore, I will not deny that images, characters, letters, and words—whether names or utterances dedicated by us to God—are sacred, not only among the Hebrews, but also the Egyptians, Greeks, and other nations, which mortals have indeed consecrated and vowed to divinity according to human prayer. Nor can I bring a more suitable example to this business than what is sung by Orpheus—that ancient priest and seer, follower of the Egyptian Hermes and founder of the Pythagorean philosophy, whom you, Sidonius, brought up a little while ago—in the hymn to Lycian Venus, when he says: *For the leaders of our fatherland, guarding divine things, established a sacred colossus near the small town.* Although some have thought this hymn belongs to Proclus, because he mentions his own fatherland. But let us hear another poem of Orpheus, composed to the stars, which is beyond controversy: *Therefore, with sacred voices, I now invoke the pure spirits.* <note>original: "Vocibus ergo sacris iam dæmones inuoco puros." Note that "dæmones" here refers to spirits or tutelary deities in the Neoplatonic sense, not necessarily evil demons.</note> Therefore, that which is either God, or which—not men first, but He alone, the best and greatest—has consecrated by His presence and assistance and infused with His power: that alone, above all others, ought truly to be called "sacred" and "holy," which God has sanctioned and reserved for His honor, not by human allurements. Hence we commonly speak of the "holy tongue" of the Jews, and "sacred letters" written by the finger of God, and "sacred names" not invented by men, but instituted by God himself. If, however, any nation uses its own words in miracles, it is proved to be a deception <term>præstigium: an illusion or juggler's trick</term> rather than a miracle, and it obtains its effect not by the assistance of God, but by a pact with demons. How dangerous that is was discussed copiously among us yesterday. In short, just as great as the distance is between the signs of God and sorcery <term>goetia: low magic or witchcraft</term> or poisoning, so great is the difference (to conclude in a few words) in sacred matters between the words of the Hebrews and the words of idolaters. This we were taught through Moses, who turned a staff into a serpent by the word of God. But the Egyptian sorcerers, whom they called "wise men," cast similar images before the royal sight using the incanted words of human discipline; the serpent of Moses devoured these to reveal the deception. For this reason, a certain noble philosopher at Rome recently seemed to me to have proposed quite learnedly that no names in a magical and lawful work have equal power...

<summary>The text distinguishes between truly "sacred" things consecrated by God's presence (like the Hebrew language) and "profane" things or human-made magic. It argues that while many nations have sacred rites, true miracles like those of Moses differ fundamentally from the demonic deceptions of Egyptian sorcerers.</summary>
<keywords>sacred vs profane, Orpheus, Moses, Hebrew language, magic, miracles, demons, Egyptian sorcery, holy tongue</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue from Johannes Reuchlin's "De Verbo Mirifico" (On the Miracle-Working Word), where the characters discuss the inherent power of the Hebrew language in magic and theology. The catchword "quondā" (formerly) links to the start of the next page.</meta>

# ON THE MIRACLE-WORKING WORD

<meta>catchword: formerly</meta>

possess [power], just as the Hebrew tongue or those derived most closely from it do, because these were formed first of all by the voice of God. But that in which nature most powerfully exercises **Magic** <note>original: "Magica"; here referring to the natural or divine power inherent in words</note> is the voice of God. Now, Sidonius, I will no longer allow you to hesitate over what is deservedly manifest. For I believe that many nations have mixed Hebrew into their arcane prayers or secret operations for no other reason than to more certainly obtain their desires. I judge this to be no less certain in the cases of Orpheus, Pythagoras, and Plato; nothing else in their hidden mysteries was so carefully concealed or so secluded as the Hebrew names of divine power. They wished to reveal these to no one in writing, just as Plato himself suggests in a letter to Dionysius of Syracuse. 

I might ask of you, Capnion <note>The Greek-style nickname for the author, Johannes Reuchlin</note>, to teach me the reason why both the Evangelists and the Apostles, while they translated the rest of Christ's works into the Greek language, did not also translate those few words which the worker of miracles himself is recorded to have used. Instead, they left them entirely unchanged and untouched, inserting "barbarous" <note>In this context, "barbarous" refers to non-Greek/Latin languages, specifically Hebrew or Aramaic</note> words into the Greek text. For instance, when the children cried out to Christ *Hosthiana*—that is, "Save us, Lord"—it was later corrupted by the ignorance of scribes and is now improperly called *Hosanna*. 

Luke, being more skilled in the Greek language and foreseeing what would happen, knew he was writing his Gospel for Greeks who would be unable to pronounce this expression according to the property of the language; he judged it better to remain silent than to offer it up to be mocked by those who would corrupt it. However, the other three Evangelists, fearing nothing of this *Hosthiana*, decided by no means to omit it because of that remarkable particle, *na*, which they say is one of the divine names. 

Likewise, Mark, in that excellent miracle which Christ performed in raising the daughter of Jairus, did not hesitate to record the very Hebrew words of Christ by which the dead girl was restored to life and light. For taking the hand of the girl lying in the tomb, he spoke in two words: *Thabiti Kumi*. The first of these pertains to life, that is, "Look up"; the second pertains to the recovery of health, that is, "Arise." 

Later, some insufficiently diligent transcriber dared to mix into the fabric of the speech what he had found in the margin of the history, so that now almost all books hold it as *Thabita Kumi*, which is interpreted as "Girl, I say to you, arise." For it is not to be believed that the Evangelist translated what is not truly there. Therefore, "Girl, I say to you" has nothing to do with the matter here. 

And I greatly wonder at certain men of no small fame and name among you in the doctrine of all sciences—men whom you hold in high esteem—who say in this passage that *Thabita* was the name of the maiden. It is as if one should think that Christ—whom I, however much I have been a Jew, have always held greater than Apollonius of Tyana or any philosophers, and now, taught by you, affirm to be even more excellent than angels and all gods, and believe to be and have been God—it is as if one should think He first wished to search for a name with which to chant an arcane incantation. 

This is what all magicians agree regarding Apollonius: when he had escaped from the hands of Tigillinus (or more truly, Nero), he encountered a girl on the road who was thought to have died during her wedding. He touched her and first diligently inquired after the maiden's name. Having received it, he is said to have secretly pronounced something by which the girl revived. And this was formerly observed as a custom among the Romans: whenever they besieged a city, they would [seek] its name...

<summary>The text argues that Hebrew words possess unique divine power, explaining why Gospel writers preserved original Hebrew/Aramaic phrases like "Hosanna" and "Talitha Cumi" rather than translating them. It contrasts Christ's divine authority with the ritualistic magic of figures like Apollonius of Tyana.</summary>
<keywords>Hebrew language, divine names, Magic, Hosanna, Jairus' daughter, Apollonius of Tyana, Johannes Reuchlin, Gospel of Mark, linguistics, sacred philology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author's philological defense of specific biblical terms, moving from the mystical power of names to the linguistic roots of Christ's commands.</meta>

->BOOK THE SECOND<-

they might search accurately for the city. Knowing this, they were accustomed to summon forth all the tutelary gods of that city with a certain song <note>original: "carmine" - referring to a ritual incantation or 'evocatio' used by Romans to lure away an enemy's protecting deities</note>, and finally to conquer them. For this very reason, it happened that no one dared to betray the secret name of the city of Rome, lest they suffer a similar fate from their enemies. From this we learn that the proper names of things are of no small use in incantations—though let such a thing be far from him who utterly abolished all worship of demons, lest even in this work he should be thought to desire to know the name of the maiden whom he had never seen before. 

But the words *Thabiti Kumi* <note>original: "Thabiti Kumi" - a transliteration of the Aramaic phrase spoken by Jesus in Mark 5:41</note> are the words of Christ, which are interpreted in Latin as: "Look, Arise." "Look," so that they may see you as living; "Arise," so that all may behold you as healthy, and may confess that by my command you have been delivered not only from death but from every sickness. But I preach to you in vain things which you yourselves have often read among our people while searching the scriptures. For example, in the thirteenth Psalm, which begins: *How long, O Lord?* In the verse: *Look upon me and hear me, O Lord my God.* And in the twenty-second, which begins: *My God, my God.* In the verse: *I will number all my bones, they themselves looked and saw me.* And in the thirty-third Psalm: *Rejoice, ye just.* In the verse: *The Lord looked down from heaven.* And in the following Psalm, in the verse: *Look toward him and be enlightened.* Where your translation <note>The author refers to the Vulgate, the standard Latin Bible of the time</note> has it thus: *Come unto him and be enlightened.* And in the praises which you chant daily at the first hour, in the letter *Gimel*: *Reveal my eyes and I shall consider.* Where it ought more truly to be placed: *I shall look upon the wonders of your law.* 

For this is the same word *Abita* <note>original: "Abita" - referring to the Hebrew root HBT (to look/behold)</note>, about which we must now dispute, since in the second person it makes *Thabita* or *Thabiti*, that is, "you shall look" or "look." For just as the Latins are often accustomed to use the future indicative in place of the imperative—as in "you shall speak in turns, the Muses love alternating verses" <note>A quote from Virgil's Eclogues</note> instead of "speak"—so also among the Hebrews <term>Hebrews: the Jewish people or the Hebrew language</term> the same is found to be a very frequent use, as here: "you shall look" for "look." 

But *Kumi* by itself is an imperative of the feminine gender, as in the second chapter of the Song of Songs: *Behold, my beloved speaks to me: Arise, make haste, my love*, where *Kumi* is placed, that is, "arise." You will find such things corrupted in many places of Holy Scripture by the unlearned, while in this age theologians are more accustomed to notice the dialectical sophistries of Aristotle <note>A critique of Scholasticism, which favored logic over linguistic study of the Bible</note> than the words of divine inspiration and the Holy Spirit. Hence, through the pursuit of human invention, the heavenly tradition itself is neglected, and the loquacity of men extinguishes the word of God. 

A similar error of the scribes is detected in the Acts of the Apostles, which has begun to be common among you, even among the learned and indeed the teachers, regarding the raising of Dorcas from the family of the saints in Joppa <note>Modern Jaffa</note>, which is a district of Judea, as Pliny says, very near to Lydda. For there they do not write the name of that little woman correctly, as I have often found it unlearnedly written in both Greek and Latin codices. By which example I would believe they also failed in the aforementioned Gospel. But the proper name of the faithful disciple was *Tabia*, without any aspiration and without a 't' in the final syllable, which its interpretation *Dorcas* <note>Greek for "gazelle"</note> teaches; and it is the Assyrian <note>Here used to mean Aramaic/Syriac</note> name *Tabia*, which signifies a "Gazelle," just as we read in the Chaldean <term>Chaldean: often used in this period to refer to Aramaic</term> book called *Illen Pithgamai* <note>original: "Illen Pithgamai" - likely referring to the Targum or Aramaic translations of the Bible</note>. 

Furthermore, that word now comes to mind which Christ is written to have said in Mark. For they bring to him a man deaf and dumb, and they pray that he might lay his hand upon him. And taking him aside from the crowd, he put his fingers into...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Wonder-Working Word" (Verbum Mirificum), focusing on the linguistic and mystical significance of Christ's final words on the cross and his use of the Psalms.</meta>

# ON THE WONDER-WORKING WORD

->[He touched] his ears, and spitting, he touched his tongue and looking up to heaven he groaned and said, *Hiphathah*, which is "be opened"<- <note>original: "Hiphathah, quod est adaperire"</note>, which ought to be written at the beginning with a smooth breathing *h* <note>original: "h psilen" - referring to the Greek 'psilos' or smooth breathing mark</note>, and at the end with a stronger aspiration. For it is the passive imperative of the active verb *Pathah*, which is "he opened," and in the future tense *Ephathah*, that is "I shall open," as in Psalm 78 in the verse: *I will open my mouth in parables.* And in the fiftieth [Psalm] in the verse: *Lord, you shall open my lips*, and so with the rest. For you are well-versed and learned not only in Greek but also in Hebrew literature, [knowing how] to form from the passive *Niphathah* (which is "it is opened") the masculine singular imperative *Hiphathah*, that is "be opened" or "be thou opened," just as the powerful word of Christ thundered forth. 

Let us come to his most surpassing death; hanging on the wood and now at the point of death, he said that which is placed in the twenty-second Psalm according to the Hebrews, but among you [Latin speakers] in the twenty-first: *Eli Eli lamah asabathani* <note>My God, my God, why have you forsaken me?</note>. For Matthew recites it just as the matter stands, although *Sabathani* is also said corruptly through the loss of the first syllable, which ought to be marked by the letter *Ayin* <term>Ayin: the sixteenth letter of the Hebrew alphabet, often representing a guttural sound</term>. Your scholars observe that Mark is even a little less corrected in this place, for he puts *Elohe*, or according to others *Elohi*, less aptly. For *Elohe* is "God," but *Elohi* is not read by the Hebrews. It could indeed be designated as *Elohai*, "my God." But the words of the Prophet [David] are not to be changed; I believe he [Christ] recalled them as he was about to die, and that he began the Psalter <note>The Book of Psalms</note> from the beginning of his Passion until his life was ended. 

Finally, in the last sigh of his passing, he reached the Psalm which is the thirtieth in order according to the Seventy Translators <note>The Septuagint, the Greek Old Testament</note>, so that both the beginning and the end of the Passion were terminated by a single number. For just as the high priests promised thirty pieces of silver to the traitor Judas before Christ was captured, so he himself did not so much promise as pay thirty Psalms to God the Father before he died. You will grant me pardon if it is not permitted to compare wicked things with good; nevertheless, consider and recount within your minds how fittingly this blessed man—if it is right to call him a man—completed his entire condemnation and torture with these thirty Psalms. He took his beginning from the time of the anxiety suffered in the garden across the brook. Of this, the entire fifty-fourth Psalm makes mention, where it is written in the manner of a prophecy: *But I will call upon God, and the Lord shall save me; evening and morning and at noon I will speak and declare.* 

And so, he spoke with God during the entire time of his Passion, at evening, morning, and noon—often in secret, which was predicted earlier through David: *My prayer shall be turned into my own bosom.* Sometimes, however, he openly pronounced certain things from those same songs, which his followers nearby heard and committed to memory, while those perhaps absent did not hear. Of the things perceived, therefore, we can judge that this was what he said: *Eli Eli lamah asabathani*, and *Into your hands I commend my spirit*. Some have a title for this [Psalm] meaning "ecstasy," which greatly befits one dying, but it is not found among the Hebrews. Nor indeed is it lacking in divine mystery that after this same thirtieth Psalm, and not before, there immediately follows the inscription *Maskil*, that is, "understanding," "knowledge," or "instruction." For after the death of Christ, whose final prayer was this thirtieth Psalm, we finally began to understand and see him whom they pierced and through whom they struck; wherefore he prayed with a singular vow, that the Father [might forgive] the one crucifying...

<summary>The author provides a linguistic analysis of Christ's last words, linking his suffering to the recitation of the first thirty Psalms and explaining the Hebrew roots of terms like 'Hiphathah' and 'Eli'. He argues that Christ's death fulfilled the 'Maskil' (understanding) promised in the scriptures.</summary>
<keywords>Hiphathah, Hebrew etymology, Passion of Christ, Psalms, Eli Eli lamah asabathani, Maskil, Septuagint, numerology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Capnion (the scholar Johannes Reuchlin), Sidonius, and the Jewish scholar Baruchias regarding the mystical power of the Hebrew language.</meta>

# BOOK TWO

->**Page 30**<-

...He would forgive those who fixed Him there, since they knew not what they did. <note>A reference to Luke 23:34, "Father, forgive them, for they know not what they do."</note> But while I am laboring to explain the Hebrew words which the Evangelists used without alteration while writing, I have somehow slipped into the secret coverings of your religion, so that you may realize I have turned toward your profession through some prior meditation and not so suddenly. 

Then **SIDONIUS** said: My dear **Capnion**, who is so skilled in divine letters, what then shall I contribute to our conversation? But if I remember rightly, **Baruchias** has provided me much peace of mind, so that I am not forced to discourse at length on the sacred names. For he teaches us that only Hebrew words are numbered among the truly sacred; he tries to affirm this both from their antiquity and from their conversation with the Divine, as well as a certain strict covenant of God with the Jews, and because the language is more solid—finally, by many arguments. If these things are true, I, who have spent my life more than others on Greek and Latin, shall be glad to have found time only for listening.

And **CAPNION** said: Indeed, I would wonder at his great knowledge of the Gospels, if he had not confessed long ago to having spent no small labor on them, a fact which the matter itself shows and the business indicates. But let us hear him while it is light. For the day has passed midday. Come again, **Baruchias**, do not shrink from bringing forth what you have received concerning the secret words—hidden, as you were saying, almost under a bushel <note>A biblical metaphor for hiding the truth; see Matthew 5:15.</note>. We both pray and urge you—for certainly your tongue (let **Sidonius** say what he will) delights us greatly, and we are both your disciples.

But since you so command, **BARUCHIAS** said: I must obey, provided you do not despise the simplicity of my speech, for I am imbued with no elegancies. And now, since we are heading toward evening, I will no longer urge you, **Capnion**, to give an account of why those writing the life and teaching of Christ in the Greek tongue also deigned to intersperse Hebrew words. After all, you have already become accustomed to concluding every prayer, whether public or private, with a single word: *Amen* <note>original: "Amen"</note>, which is also found in the writings of Paul.

To this **CAPNION** said: I will settle this briefly so you do not hesitate. However, it is not safe enough in so excellent a matter for me to use my own invention; instead, I shall cite **Origen**, the light of our theologians, of whom it is said: "where he is good, no one is better." For he says in his book *Against Celsus*: "In certain sacred names a wonderful power lies hidden." <note>Origen was a 3rd-century Christian scholar; his work *Contra Celsum* defended Christianity against pagan philosophy.</note> Therefore, these very names are not to be translated from the Hebrew language into another, but are to be preserved in their own characters. You have what you have long wished to draw out: namely, that there is a miraculous power in divine names and prayers, because of which both the forms of the characters and the words themselves remain uncorrupted; otherwise, just as when the frame of a man is destroyed, the spirit enclosed in the body and all its power likewise vanishes.

Furthermore, as **Iamblichus** replied to **Porphyry**: "If those names had been established by the pact and agreement of men, it would make no difference to exchange some for others." <note>Iamblichus and Porphyry were Neoplatonist philosophers who debated the efficacy of "theurgy" or divine rituals.</note> But we understood long ago, **Baruchias**, and we confess it to be true, that they were received from the Divine, not produced by humanity. To which that same noble Pythagorean, **Iamblichus**, adds: "For names translated into another language do not preserve entirely the same meaning; rather, there are certain things proper to individual nations which cannot be signified to other nations by the voice." Added to this is the fact that if the properties of the significations...

<summary>The speakers discuss why Hebrew words like "Amen" are preserved in the Greek New Testament, concluding that sacred names possess a divine power that is lost if translated into other languages. They cite Origen and Iamblichus to argue that Hebrew is a divinely-given language rather than a human invention.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue concerning the "Wonder-Working Word," specifically debating the inherent power of language and divine will. The catchword "di uocē" (of the voice) links to the subsequent page.</meta>

# ON THE WONDER-WORKING WORD

...though we may be able to interpret them through another language, they nevertheless do not preserve the same power when changed. I would wish to answer you using those authors, but beware of thinking that I feel the same as you regarding all words—namely, that they possess that majesty which you spread so widely by reputation. For who will doubt that God Almighty—and as the Greeks say, the *All-Holder* <note>original: "omnitenentē" / Greek: "pantokrator"</note>—is able to adorn words with virtues? Therefore, the power of God is evident, but not yet His will, which alone is the cause of things. For God has done all things whatsoever He willed in heaven and on earth. For this reason, unless you show that God willed this or that other word to prevail with such and such virtue, you will have accomplished nothing.

But Baruchias said: "I did not promise to teach you, but to report what I once received concerning the secrets from our college. If I perform this duty rightly, there will certainly be plenty for me to rejoice in, nor will I bind myself to any other stake. Since yesterday evening it was agreed with one voice that in divine matters one must believe—although I know it will never be a matter of doubt among you—that God can do all things, a fact which our mind grasps by its very nature. If God is above all things and He upon whom all things depend, both existing and non-existing <note>A philosophical distinction: things that are real and things that are merely possible or conceptual.</note>, as even the eminent theologians assert. Thus, the highest power—indeed, a power superior to all highest power—pertaining to both the existent and the non-existent, lies with God, and it is bounded by no limits. Otherwise, it would not be the highest or above the highest. Indeed, if He shows this power dwelling in a word or a thing, it matters not, provided He desires His virtue to inhabit that place.

However, we clearly recognize this decree of God and His will either through the experience of deeds or by the speech of divine inspiration. In this place, it is necessary to apply both Holy Scripture and the dogmas of contemplative men through whose mouths the Divinity speaks, as well as the heroic histories of the blessed. For who has known the mind of the Lord of eternity? No one, except the Spirit who is within Him, and finally he to whom He reveals Himself. Just as among men, the least of creatures knows nothing of a man's thoughts except his own spirit which is in him, or some friend to whom the spirit has announced them through the man—or the man through his spirit—or shown them by signs.

That God spoke in this way with Abraham, Moses, and Joshua, and showed them the virtues of divine words, and confirmed them with wondrous effects, our ancestors—both the learned and those who experienced it themselves—have commended to memory through letters for us, the offshoots of their lineage. Receiving these with a grateful heart, we experience and feel them daily if we wish.

But to get to the matter: for those desiring to hear of portents, let those things come to mind which were discussed abundantly by us above: that only the first and greatest God performs portents, miracles, prodigies, and signs—especially those which are true and not deceptive illusions <note>original: "præstygiosa"</note>. Or, when He sometimes deigns to grant His divine honor to His angels, He entrusts them with wonderful works to be performed. Yet this is done in His own name, lest He give His honor to strangers and His glory to another, and man turn to the worship of the creature, having abandoned the Creator. For this reason, He places His name in the angels, lest that which He always hates most sharply of all—the worship of forms, which in Greek we call *idolatry* <note>original: "idolatriam"</note>—be admitted by us. Whence He says to His people: 'Behold, I send my angel to go before you, and keep you in the way, and bring you into the place which I have prepared. Observe him and hear his voice...'"

<summary>Baruchias argues that while God is omnipotent, the power of specific words depends entirely on Divine Will. He asserts that this power is revealed through scripture, tradition, and the history of prophets like Moses and Abraham.</summary>
<keywords>Divine Will, omnipotence, Baruchias, sacred language, idolatry, miracles, angels, Abraham, Moses, Joshua</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from Book One regarding the nature of divine power, now transitioning into a specific study of the names of God and the celestial hierarchy.</meta>

# SECOND BOOK

Listen to his voice, and do not think him to be despised, for he will not let it pass when you have sinned. For my name is in him. <note>A reference to Exodus 23:21, discussing the Angel of the Lord who bears the Divine Name.</note> Since, therefore, wonderful works are carried out first by God, and secondly by angels according to our prayer, it seemed most fitting to me to speak first of the divine name which is known to pertain to God himself, as far as memory serves; then of that which also concerns the angels at times, according to the thinness of our human weakness. In this matter we shall observe an order, so that we may first recount to you those names of God whose meaning has long been clear. After these, we shall attempt those whose meaning we have received as not yet manifest, or revealed only to a few. Therefore, many of us within the memory of our fathers, and some of yours even to this day, are read who have dared to write about secret names; wherefore my part in discussing them will be easier if I explain summarily those things which you have seen described at length among various authors. Jerome <note>St. Jerome (c. 347–420 AD), the translator of the Vulgate Bible.</note> to Marcella commemorates ten names of God. Dionysius the Areopagite <note>A Christian theologian and philosopher of the late 5th to early 6th century, famous for his works on celestial hierarchies.</note> nearly forty-five; others seventy-two; others more, others fewer, each according to his own capacity. Indeed, what fixed number of names can there be for him who is both unnamable and celebrated by every name? Therefore, let us neither thrust ourselves into a corner from which we cannot break out, nor plead the cause so slowly and superficially that we are proved to hold no memory of divine honor. Truly, I desire that you assent to my words no more than the sacred utterances themselves allow, nor shall I myself assent otherwise, lest I—breathing mud and clay, a worm and not a man—should seem injurious toward God the Best, and like another Uzzah <note>original: "Oza". A biblical figure who was struck dead for touching the Ark of the Covenant (2 Samuel 6:6-7).</note> touch the ark and add something beyond that which God himself has spoken. For I judge that the first sin of the human race was that which Eve, the mother of all living, added to the words of God more than God himself had said, which was indeed the beginning for subsequent crimes and the origin of divine vengeance. For before the woman had arisen, God said to Adam: "Eat from every tree of paradise, but from the tree of the knowledge of good and evil you shall not eat." But let us see how the newly born Eve changed the words of the Creator; speaking to the serpent, she said: "Of the fruit of the tree which is in the middle of paradise, God commanded us that we should not eat, and that we should not touch it, lest perhaps we die." She added the plural pronoun "us," although he had nowhere commanded Eve not to eat, since she was not yet born. She joined to it, "And that we should not touch it," which God also had not said. Wherefore, as if she wished to make the Lord a liar, she again added a particle of doubt and unbelief, saying, "Lest perhaps we die," which the Creator had threatened singularly and firmly to Adam alone: "For on whatever day you eat of it, you shall die the death." Whence the serpent for his counsel, and Eve for her aid, and Adam for his transgression, paid the penalties. Therefore, lest I fall into the same vice or crime before the trembling Author of the universe, his supreme mercy must be prayed to, that he might notice our benevolence and piety rather than our ruin if by chance the foot should slip; to which matter may he deign to bring help from the most sacred inner chambers of his love toward us, for the glory and honor of his name, blessed unto eternal ages. But what might be that word so illustrious, so effective, so venerable, so wholesome, in which more easily of our prayer...

<summary>The author introduces the study of the Names of God, citing authorities like Jerome and Dionysius while warning against the spiritual danger of misinterpreting or adding to divine revelation, using the fall of Eve as a cautionary example.</summary>
<keywords>Names of God, Angelology, Jerome, Dionysius the Areopagite, Eve, Original Sin, Uzzah, Divine Law</keywords>

--- Page 65 ---
<meta>The previous page likely discussed the multiplicity of divine names and the limitations of human language in describing the infinite. This page continues the investigation into the "essential" name of God as revealed to Moses.</meta>

-># ON THE WONDER-WORKING WORD<-

<meta>catchword: I have prefaced</meta>

We become masters of the subject when we consider that many names of the most high God are found, as we have said. For as the prophet Zechariah exclaims: *In that day there shall be one God and his name shall be one* <note>Zechariah 14:9</note>, we are reminded that at this present time there are many names for God, because in the future age there will be only one. Why do we drag out the hours, why do we waste time in vain, why do we wait so long, as if much discussion were needed to explain how that which lacks a name can be designated by so many? Does a different name for the divinity exist among those in heaven than among mortals? Will the customary naming of this world cease in the future life of our souls? Does God have a name when we consider his immanence within himself? Or indeed, is no name necessary for him then? Setting aside everything that pertains no more to the matter than to the delay and slowness of our business, let us explain in a few words. For although the supreme divinity among the immortals, the heavenly beings, and those above the heavens is called by some super-eminent name according to their custom (as is likely), we shall nevertheless treat more of those things which concern our own situation. We say that, according to human contemplation of the sacred names divinely handed down to us, we attribute one kind to **essence**, another to **power**, another to **operation**, and the rest to our **affection** toward God.

By **essence**, I mean God’s separation from all things and his total enclosure within himself—and his extreme, deep, and solitary withdrawal (as that noble philosopher of our age says) into the most remote recess of his divinity, hiding himself in the innermost abyss of his own darkness. According to this, they affirm that God established a venerable name for himself among men; for since he alone knows himself, he alone gives himself a name. Now, let us see by what argument wise men prove this, and what we may perceive through that most sacred name of essence. Moses said to God: *Behold, I go to the children of Israel and I shall say to them, "The God of your fathers has sent me to you," and they will say to me, "What is his name?" What shall I say to them?* And God said to Moses: **Ehieh** <note>Hebrew: אהיה, "I will be" or "I am"</note>, which means *I shall be*; and he said: *Thus shall you say to the children of Israel, "Ehieh has sent me to you."* <note>Exodus 3:14</note>

Plato had learned this name during his long travels among the Assyrians <note>A common Renaissance belief that Greek philosophers studied Near Eastern wisdom</note>, which he finally translated for the Greeks into two letters: **On** <note>Greek: Ὄν, "Being" or "That which is"</note>. For in the *Timaeus* he says: *It is therefore, in my opinion, first to be distinguished what is **On**—that is, the being which always is and has no beginning—and what is that which has a beginning but never truly is.* The former is grasped by the intellect with reason as always being in itself; the latter is perceived by opinion with irrational sense as something generated and perishable, as that which never truly exists. By that name, therefore, Plato distinguishes the world from the Creator, and a multitude of most learned men followed him, except that some changed the neuter gender into the masculine. For Plato names the most excellent author of the universe "that which is" with the single word **To On** <note>Greek: τὸ ὄν (neuter)</note>, but many of his followers call him "he who is," **Ho On** <note>Greek: ὁ ὤν (masculine)</note>. This change is consistent with the Hebrews; for because they follow a nature that is not monstrous <note>i.e., they follow natural grammatical gender</note>, they have only two genders, masculine and feminine. Thus, a neuter word among the Latins or Greeks is easily included under their masculine. Therefore, whether one says "he who is" or "that which is," it is pronounced by its essential name according to Moses as **Ehieh**, which is derived from the substantive verb **Hayah** <note>Hebrew: היה, "to be"</note>.

<summary>The author discusses the transition from many divine names to one essential name, identifying the Hebrew 'Ehieh' with Plato's philosophical concept of 'Being' (On).</summary>
<keywords>Divine Names, Zechariah, Moses, Plato, Timaeus, Ehieh, Being, Essence, Hebrew Etymology, Renaissance Neoplatonism</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue from Book One, transitioning into a deeper mystical discussion of the names of God. The speaker is addressing a group of learned men, including "Capnion" (Johannes Reuchlin).</meta>

->BOOK TWO<-

...derive according to the laws of grammar, with a letter added to the first. I do not know if it is entirely worthy of forgiveness that I speak so barbarically <note>The speaker is apologizing for using technical or non-classical "barbarous" terminology</note> in so elegant a cause before you, most eloquent debaters. But though a Demosthenes or a Cicero might delay, I truly believe that such a man would be mute and tongueless in the face of the mysteries of God. For your Origen <note>Origen of Alexandria, an early Christian scholar</note>, who was not inferior to the most splendid orators, speaks in these very words in his twentieth homily on Exodus, if I recall: "All men, in comparison to the Divine Word, must be considered not only uneloquent but even mute." Therefore, if you can listen to my perpetual barbarism without annoyance, I shall gladly proceed.

"Indeed, proceed," said **SIDONIUS**. "For it is no secret to us that this province of study is the most difficult of all; and furthermore, I myself believe that divine matters can in no way be sufficiently expressed in a human manner. I am mindful of what history relates concerning Simonides, a man of outstanding learning. For when the Tyrant Hiero asked him what God was, Simonides requested a space of one day to deliberate. When Hiero asked again the next day, Simonides asked for two days. Then, as the Tyrant asked more frequently, Simonides always doubled the number of days. When Hiero finally asked why he did so, he replied: 'Because the longer I consider it, the more obscure the matter seems to me.' Therefore, I marvel that this business has been handled by you thus far even in our common speech and so lucidly; so do not delay us further."

**CAPNION** <note>The scholar Johannes Reuchlin</note> also urged him toward this. Then he said:

You have the name of the most high God, which certain learned men have thought to be the first of all, since it seems to designate the very essence of God himself, and does not share it with any other things. For nothing exists outside of God, because all things consist of a certain proportion of composition. God alone is most simple, and for that reason He "is who He is" <note>original: *est qui est*</note>; everything else, however, exists by that which makes it be, rather than being essence itself. 

Nevertheless, other men—by no means to be despised and most studious in the contemplation of the most secret things—enter much more closely into the very inner shrines of divinity. They most reverently weigh the magnitude of a certain other name, and above this name *Ehieh* <note>Hebrew: "I Am"</note> or *On* <note>Greek: "The Being/He who is"</note> they establish another. For as your Dionysius the Areopagite <note>A Christian mystic and philosopher</note> explains: "He who is" <note>original: *On*</note>, that is, He who exists, is a certain 'supereffential' <note>term: *supereffentiale* (beyond the nature of being or essence)</note> power of the whole of being; the founding cause and maker of entity, subsistence, substance, essence, and nature; the principle and measure of eternities and times; the entity and age of beings and the time of those things which come to be; the 'being' for those things which exist in any way at all, and the 'generation' for those things which are produced in any way. 

Here it is clearly perceived how the First Being concurs with flowing things, providing them the strength to persist. And although the most simple essence of God—through which He makes Himself known simply and freely—embraces 'being' itself in an excellent way so as to be the 'supereffential' of the whole of being, yet it does not designate God as enclosed and resting, but as looking upon things themselves and insinuating Himself with things to grant them subsistence. This you will easily understand from the words of the same Dionysius which you have just heard, after sharp meditation. Wherefore, considering the separate retreat of His ineffable super-eminence within Himself, they primarily venerate the most holy name **HU** <note>Hebrew: "He"</note>.

<summary>The dialogue explores the inadequacy of human language to describe God, citing Simonides and Dionysius the Areopagite to explain divine names like "Ehieh" (I Am) and "Hu" (He) as expressions of God's "supereffential" nature.</summary>
<keywords>Divine Names, Dionysius the Areopagite, Simonides, Hiero, Capnion, Reuchlin, Essence, Supereffential, Ehieh, Hu, Theology</keywords>

--- Page 67 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the mystical names of God, specifically transitioning from the name 'Ehieh' (I Am) to the name 'Hu' (He/The Same), exploring its philosophical and poetic parallels.</meta>

# ON THE WONDER-WORKING WORD

And they place this name even before *Ehieh* <note>Hebrew: "I Am"</note>, which God himself revealed to Isaiah, the most noble of prophets, saying: "I am the Lord, *Hu* <note>Hebrew: "He" or "The Same"</note> is my name, I will not give my glory to another." <note>Isaiah 42:8</note> You see now that the aforementioned name contains a light beneath a bushel and indeed restrains its rays; it designates God dwelling solitarily in hidden retreats without any outflowing of powers, but containing himself in his own most simple and absolute consistency—not going out, governing nothing, but permitting all things, accepting those things which are done well, and likewise not recording those things done poorly. Such is the way God speaks later in the same prophet: "I am, I am *Hu*, blotting out your iniquities with myself, and I will not remember your sins." <note>Isaiah 43:25</note> For because *Hu* does not give his glory to another, because he does not remember sins, and because he blots out iniquities "with himself" and not for the sake of something else outside himself, it declares the significance of the name. For *Hu* denotes God as so separated from things that he either acts, permits, or rests all things within himself. And often this name is placed before following negative statements, as God spoke a little earlier: "That you may understand that I am *Hu*; before me no god was formed, and after me there shall be none." <note>Isaiah 43:10</note>

This name, so august and distinguished, was once called *Tauton* <note>Greek: "The Same"</note> by the Greek philosophers, especially the Platonists, and by the Latins it is called "The Very Same" <note>Latin: *Idem ipsum*</note>. For the one hundred and first Psalm <note>Psalm 102 in modern numbering</note> pronounces thus concerning God: "And thou art *Hu*, and thy years shall not fail." You chant this in Latin in the assembly of sacred rites: "But thou art the very same." <note>Latin: *Tu autem idem ipse es*</note> And Virgil transferred the same name to Jupiter with equal understanding when Jupiter was promulgating an oracle, saying: "Whatever fortune each man has today, whatever hope each cuts out for himself, whether he be Trojan or Rutulian, I shall hold them with no distinction; whether the camp is held in siege by the fates of the Italians, or by Troy’s wretched error and sinister warnings. Nor do I release the Rutulians; each man’s own beginnings shall bring him labor and fortune. King Jupiter is the same <note>Latin: *idem*</note> to all." <note>Aeneid, Book X</note> You see this name "The Same" attributed to King Jupiter.

Therefore, a term of such majesty, so protective and helpful as *Tauton*—which is *Hu*—is super-essentially eternal, unchangeable, remaining in itself, according to the same things and behaving in the same way. It is similarly present to all things, and is itself, according to itself, firmly and undefiledly placed within the most beautiful boundaries of substantive identity. It is untransmutable, inflexible, invariable, unmixed, immaterial, most simple, needing nothing, incapable of increase or diminution, unbegotten—not as if not yet born, or as if imperfect, or because it is not begotten by this or that, or because it exists nowhere or in no way—but as unbegotten above all things and absolutely unbegotten. It always is, and is perfect in itself, and is the same according to itself, and is uniformly and identically limited by itself.

I fear here that in translating the Greek <note>original: *transserendis graecis*</note> I may poorly offend learned ears. But perhaps I do not have to deliver a speech before a judge, nor do you sit as listeners in a forum or on the benches of a courtroom, but in an Academy of the most secret philosophy, which desires naked truths more than adorned words—although if it were possible for me, I ought indeed, according to Horace Flaccus, to say words that are both pleasant and fitting alongside the facts. However it may be, I would fix the sublime dignity of that name at the very summit of all things, if it did not sometimes—indeed rather often—flow down into these sublunary things and communicate with objects in such a way that it takes a surname from them,

<summary>The author explores the divine name 'Hu' (The Same), linking Hebrew prophecy with Platonic philosophy and Virgilian poetry to describe God's absolute, unchanging identity. He argues that this name represents God in His most solitary, self-contained state, yet one that remains 'the same' for all creation.</summary>
<keywords>Hu, Divine Names, Platonism, Isaiah, Virgil, Jupiter, Identity, Unbegotten, Super-essential, Theology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical and theological exploration of divine names, specifically focusing on the identity and essence of God as expressed through the name "Hu" and the element of fire. It follows the previous discussion on the unity of the divine nature.</meta>

# SECOND BOOK
<sig>f ii</sig>

Whatever we consider to exist not as something else but as the very same thing, applies not only to substances—as Dido says to Aeneas: *For your sake, the same <note>original: "eundem"</note> modesty is extinguished*—but even to the consequences of substances. As Anna, Dido’s sister, says: *You should have called me to the same fates; the same grief and the same hour would have taken us both by the sword.* Likewise, Virgil says of Aeneas’s companions: *The same passion possesses them all at once.* Isaiah seems to me to have explained this participation of things with the "Same" word, through whom God speaks: *I am He <note>original: "Hu"</note>, I am the first and I am the last.* And a little later, in the fifty-first chapter: *I am, I am He <note>Hu</note> who comforts you,* by which it clearly becomes known to us that this "Hu" is active in the beginning, the end, and the middle of all things.

To demonstrate that your views agree with ours, listen again to the Areopagite <note>Dionysius the Areopagite, a Christian theologian and philosopher</note>, who says of "Hu"—which he calls *The Same* <note>original: "Tauton"</note>—the following: *The Same itself radiates from itself to all things fit to participate in it, connecting some things to others by the abundance and origin of identity within itself, and containing opposites identically through the one and singular pre-eminent cause of all identity.* Whence, like a king, He penetrates all things—heavenly, earthly, and infernal—by His nod. Nor is there any power in heaven, on earth, or in the underworld that can resist the power of this name; and therefore He is called the Lord of Hosts <note>original: "dominus uirtutum"</note>, "Hu," because every virtue and power is subject to Him. The prophet asks in the twenty-third Psalm: *Who is He <note>Hu</note>, that King of Glory?* The spirit in the prophet responds: *The Lord of Hosts is He <note>Hu</note>, He is the King of Glory forever.*

Another thing comes to mind which I recall by the mere mention of the aforementioned name. For among the seven names consecrated together by Moses, alongside "Hu," there is also placed "Esth" <note>Hebrew: Esh (אש), meaning fire</note> in a single context of speech in the repetition of the law which you call Deuteronomy, which in Latin we call *fire*. Therefore, He more often hears [prayers] in fire, as with Abraham, Elijah, David, and others of whom histories exist, as if in a matter agreeable to divinity. Moses saw God in fire, and an angel in a flame of fire. Ezekiel’s divine vision was the splendor of fire, and lightning coming out of the fire.

Likewise, Plato maintained that the supreme God exists in a fiery essence. His ungrateful disciple, Aristotle—as he often does elsewhere, so now too not without slander—reproached him in a certain book written to Alexander, King of the Macedonians. There he found—or rather, did not so much find as learn from the Brahmans of India—that a "fifth essence" is the dwelling place of divinity. *We do not,* he says, *plainly say that God is in a fiery essence, as some who wander into error concerning the divine do.* Then he attempts to cite a witness of the truth (supposedly better than Plato) when he adds: *For so also Homer says: "The lot of Jove is the heaven; he sits in the broad ether among the clouds."*

Therefore, the prophet Zechariah spoke better—and certainly much better, since he heard God himself speaking from the mouth of an angel: *I will be to her,* God said, *a wall of fire round about, and for the honor of "Ehieh" <note>Hebrew: "I Am"</note> in her midst.* Regarding this, so that I may also flatter our Sidonius in citing philosophers, that man Plutarch—a man excellently learned in many arts—makes mention in his collection of physical dogmas. *They describe,* he says, *the knowledge of God thus: an intellectual and fiery spirit not having a form, but transforming itself into whatever it wishes and equaling itself to all things.* Whence Zoroaster says: *All things are begotten from one fire.* You also, Capnion...

<summary>This page explores the divine name "Hu" (He/The Same) and the concept of God as a "fiery essence," drawing parallels between Hebrew scripture, Platonic philosophy, and mystical traditions.</summary>
<keywords>Divine Names, Hu, Fire, Plato, Aristotle, Zechariah, Dionysius the Areopagite, Metaphysics, Identity</keywords>

--- Page 69 ---
ON THE WONDER-WORKING WORD

Indeed, we Christians (for I do not wish to abandon the profession I have begun) commonly call God "fire," following Paul of Tarsus, who, writing to the Hebrews, says: *God is a consuming fire.* <note>Hebrews 12:29</note> Yet you venerate this name as attributed to the Holy Spirit under a certain distinct property of divinity, which John the Baptist taught in both Mark and Luke, saying: *I indeed baptize you with water unto repentance, but he who comes after me is mightier than I, whose shoes I am not worthy to carry; he will baptize you with the Holy Spirit and with fire.* <note>Matthew 3:11; Luke 3:16</note> Through the figure of *hendiadys* <note>A literary device where two words express a single complex idea; here, "Spirit and fire" means "Fiery Spirit."</note>, this means "in the Holy Spirit, that is, fire," in whose likeness he appeared to the apostles after Christ was taken up.

"Your recitation pleases me greatly," said Capnion <note>The scholar Johannes Reuchlin’s Greek-style pseudonym, meaning "Smoky."</note>, "since I believe it agrees not a little with our religion. First of all, there is nothing I have ever received with greater pleasure than the way you have arranged these divine names—not by human impulse, I believe—so that, contrary to the opinion of many, even among our own people whom we crown as theologians, you have established not *Ehieh* <note>Hebrew: "I AM"</note> as they think, but *Hu* <note>Hebrew: "HE"</note> as the first, so that the order is: *Hu*, *Ehieh*, *Esth* <note>He, I Am, Fire</note>. It is as if one were speaking to the ineffable one Trinity and triune Unity: 'You are the same Fire,' just as we say regarding the nature of visible things: substance, power, and operation, all within the same single individual."

"And perhaps," said Sidonius, "just as Orpheus arranged the sacred hymns according to consecrated names: Night, Heaven, and Ether. Or Homer: Zeus, Athena, and Aphrodite. Of the first, he says: *Jupiter, veiling the clouds and reigning in the ether.* Of the second: *If wise Minerva grants me beauty.* For because of the head of Jupiter, from whose brain she is said to have been born, we call her the God of Great Counsel and Much Counsel, whom you Hebrews call *Hochma* <note>Hebrew: Wisdom (Chokhmah)</note>. Of the third: *So spoke the father of men and gods and laughed, and called upon shining Venus, to whom he said: 'Daughter, the command of war is not left to your care; rather, your task is to kindle the gods with soft embrace and flames.'* Regarding this Trinity, I might sing that line of Maro <note>Virgil</note>, or rather of Pythagoras: *God delights in an odd number.* <note>Virgil, Eclogues 8.75</note> For I seem to see that you have encompassed the entire majesty of the divine essence in a super-eminent triad. From this, perhaps, he creates beings, seizes what is created, and perfects what is seized. But why do I delay Baruchias? Come, if you have anything further."

Then he replied: "I do indeed still have a very few things which I once skillfully impressed upon my memory, yet they are nothing compared to this class of names I have just reviewed. For I cannot imagine, among all that I have either read or learned by hearing, that any other name remains besides those I have already brought forth. For we embrace the divine essence by these three names alone: *Hu*, *Ehieh*, and *Esth*, which would signify the essence of God even if there were now no world, whether exemplary or corporeal. For then God would be both the same self, and his own essence, and would be of a fiery nature. Not indeed as elemental fire is, nor as stellar or celestial fire, but as an inexpressible splendor in himself, of which all other fires are but shadow and darkness. This is what the monuments of the Chaldeans indicate in Greek verses found in Synesius: *Do not look down upon the black world without light, beneath which deep and dark shadows are spread; it is filthy and likewise obscure, and likewise feeding idols.* For in this way, even that world—if there is any archetype—is a shadow and an image—

<summary>The dialogue explores the divine nature through a linguistic and mystical lens, equating the Christian Holy Spirit with "Divine Fire" and aligning Hebrew names for God (Hu, Ehieh, Esth) with Pythagorean and Classical triads.</summary>

# SECOND BOOK

->f iii<-

will be in comparison to the divine light within itself, how much more so is this world of ours, a partner to matter and dregs, which is called both the shadow of the higher world and a certain mere likeness. But I know not what Sidonius <note>Likely a reference to a contemporary or classical interlocutor in the dialogue</note> whispered in my ear concerning *Hochma* <note>original: "Hochma"; Hebrew for Wisdom (Chokhmah)</note>, as if it were a name among the consecrated titles of the Essence. I confess indeed that since nothing "happens" to God <note>i.e., God has no "accidents" or temporary qualities</note>, nor does anything foreign cohere to Him. For nothing is simpler or more "One." Yet daily with David we cry out to God: *You have made all things in wisdom.* Therefore, before all things that were made, Wisdom was with God. If she were not outside of God—for otherwise she would by no means have been before every creature—she was therefore the essence of God. For we showed above that the word for "essence" is the same; wherefore Sidonius, being moved as I myself conjecture, feared lest among those things which are *Ousias* <note>original: "Vsias"; Greek for Essences/Substances</note>, I might through some negligent sluggishness omit *Hochma*, that is, Wisdom. But when he meditates within his own mind that God is nothing but His own essence, it will not be difficult to know in what manner we distribute the names signifying God, namely according to human custom; for we know the supreme and most excellent nature of divinity improperly and imperfectly, and for that reason we also name it imperfectly. 

But if any names have been handed down to us by divine means, we arrange them according to the capacity of our understanding, so that we think some signify God according to Himself—stripped of all garments, naked, and separated from individual applications—just as we see ourselves "happen" to other things. Such is when He is that super-substantial fire; when He is Being itself, embracing uniquely and eternally the past, present, and future, bound together into one sea of eternity by a unifying bond; and even when He is the "Same" itself, mixed with no proportion of "otherness." These three designate a certain pure nature of substance, even according to our mortal and weak knowledge of things. 

Wisdom, however, is of such a kind as we perceive "happening" to men, wherefore we call one man wiser than another. We similarly attribute this to God, and its name is *Hochma*; although among the sacred names signifying God it is judged of no small efficacy, nevertheless it is permitted to name God not at all *Katauton* <note>original: "Katauton"; Greek for "according to itself"</note>, that is, according to Himself, but *Periauton* <note>original: "Periauton"; Greek for "around itself"</note>, that is, concerning those things around Him. The evidence for this is the sacred utterances: *That which is foolish of God is wiser than men.* Thus it is that Wisdom herself in Proverbs reveals this mystery concerning herself when she says: *The Lord possessed me in the beginning of his way, before he made anything from the start. From eternity I was poured out from the head before the earth. The depths were not yet when I was conceived, nor yet the springs heavy with water. Before the mountains were established, before the hills, I was conceived.* 

Later it follows: *And I was "Ehieh" <note>original: "Ehieh"; Hebrew for "I Am" or "I Will Be"</note> according to itself the craftsman, and "Ehieh" a daily delight.* Which you read more elegantly thus: *I was with him composing all things, and I was delighted through every single day.* Now, with the veil removed, you certainly discern that the Wisdom of God is a certain emanation; although produced before the constitution of the world, it nevertheless arose from the "head" of the supreme God by the contemplation of future things, through which all things were made, and without her nothing was made—as if she had been a concomitant habit and industry of *Ehieh* itself, that is, of the First Being, whom he here calls the craftsman. I speak in a human manner on account of our weakness. For in a cause of this kind...

He uses the Book of Proverbs to illustrate Wisdom's role as a craftsman present before the creation of the physical world.</summary>

# ON THE WONDER-WORKING WORD
### iii 1
<meta>catchword: bri</meta>

We sometimes understand things that we are unable to utter. Indeed, I judge that wisdom to have been a living substance and an energy working according to the will of the Creator, providing for the entire universe. And although it is a holy opinion that this was God himself, nevertheless my mind compels me to understand there a different property of God than the naked immanence of his very self. Therefore, I believe <term>Hochma</term> <note>Wisdom; the second Sephirah</note> is among those things that accompany God’s hidden seclusion within himself, just as the strength of God—which, together with the wisdom mentioned a moment ago, contributed not a little to the machine of the world—according to the testimony of Jeremiah concerning the Creator, "who made the earth in his strength, and prepared the world in his wisdom, and in <term>Binah</term> <note>Understanding; the third Sephirah</note>, that is, in his understanding or providence, stretched out the heavens." Here again you have a religious name which the Hebrews place after the Crown and Wisdom among the ten numerations attributed to God—not as he is uncommunicated, but as he is radiating; not enclosed, but appearing; not abstract, but proceeding forth. 

The first of these is the inaccessible abyss of divinity, the sea of majesty, and the infinite power over all things, both those that are and those that are not. No contradiction shall escape this, no entanglement prohibits it, no dialectical reasoning of philosophy governs it, nor do the perplexities of the sophists or the phantasms of logic; finally, this <term>Kether</term> <note>Crown; the first Sephirah</note>, a diadem hanging entirely from the divinity, penetrates through all broken barriers. They call its name the Crown. 

There follow yet nine garments of God; as often as the very mind of the blessed touches their fringes, so often God responds: "Someone has touched me, for I too felt power go out from me." For these are not so much the enjoyments of the nine beatitudes as they are the objects of those enjoyments, which finally meet with the minds of the blessed in the nine hierarchies and allow for each a perpetual enjoyment of happiness. But if they should take up that first numeration back into themselves, they will correspondingly fulfill the end of the ten words written by the finger of God. 

Regarding *Hochma* and *Binah*, Solomon says in the Proverbs: "Blessed is the man who has attained wisdom and who abounds in understanding." And in Job, Elihu the son of Barachel answered: "I said, days shall speak and a multitude of years shall know wisdom, but certainly it is the spirit in men, and the inspiration of the Almighty God gives them understanding." 

Regarding <term>Nezah</term> <note>Victory or Endurance; the seventh Sephirah</note>, the prophet Samuel wrote in his book, which you call the First Book of Kings: "The Triumphant One in Israel will not spare, and will not be turned by repentance. For he is not a man that he should repent." After this divine name, King David, giving thanks to God for the costs collected for the future temple, placed another near the end of the first book of the *Words of the Days* <note>original: "verborum dierum"; the Hebrew title for Chronicles</note>, when he said: "Thine, O Lord, is the magnificence, and the power, and the glory, and the victory, and <term>Hod</term> <note>Splendor or Majesty; the eighth Sephirah</note>," which they translate variously as praise or confession. The hundred and third Psalm <note>Psalm 104 in modern numbering</note> will manifest this very garment of divinity, in which God is extolled with these words: "Thou hast put on *Hod* and beauty, clothed with light as with a garment." 

Following this confession of praise and glory is another holy and venerable name, a name renowned and full of heraldry, which is placed near the middle among the ten <term>Sephiroth</term> <note>The ten emanations or attributes of God in Kabbalah</note> like the heart in a living being: <term>Tiphereth</term> <note>Beauty or Adornment; the sixth Sephirah</note>. For so it is in the book of the *Words of the Days* already cited, which they call the *Book of Things Left Out* <note>original: "Paralipomenon"; the Greek name for Chronicles</note>. Its book begins...
