# BOOK TWO

<margin>f iiii</margin>

is said in the song noted in the book <note>referring to the Book of Chronicles</note>: "Now therefore, our God, we thank thee, and praise thy glorious name <term>Tiphereth</term> <note>Beauty or Glory</note>." Psalm 95 treats both of these: "<term>Hod</term> <note>Splendor</note> and beauty are in his sight: strength and <term>Tiphereth</term> <note>Beauty</note> are in his sanctuary." You may contemplate the power of this name in Psalm 88, when it is said: "For thou art the <term>Tiphereth</term> <note>Glory</note> of their strength and valor, O God." And in Isaiah 63: "So didst thou lead thy people, to make thyself a name of <term>Tiphereth</term> <note>Glory</note>." But truly, after all these things, let us speak of the august girdle of the most high God, which he himself is said to have used, together with other garments worthy of a prince, in the ordering of the world. For "girdle" is also a name for an ornament in civil law. Concerning this, it is sung in Psalm 64: "Hear us, O God our savior, the hope of all the ends of the earth and in the sea afar off; preparing the mountains in thy strength, being girded with <term>Geburah</term> <note>Power or Might</note>." When, therefore, such majesty comes forth from its hiding place, adorned with a crown, wisdom, understanding, victory, proclamation, and beauty—each of which he girds upon himself by his own power—what else is this than a king proceeding into his kingdom? For God is our king before the ages; he is the Lord of hosts and the King of glory, a great King above all gods, King of all the earth; he shall reign forever and beyond. Wherefore arises that consecrated name <term>Malchuth</term> <note>Kingdom</note>, which regards so illustrious a kingdom, so luminous an empire, of whose vast circuit in the eternal course there shall be no end. Concerning which it is written: "Thy kingdom is a kingdom of all ages." There follow two insignia of the king, the scepter and the sword, that is, mercy and fear. The name of the former is <term>Hesed</term> <note>Loving-kindness or Mercy</note>, which the royal prophet <note>King David</note> remembers, saying: "Lord, thy mercy is in heaven." And not only this, but even above the heavens, as in Psalm 107. And Moses, in that divine invocation of the highest Creator among the most sacred adjurations, did not hesitate to bring forth even this name devoted to holiness, saying: "Lord <term>Tetragrammaton</term> <note>The four-letter name of God, YHWH</note>, God merciful and gracious, patient and of much mercy," which is <term>Hesed</term>. Finally, there is added the principle of divine worship and perfect religion, which indeed, if we rightly examine the individual properties of contemplations as far as the outflow of majesty itself, follows last toward us. But when we direct our gaze from ourselves toward the clothing and ornament of the supreme deity, surely not without justice—on account of the reversal of the path—it occupies the first seats and is called <term>Pahad</term> <note>Awe or Dread</note>, that is, fear. Whence that trivial saying is not inelegantly sung by the common people: "Fear first made gods in the world." And there are some, and those noble grammarians, who derived "gods" <note>Latin: *deos*</note> from "fear" <note>Greek: *phobos*</note>. For *phobos* is fear in Greek. However, this is not that fear by which the guilty dread torture. For that is worthy of furies and harmful demons and adverse spirits <note>original: *manibus*</note>, of whom the inhabitants of the lower world complain in Virgil: "Each of us suffers his own spirit." That is not the divine and saving fear, nor does it proceed from God, but it is a madness and the worm of a guilty conscience, which daily gnaws at and burns the evil mind, the evil soul. Which Marcus Tullius Cicero, according to his custom, discusses excellently and copiously against Piso. "Do not," he says, "think, O Conscript Fathers, that you see as on the stage men of crime terrified by the impulse of the gods with the burning torches of the Furies; rather, it is his own fraud, his own deed, his own crime..."

It concludes by distinguishing between holy "Fear" (Pahad) and the psychological torment of a guilty conscience as described by Virgil and Cicero.</summary>

# ON THE WONDER-WORKING WORD

...[the devi]l, by his audacity, casts one down from health and sanity of mind. These are the furies of the wicked, these are the flames, these are the torches. However, *Pahad* <note>Hebrew: "Dread" or "Fear," often associated with the divine attribute of Justice/Severity</note> is an internal fear, more excellent than love. Thus it is that a certain most noble philosopher of our age seemed to me to have rightly said that just as external fear is inferior to love, so internal fear is superior to love. You yourselves surely understand this to be true without any teacher; how much better will you recognize that such a holy, such a worshipful, such a venerable name was breathed into men by the divine spirit, not only as something consecrated but as divine, as effective, and as the firmest sacrament of religion. If you doubt, read the monuments of secrets, read the holy scriptures written concerning the patriarch Jacob. For Jacob swore by the *Pahad* <note>original: "Pahad"</note> of his father *Isaac* <note>original: "Ishac"</note>, which is interpreted as "Fear." And he said to his father-in-law: "Unless the God of my father Abraham and the Fear of Isaac had been with me, you would have sent me away empty-handed." You have heard briefly what the most observable names of unutterable consistency are; finally, the ten emanations <term>Sephirot: the ten attributes or channels in Kabbalah through which the Divine is revealed</term> derived both within themselves and outwardly, and their praise, honors, glory, dignities, and sacred titles, signifying the mode of the disposition of the divine essence, and chosen from the most sacred letters themselves. For us, therefore, now with such majesty, the whole *Elohim* <note>A Hebrew name for God, grammatically plural but used as singular to denote the fullness of divine power</note> has gone forth into the universe of things, clothed in so many ornaments, so many garments, and for that reason, though one, is named "Gods" by the plural word *Elohim*. But when we were treating the words of essence, it had to be briefly commemorated what the truth holds: that no name unfolded by us from those same three—insofar as it designates the sole and proper essence of God—neglects created things. Rather, it invests and names any creature whatever that depends on the Highest with its own ornament, beauty, surname, and appellation. And to begin from the highest, which is "the same itself": whatever is contained by itself and its own limits, whatever has obtained a proper nature, an uncommunicated place, a peculiar dimension, whatever has obtained a distinguished mark of difference and a separate condition of status, is said to be nothing else but "the same itself." Concerning that which "is," however, we see how great its communication is into individual created things. For not only that which rests upon its own substance, but even that which adheres to another, strives to usurp the title of "being," so that finally all things whatsoever that depend on the First are said to "be." But what shall we say of fire, since nothing is said to be anywhere that lacks its spark, to such an extent that Heraclitus taught that all things were born from fire? Wherefore, to our intellect also, the distinguished, adorable, and most excellent name of the supersubstantial deity offers itself: the Unnamable, by which—besides the one, only, first God—nothing else can be named. It is called among the Greeks the *Ineffable Four-Lettered Name* <note>original: "Tetragrammaton Anechphoniton"</note>. Since this name works all things, it names none of its works after itself, calls nothing, affects nothing with its own name. For the interpreters themselves, finding no word equal to it, called it "Lord" at least on account of its supereminence. Not because the letters produce this sound—since they form no syllable—but signifying the most simple, they refuse to be compounded; and existing as images of the incomprehensible voice, the individual letters are vowels: *Ihuh* <note>The Tetragrammaton YHWH, here rendered with vowels to emphasize its breath-like nature</note>. This the Chaldeans <note>Often used in this period to refer to practitioners of ancient Near Eastern or mystical wisdom</note> are accustomed to depict with only four points duly ordered, lest the characters of so holy a...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the divine names from Book One, focusing specifically on the history and reverent handling of the four-letter name of God (the Tetragrammaton) among the Hebrews and early patriarchs.</meta>

# SECOND BOOK

...things written by the finger of God might become cheapened by daily mortal use. The Hebrews have now likewise adopted this custom, especially when they attempt to write on paper. At such times, they either do not complete the four letters, or they introduce a Chaldean <note>A reference to Aramaic or Babylonian script styles</note> shape. Furthermore, there are those who use only three points connected by a semicircle, and others in different ways. All of this was invented out of reverence for so great a name, lest the mystery uttered by the divine word of such great majesty be held in contempt by frequent handling.

Now, at last, you have received the most secret seals of all mysteries: the Name Tetragrammaton <note>The four-letter name of God, YHWH</note>, never discovered by man but handed down by the one God. A name holy and to be honored. The one name by which God alone is to be blessed according to ancestral religion. A most powerful name which those above worship, those below observe, and the very nature of the universe kisses; which, when absorbed by constant worshippers and fed into priestly minds, is said to grant miraculous powers to human faculty. This name was known to the first parents of our race by divine inspiration along with the breath of life. For concerning her firstborn, Eve said: *I have possessed a man through the Tetragrammaton* <note>original: "Possedi hominē per Tetragrammon." A translation of Genesis 4:1</note>. For so the holy characters contain, which neither is found to have uttered before.

The name was thereafter passed down into the good and fruitful generation of the first-formed. For when a most pleasant son was born to Seth himself (who was formed in the image and likeness of Adam), on that day the name Tetragrammaton began to be invoked. This was because previously an invisible race of men born from the lineage of Cain inhabited the earth, with whom God did not have much conversation. Seth, therefore, because he was a worshipper of divinity, rejoiced so greatly over his offspring that with his wife and family he invoked the name of God as taught by his parents.

And yet there are those who teach in this place that Enos, the grandson of Adam, was the very first to begin the calling upon the name Tetragrammaton. They are perhaps caught by an error of the scribes, not fearing what the Stagirite philosopher <note>Aristotle</note> warns in his *Sophistical Refutations*: that those unskilled in idioms are easily deceived. We see this happen to those who would rather be entangled for their whole lives in logical quibbles and spider webs than spend a few years on divine literature. For when Eusebius Jerome <note>St. Jerome, the translator of the Vulgate</note> translated it in this way: *But to Seth also a son was born whom he called Enos; Here [or "Then"] he began to invoke the name of the Lord,* some judged the part of speech "Hic" <note>Latin for "this man" or "here/at this time"</note> to be a pronoun, when it is actually an adverb of time to be pronounced with a circumflex accent. It signifies the same as "then." Just as in Maro <note>The poet Virgil</note>: *Here [Then] the almighty father, thrice bright from high heaven, thundered*—that is, *then* the almighty father thundered from heaven. Just as where a sign of place exists, by similar custom it sometimes signifies "when," as in: *where [when] the time shall be, I will wash all in the spring.*

Therefore, as soon as a son was born to Seth himself, *here* it began to be invoked—that is, *then* the name Tetragrammaton was called upon. But now it has come to such misuse that commonly, instead of the adverb "here," the pronoun "that man" is read, as if that man Enos first began to invoke the name of the Lord. Whether that be true, you yourselves understand both from the sacred scriptures and the Chaldean exposition. Subsequently, this most holy name, most worthy of worship, passed by a certain right of inheritance and perpetual succession, not through the reprobate generation of Cain, but through the diligent religious zeal of Seth...

<summary>This page discusses the reverent scribal habits regarding the Tetragrammaton and argues, through grammatical analysis of Jerome and Virgil, that the invocation of God's name began with the birth of Enos as a marker of time, rather than Enos being the sole inventor of the practice.</summary>
<keywords>Tetragrammaton, Seth, Enos, Cain, Jerome, Aristotle, Virgil, Hebrew linguistics, divine names, lineage of the Patriarchs</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the divine name from the previous section, focusing on its transmission from the first humans to the Patriarchs. The catchword "cies" from the previous page likely completed a word like "facies" (faces/appearances) or "species" (kinds).</meta>

# ON THE WONDER-WORKING WORD

Through devotion and constant faith toward God, and endowed with integrity of virtue, [the Name] descended through a continuous line of posterity all the way to Abraham. When he went into the land of Canaan, he built an altar to God and there invoked the name **Tetragrammaton** <note>original: "Tetragrammaton"; the four-letter Hebrew name of God, YHWH, considered too sacred to pronounce by many scholars of this period.</note>. To him, a little later, God appeared again, saying: "I am the **Tetragrammaton** <note>original: "Tetragrammus"</note> who led you out of Ur of the Chaldeans." His son Isaac, at the well of the oath, Beersheba, imitating his father, also established an altar to God and likewise invoked the name **Tetragrammaton**. 

Then Jacob, his son, when he had lain down in Haran, saw God leaning upon a ladder which, raised from the earth, touched heaven; God said to him in a dream: "I am the **Tetragrammaton**, the God of Abraham your father, and the God of Isaac." When Jacob awoke, he said: "Surely the **Tetragrammaton** is in this place." Later, when he wrestled with God in the camp during the night and asked for His name, God responded with a rebuke: "Why do you ask my name?" For He judged it superfluous for him to seek what he already possessed.

Then **CAPNION** <note>The scholar Johannes Reuchlin’s pseudonym in this dialogue.</note> said: "With your permission, I must interrupt this discussion. For you seem to be led by the momentum of your own copious speech through trackless paths and are almost being swept away. Do you not remember that God said to Moses concerning this name: 'I am the Lord who appeared to Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob as God Almighty <note>original: "in deo omnipotente"; referring to the Hebrew title El Shaddai.</note>, and my name **Tetragrammaton** I did not make known to them'? Yet you dare to teach us that they knew such a hidden name, as if it were an inheritance derived from the first mother of the human race. If that were so, the writers of the commentaries—great, grave, and distinguished theologians—would not have correctly handed down to us that it was first revealed to Moses alone, and was not known to the fathers before him; nor would it have been instituted by God, but rather devised by Eve and bequeathed to her descendants."

"You recite true things," said **BARUCHIAS**, "but you draw false conclusions. You must, in turn, grant me indulgence if, setting aside modesty, I use the custom of the logicians in speaking. Truly, that Eve pronounced this name is witnessed by the consecrated letters <note>referring to the Hebrew text of Genesis.</note>. But that she first devised it, or fashioned it, or instituted it—as if it were a human invention and a new beginning—I shall by no means agree with you. And if you remember, I removed that possibility by saying that, in my opinion, it was breathed into our first parents along with the breath of life and known through divine inspiration. This is thought to be greatly confirmed by that most noble herald of divine secrets, Isaiah, when he says: 'You, O **Tetragrammaton**, are our father, our redeemer; your name is from the ages, or from eternity.' Therefore, before the time in which our earthly fathers were created, that name existed. 

As for the words of God you bring forward, where He says He appeared to the ancestors in a previous age as 'God Almighty' and did not indicate the name **Tetragrammaton** to them, this is worthy of investigation so that I may untie this knot. Let us, therefore, make a trial of the matter. Who is there who, hearing Ezekiel speaking of precious stones, does not remember having seen one of them? And what wealthy merchant is there to whom the Sardius, Topaz, Jasper, Chrysolite, Onyx, Beryl, Sapphire, Carbuncle, Emerald—or some of these, or many, or all, or even those which Moses added: the Ligure, Agate, and Amethyst—were never known? Likewise, who is there who does not recognize many and various species from the lilies of the field, the flowers of the plain, and the herbs of the gardens..."

<summary>This page features a dialogue between Capnion and Baruchias regarding whether the sacred four-letter name of God (Tetragrammaton) was known to the Patriarchs before Moses. Baruchias argues that the name is eternal and was divinely revealed to the first humans, rather than being a later invention or a secret kept until the time of the Exodus.</summary>
<keywords>Tetragrammaton, Abraham, Isaac, Jacob, Moses, Eve, Isaiah, Ezekiel, precious stones, divine revelation, sacred names, Johannes Reuchlin</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from Book Two regarding the distinction between knowing a thing's name and knowing its true power or essence, specifically leading into the divine name of God.</meta>

# BOOK TWO

...he has noted their appearances along with their names and shapes: *Here is the potherb, here the beets spreading their arms wide,* as the poet says, *and the fertile sorrel, the mallows, and the green violets; here the chickpea and the leeks that owe their name to the head.* <note>The author is quoting Virgil or a similar classical poet; the "head" refers to the Latin *caput* from which *capitatus* (headed) leeks derive.</note> *Here also the poppy, cool and harmful to the brain, and lettuce, the pleasant rest after noble meals, and the heavy gourd hanging down with its wide belly.* All these things even we, the common sort of the flock, recognize; but only distinguished and extraordinary men know, besides the names, the virtues and powers of each, which either some learned natural philosopher or an experienced practitioner of medicine <term>Empiricus: a physician who relies on practical experience rather than just theory</term> has pointed out. Therefore, even a rustic man will not be lying if he says he knows Coral and Emerald, provided some merchant has shown him the method of recognizing them by color, shape, and price. For he knows that Coral is not Emerald, nor vice versa. But he who professes to know these little stones truly must be thoroughly learned in their powers and operations—what sort of thing each is and how great—and that an Emerald hung around the neck wards off the occasion of epilepsy, and Coral repels stomach pain, as well as the certain law for hanging them and other things agreeable to that craft. Therefore, whoever has not been instructed in such things, whoever he may be, even if he has named and selected a gem, I would rightly say (as I teach) that I have not yet truly shown him the gem itself, nor the herb, nor any other thing. For I have not yet shown him its property, its power, and its operations.

In this way, God spoke to Moses concerning this Name, because He appeared to the fathers as God Almighty. For in His omnipotence in former years, God Himself performed miracles. But when the time for the liberation of Israel arrived, He kindly wished to delegate His divine and wondrous power to men through the hidden virtue of this name, the <term>Tetragrammaton: the four-letter Hebrew name of God, YHWH</term>, which was never before revealed to any mortal. For long ago, by a singular covenant, He enclosed within that word a majesty of energy and wondrous power which lay hidden from the patriarchs and our ancestors until the time of Moses. 

There are, therefore, three things: there is the name, there is the meaning, and there is the divine covenant or confederation. The name lifts the human mind. The meaning clings to the lifted mind. Our ancestors knew these two—both the name and the meaning—and therefore they called upon God in that same name, and He was favorable to them in their lives. But the third thing, which is much greater, in which is contained the surpassing power of the word delegated to man by the Most High God, was shown to Moses and not to the fathers. This is weighed more clearly from the Hebrew speech. For where we read "I have indicated," the Hebrew letter contains *Nodaethi* <note>original: "Nodaethi" (נודעתי), meaning "I made myself known"</note>, that is, "I have caused to be known." For in the former age, God did not cause His name, the Tetragrammaton, to be known and understood as to the great wonders that lie deep within, entrusted to human frailty; rather, He caused it to be recognized, worshipped, and observed generally, like the other divine names. 

But now, God has set this name forward and placed it at the summit above other names as wonderful, as great, as terrible, and as known through the miracles performed by God through man. Wherefore Isaiah says: *Tetragrammaton, you are my God; I will exalt you, I will confess your name, for you have done wonderful things.* <note>A translation of Isaiah 25:1, substituting the Tetragrammaton for "Lord".</note> This is what it means to know the name Tetragrammaton: when not only the characters and the word do not escape us, but also its pronunciation (which is hidden) and its concealed virtue...

<summary>The author argues that true knowledge of a thing—whether a plant, a gem like emerald, or the divine name—requires understanding its hidden powers and "virtues" rather than just its name. He explains that while the Patriarchs knew God's name, Moses was the first to be granted the "energy" and miraculous power delegated through the Tetragrammaton.</summary>

<keywords>Tetragrammaton, Moses, Isaiah, Emerald, Coral, Nodaethi, miracles, divine power, herbalism, lapidary, Hebrew names of God</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Wonder-Working Word" (Verbum Mirificum), specifically focusing on the divine name revealed to Moses and the patriarchs as a source of miraculous power and a sign of God's covenant.</meta>

# ON THE WONDER-WORKING WORD

...and the following signs of miracles, all of which will become manifest both from the fourth chapter of Exodus and from the later speech of God himself. "I," says the Lord, "will lead you out of the workhouse <note>original: *ergaſtulo*; referring to the forced labor of the Israelites</note> of the Egyptians, and I will rescue you from slavery, and I will redeem you with an outstretched arm and with great judgments; and I will take you to myself as a people, and I will be your God, and you shall know that I am the *Tetragrammaton* <note>The four-letter Hebrew name of God, YHWH, often considered too holy to pronounce</note> your God." You shall know now the *Tetragrammaton*. By what teaching? Because "in an outstretched arm"—behold the miracles. This is the knowledge which the Peripatetics <note>Followers of Aristotle's philosophy</note> call "posterior knowledge from a sign," from which faith arises. You shall know, but by what capacity of our fragility? Because "in great judgments." For judgment belongs to God, the sentence of God, which the entire nation must obey without any preparation—behold hope. You shall know, but what will be the manner of knowing? Surely the covenant of God with man, because "I will take you to myself as a people, and I will be your God"—behold love and affection on both sides. For there is need not only of a common obligation and bond, but we shall necessarily enter into a new covenant with God, based upon the *Tetragrammaton* word, if we desire its power to work within us. For the Lord of the universe spoke to Moses concerning the common people of the Jews before this very covenant of future society: "Seeing, I have seen the affliction of my people who are in Egypt." Therefore, they had been adopted into the people of God beforehand. And what else is it that he now says, "I will take you to myself as a people," except this pact, this new fellowship which contemplative men enter into at God's warning, and most recently strike a covenant upon the *Tetragrammaton*? This is what the messenger of the *Tetragrammaton* replied to Manoah <note>The father of Samson (Judges 13)</note> when he asked about the name: "Why do you ask my name?" But "it is wonderful" <note>original: *mirificum*</note>; by which answer he had recognized the name, and upon it he sacrificed a young goat, which among the ancients was a sign of a covenant, just as you also read in Homer. Wherefore it follows: "And he placed the offering upon the rock, offering it to the *Tetragrammaton*, who performs wonders." From this arose what Manoah said to his wife: "We shall surely die, because we have seen the *Tetragrammaton*." He would not have feared to see angels, whose frequent conversation with our ancestors was within the memory of our fathers. But because he had understood the voice of God through Moses saying, "No man shall see me and live," he therefore dreaded that he had seen God until he was comforted. To the Patriarch Jacob, the name is not disclosed even when he asks, but only this is replied: "Why do you ask my name?" and it is not added "Which is wonderful." But to this man [Manoah], because he had seen the miracle of the burnt offering, the answer is given concerning the name which is "wonder-working" <note>original: *mirificum*</note>, by which it could be known that it was the *Tetragrammaton*. Although you read in that passage of Judges, "My name, which is wonderful" <note>original: *mirabile*</note>, it does not hold the same meaning here as in the eighth Psalm: "O Lord, our Lord, how wonderful <note>original: *admirabile*</note> is your name in all the earth," that is, worthy of admiration and glorious, which the word *Adir* <note>Hebrew: mighty/majestic</note> signifies; but here the word is *Peli* <note>Hebrew: secret/wonderful/miraculous</note>, which means a worker of miracles. You have here the origin of such a great name, its form, its significance, and its power, which belongs to God alone, the Best and Greatest; which among all names is called "Great" by excellence. Just as Joshua spoke to God: "What will you do for your great name?" which in Deuteronomy is called "Terrible," and in the Psalms "Good," "honorable," "holy," "praiseworthy," "eternal," "beatific," and in...

<summary>This page explores the theological significance of the Tetragrammaton (the four-letter name of God) as a "wonder-working word" that establishes a covenant between God and humanity. The author distinguishes between different Hebrew terms for "wonderful" to emphasize God's role as a performer of miracles.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the divine names of God from Book One, moving into the specific mystical and linguistic properties of the Tetragrammaton as found in the Prophets and classical philosophy.</meta>

# SECOND BOOK
g

Isaiah calls it Excellent, ancient, and from eternity; and in Jeremiah, it is Invoked over us; and in Ezekiel, Gone out among the nations; and in Daniel, Blessed; and in Amos, the **Tetragrammaton** <note>The four-letter name of God, YHWH</note> is his name; and in Malachi, Great among the nations from the rising of the sun even to its setting, and awesome among the peoples. I shall deliberately omit, most learned men, the childish little grammatical quibbles in this so excellent a cause and so salutary a business, in which great theologians involve themselves with such zeal in this place that—it shames me to hear it—they even descend into empty contentions. 

There remains for me only that famous and most powerful pronunciation which is said to be most secretly hidden in the abyss of mysteries; if this name possesses it, it is not "ineffable" <note>Unspeakable or unutterable</note>; if it does not possess it, it is not a name. What then shall we do? Shall we deny it is a name, when we have brought forth such a great number of witnesses against our own doubt? Shall we deny it is ineffable, which all men, both Greeks and Latins, call it? Concerning which Solomon says in Proverbs: *Who has gathered the waters as if in a garment? Who has established all the ends of the earth? What is his name, and what is his son’s name, if you know?* <note>Proverbs 30:4</note> It is as if he were to say: "Speak the name itself if you know it, and utter that which is unutterable," as if he considers it difficult for us to hold the explanation of that pronunciation. 

I shall cite your Sidonian Hermes, that **Trismegistus** <note>Hermes "Thrice-Greatest," a legendary Hellenistic figure associated with wisdom and magic</note> (that is, "thrice greatest") in the *Dialogue of Asclepius*. "For I do not hope," he says, "that the creator of all majesty and the father or lord of all things can be called by a single name, however much it might be composed of many." But the God of our fathers—not of the profane, nor of the poets, nor of the philosophers, nor of the heathens, but the God of Abraham, the God of Isaac, the God of Jacob—imposed a proper name upon his own nature, which is otherwise unknown and unnamable. He is likewise the institutor and teacher of the "impossible pronunciation." He taught Moses to utter the unutterable name when he was complaining about the clumsiness of his tongue. 

By now, that pronunciation is known to you from that passage, which is contained within three clauses under a single period. I would not dare to step beyond the boundaries of the mysteries, lest some unworthy person standing by the wall should overhear. Since indeed a name, as Plato says in the *Cratylus*, is an imitation of the essence itself, and that happens for us just as it does for painters or musicians through a manifold mixing and various distinction until we apprehend that which we wish to imitate—as if we desire to look into a noble structure with sharp investigation. First, we resolve its individual parts, then we build it up again as it was first constructed, and finally, we preserve the framework itself in the mind. 

Now, that distinction or resolution of names is made into syllables, and of syllables into letters, and of letters into properties and accidents. Then, when the letters are entwined again, the combination of the word expresses the thing. Therefore, our ancestors, relying on divine zeal and heavenly light, attained in like manner the most full exposition of the graces of this name and deigned to hand it down to us, their descendants, which you call the **Shem HaMephorash** <note>original: "Semhammaphoras"; the "Explicit Name" of God, often associated with 72 names derived from Exodus</note>. I have known many who certainly relied on this foundation of secrets to attempt things which nature itself would not have been able to do. But lest you also lack this very thing, I will fulfill it in a few words. The Lord of four letters leaned upon the top of the ladder rising from the earth and touching the heavens with its summit; Jacob lies upon the ground, resting in sleep. The angels of God ascend first...

He introduces the concept of the Shem HaMephorash as a linguistic key to divine power.</summary>

# ON THE WONDER-WORKING WORD
<note>original: "DE VERBO MIRIFICO"</note>

...they descend afterward. To Jacob himself the promise is brought; he awakens, affirms that the Lord is in that place, trembles and fears, sacrifices at an erected altar, and vows a vow. Let us complete the ladder with its steps, for in the Song of the sons of Korah it is said: *According to your name, O God, so is your praise.* Therefore, the *Schemhammaphoras* <note>The "Explicit Name" of God, a kabbalistic term for the 72-fold name derived from Exodus</note> shall be the praise of God; thus the steps of the ladder are divided according to His praises. Now, Hebrew praises are called *Thillim* <note>Tehillim, the Hebrew title for the Book of Psalms</note>, by which name the Book of Psalms is inscribed. The *Schemhammaphoras* will therefore be the revealed exposition of the divine name from the Book of Psalms. Each verse marks one step of the ladder, and every one of them contains three sacred characters. From these, by the suggestion of the Spirit of God, one distinguished seal is forged—to be worshipped and observed—formed of three letters, within which they say is included an angel of God bearing with him the majesty of the divine name. These are the 72 angels who ascend and descend. Into their powers the august name of the Divinity is resolved, and by those same angelic syllables, it is finally restored. It is not that the name ceases to exist when resolved, nor begins to exist again when restored, but it is like the sun in the multiplication of its rays and poured-out light. For as the most contemplative of the Greek philosophers says: *What the sun is to sensible things, God is to intelligible things.* This is the Psalmody of the seventy-two verses, which I shall pronounce in English <note>original: "latine" (Latin); the speaker is addressing a Latin-speaking audience, here translated for the reader</note> according to the custom of your Church, and I will point out each holy seal with my finger; therefore, pay attention most diligently. Where the name of four letters—that is, the *Tetragrammaton* <note>The four-letter name of God, YHWH</note>—is placed, I shall there, after the manner of the ancients, borrow the interpretation of *Adonai* <note>The Hebrew word for "Lord," used as a substitute for the unspoken Tetragrammaton</note> itself.

# ON THE WONDERFUL WORD

->Serve the Lord in gladness; enter into his presence with exultation.<-

<note>Psalm 37:4</note>

->I will confess to the Lord greatly with my mouth, and in the midst of many I will praise him.<-
<note>Psalm 109:30</note>
->Return, O my soul, into your rest, for the Lord has dealt bountifully with you.<-
<note>Psalm 116:7</note>

Do not let this seemingly indiscriminate collection of verses disturb you or lead you into amazement, as if it stood without order and by chance, consisting of human will and clever artifice rather than divine fate. For what both Plutarch and other students of Homer write concerning his poems—that they were not composed in a numbered sequence at the start as they are now, but were scattered and pulled apart, and finally reduced to order by the grammarians who lived around the time of Pisistratus and Aristarchus—our people, and even your most learned men of Holy Scripture, say something not unlike this regarding the hymns of the Psalms. For instance, Hilary, the leader and bishop, says in his commentaries on that book: "It must not be ignored that among the Hebrews the numbering of the Psalms is indistinct, and they were written without any notation of order. For there, they are not pre-marked as first, second, third, fiftieth, or hundredth, but are mixed together into one without any prescription of order. For Ezra, as ancient traditions report, collected them into one volume when they were unarranged and dispersed according to the diversity of authors and times." 

Since, therefore, that tradition of the <term>Shem HaMephorash</term><note>The "Explicit Name" of God in Jewish mysticism, often associated with 72 names derived from Exodus</note> existed before the seventy-two translators <note>The Septuagint</note>—who are said to have established the number and order of the Psalms—and indeed existed long before, you can certainly judge that it is not without reason that these verses, conceived by divine inspiration, do not serve human number and order. Rather, they are subject to a divine, proper, and unique number—innumerably numerable and infinitely finite—from which they finally proceeded. They are referred back again to the numbers of that same adorable name, the <term>Tetragrammaton</term><note>The four-letter name of God, YHWH</note>, in a manner known only to the wise.

Then **SIDONIUS** said: You seem to me to have pointed out clearly with your finger exactly what you promised. But I do not quite understand what you intend the number of that Name to be, to which the seventy-two are referred, unless it is the "four-ness" of letters of which that so-celebrated Name is said to consist, and for which reason it is called the Tetragrammaton. I certainly find that the most ancient philosophers and princes of philosophy always valued this more highly than the other secrets of any religion whatsoever. This is especially true of Pythagoras himself and the noble Pythagoreans, who likewise call that which you name the Tetragrammaton the <term>Tetractys</term><note>A triangular figure of ten points arranged in four rows, sacred to Pythagoreans</note> according to the custom of their discipline. They prefer this to all gods and virtues of the gods, and describe it as being the fountain of eternal nature flowing into our souls. A witness to this is the poem they call the "Golden Verses of Pythagoras," in which the Tetractys is used in place of an oath, as the highest thing upon which faith and the credibility of matters rests, since...

<meta>catchword: since</meta>

<summary>This page discusses the mystical arrangement of the Psalms and links the Hebrew Tetragrammaton (the four-letter name of God) to the Pythagorean Tetractys, arguing for a divine mathematical order underlying sacred texts.</summary>

--- Page 82 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the sacred nature of the number four (the Quaternary) and its relationship to the divine name and the structure of the universe.</meta>

->BOOK TWO<-

...by the perfect definition of its own substance. The most excellent poets have imitated this subtle and sharp kind of contemplation—as they have with the rest of his philosophy—distinguishing the chaos of the world into four elements; the Heavens into four parts of the year; the Air into the East, West, South, and North winds; the Earth into four spaces according to five zones; Divinity into the common, the noble, the celestial, and the royal palaces; and Humanity into flesh, spirit, the shades, and the ghost. We read of these partly at the beginning of Ovid’s *Metamorphoses*, and partly in those verses which are widely circulated:

->Four things there are of man: the shades, the flesh, the spirit, the ghost,<-
->These four places receive those four parts.<-
->The earth covers the flesh, the ghost flits around the tomb,<-
->The underworld holds the shades, the spirit seeks the stars.<-
<note>original: "Bis duo sunt hominis, manes, caro, spiritus, umbra..." This mnemonic verse was common in Renaissance Neoplatonism to describe the components of the human soul and body.</note>

The Stoics <term>Stoics: ancient philosophers who emphasized virtue and natural law</term> praise the splendor of our life based on the four virtues. Mathematicians find the first solid demonstration in the quaternary. Natural philosophers divide the judicial powers into four: intellect, reason, opinion, and sense. Arithmeticians call "One" a potential square <term>tetragon: a four-sided figure or square</term>. For when "One" breaks out into action, it is a square number. It is the first to fulfill the entire difference of numbers, and perfectly completes the "Ten" <note>the Decad</note> under four terms. For one, two, three, and four give birth to the substance of the number ten; because of this, I might more easily believe those ten *Sephiroth* <term>Sephiroth: the ten attributes or emanations in Jewish Kabbalah</term> which you dared to introduce after the name *Tetragrammaton* <note>The four-letter name of God, YHWH</note> (even though it is a thing unfamiliar to the common people).

What more? That same number, within those same four limits, contains all the harmony of music; indeed, it gathers together all of mathematics: the point, the line, the surface, and the body. It embraces all of physics, in which the power of nature’s seeds is taught first, second the natural sprouting, third the maturing form, and fourth the composite thing. Finally, it suggests the whole of metaphysics: essence, being, power, and action. This triplicity surely unfolds a marvelous quaternity, so that in the beginning there is the point, the seed, the essence. Second: the line, the sprouting, the being. Third: the surface, the form, the power. Fourth: the body, the composite, the action. These seem to me to be the twelve stones which you recited by their names before Baruchia, and which your Moses reduced into a quaternary order.

Therefore, in the entire universe of the world and in its individual parts, that divine quaternity shines back. In the supernal realms (as it is in Orphic theology), the Muse, Dionysus, Apollo, and Venus—those heavenly deities—instill deifying attractions into our minds: the Muse inspires poetry, Dionysus mystery, Apollo prophecy, and Venus love. In the celestial realms, there are four triplicities. Below heaven, four seasons; below time, four qualities; below qualities, the four elements. Below the elements: substance, quality, quantity, and motion. Finally, under substance itself: the corporeal, the vegetable, the sensitive, and the intelligent. Under quantity: the point, length, width, and depth (just as we say of number: one, two, three, four). Under quality: hot, cold, dry, and moist. Under motion: ascending, descending, progressing, and circular.

We have spoken of things super-celestial, eternal, temporal, and mixed. Let us see in what way the human soul has allied itself with the quaternity. Indeed, our soul ascends through four steps to the ultimate spirit within itself: through sense, imagination, phantasy, and intelligence. You hold, therefore, the nature...

<summary>This page explores the universal significance of the number four (the Quaternary) across poetry, mathematics, music, and theology, linking it to the ten Sephiroth and the Tetragrammaton. It argues that the structure of the soul and the physical world are both governed by this four-fold divine pattern.</summary>
<keywords>Quaternary, Tetragrammaton, Sephiroth, Ovid, Stoics, Orphic theology, four elements, four virtues, sacred geometry, Kabbalah</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue on the mystical power of the "Mirific Word" (the Wonder-Working Word), focusing on the sacred nature of the number four and its relationship to the divine name.</meta>

# ON THE WONDER-WORKING WORD

For the whole universe consists of a fourfold nature <note>original: "quaternitate"</note>, which the Maker of all things would not have fashioned in such a way unless there were first some Idea of the "quaternion" within Himself before He turned toward the work of creation. Hence, the Lacedaemonians <note>Spartans</note> seem to me to have rightly depicted the highest Jupiter with four ears, since he himself is also the supreme quaternion—that is, according to the Pythagoreans, the *Tetractys* <note>The "four-fold" or "four-group," a mystical symbol of ten points arranged in a triangle representing the organization of the universe</note>. Its emanation is found through "form" in the angels, through "true participation" in rational souls, through "imagination" in those lacking reason, and through "shadow" in the shapes of bodies; it flows down from the heights to the depths in the accustomed manner of an eternal rain, watering all things.

"I listen quite gladly, Sidonius," said **BARUCHIAS**, "to those things which you apply to our own discipline. For as I see it, everything squares correctly with the rule. For I believe this to be that famous river which goes out from that celebrated place of pleasure <note>Eden</note> to water paradise with four heads. This is what Orpheus and Homer call the Ocean, the origin of divine and human things: first always existing, second flowing into the generation of the gods, third watering earthly things, and fourth trickling into all fluid nature. This is that river Chebar <note>original: "Cobar"</note> beside which Ezekiel, touched by the hand of God, saw the likeness of four living creatures, and four within four. In that same river were four cherubim on four wheels, and a wheel in the middle of a wheel. It is the same living creature that he had seen by the river Chebar. This very thing is the great sea in which, according to the vision of Daniel, the four winds of heaven fought, and four great beasts ascended from the sea. They wished its divinity to be called Neptune, who is said in the Orphic hymns to drive a four-yoked chariot."

Therefore, the mystery of the *Tetragrammaton* <note>The four-letter Hebrew name of God, YHWH</note> was known to the most ancient sages, and indeed even to foreigners; although it was not used publicly, it was nevertheless not unknown in private. These sages, handing down all the disciplines of the best things under a riddle <note>an enigma</note>, appointed certain most skillful craftsmen for themselves in this business, so that they might fittingly carve the whole universe of all things with a chisel <note>original: "celte"</note>, and shape it into a single image for our sight, lest there be frequent need for this supreme Name, which they feared would be cheapened by common use. Indeed, they called this a "sacred image," and its carvers were for that reason called *hieroglyphics*.

It was in this manner: First an eye, second a staff, third a shield, fourth a serpent. See how aptly these figures are visible even to the profane and the unrefined! But there exist numbers much simpler and likewise "formal" <note>pertaining to the essential form</note> which we contemplate more sharply than figures, and which we believe approach closer to divine things. For this reason, this very Name—so famous—stands out as much more desirable to us philosophers, because it embraces at once the geometric secrets in the shapes of the characters and the arithmetic secrets in the forms of the numbers.

So that I may perform the duty of a mathematician without distinction, I might most rightly say: *One, even, odd, the whole, reduction.* Nothing is prior to the infinite, nothing prior to the one, nothing prior to the point; and this is the principle of all things. Then, all things exist up to the one and up to the point, and beyond the one and the point, there is nothing. This, therefore, is the end of all things. You now hold the first letter of the *Tetragrammaton*, which both reflects the figure of a point, and is called *Yod*—that is, the "Beginning"—and designates the "ten"—that is, the end of number—and every number...

<summary>The text explores the mystical significance of the number four (the quaternion) across Greek, Egyptian, and Hebrew traditions, linking the Pythagorean Tetractys to the four-letter Name of God and the four rivers of Paradise.</summary>
<keywords>Tetragrammaton, Tetractys, Ezekiel, Chebar, Hieroglyphics, Yod, Numerology, Pythagoreanism, Four Elements</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical discussion of numerology and the divine, specifically focusing on the properties of the number one (Unity) and the transition to multiplicity.</meta>

# BOOK TWO

<note>Signature: g iiii</note>

...it penetrates most simply, because it bears the mark of unity. Unity, moreover, is the common measure of all numbers. And as the **Arithmeticians** <note>Mathematicians or number theorists</note> write, it holds the place of the point; since it is the beginning and the origin of length, it is itself capable of neither interval nor length. Just as a point placed upon a point results in nothing but a point—as if you joined nothing to nothing—so it happens that unity multiplied by itself produces nothing other than itself. For if you say "one times one," it is nothing but one; or again, "one times one" does not wander further, but remains in one. For it is always the same and immutable substance, the leader of the odd-numbered series, and in a certain way the maker and form of oddness. It is the beginning, the communication, and the end of all things, above number, above the whole, devoid of division, and lacking parts.

For this reason, it most conveniently demonstrates to our "sickly eyes" <note>A metaphor for limited human perception</note> the supreme author of this world, and describes the nature of divinity to human weakness—from which, by which, through which, in which, and toward which all things exist. Let us use, if you please, the testimony of the most divine theologian **Dionysius the Areopagite** <note>A 5th-6th century Christian philosopher who wrote on the "Divine Names"</note>, writing thus about being and exemplars: *For in the monad <note>original: "monade" - the unit</note> every number exists uniformly, and unity contains every number within itself uniquely; every number is indeed joined together in the monad. However, as much as it proceeds out of the monad, so much is it distinguished and multiplied.*

We have spoken of immanence; let us speak of procession. Just as the motion of a point creates a line, and the motion of a line creates a surface, so the motion of unity creates the "other," and "otherness" creates the number two <term>binary: the number two, representing the first step away from unity into duality</term>. For "the other," according to **Pythagoras** and the heirs of his wisdom, says **Boethius**, is ascribed to no other than the number two alone. Hence, on the authority of **Servius the Grammarian**, "the other" <note>original: "Alter"</note> is said only of two things, while the binary indicates a certain composition. For it is a heap of unities. Composition is not understood without the suspicion of material proportion. Material proportion cannot exist without formal proportion, just as in truth matter cannot exist without form. **Aristotle** rightly judges form to be something divine.

The odd number, and indeed the number three <note>the ternary</note> most of all, is asserted to be proper to divine matters. Not unlike this, **Alphesiboeus** sang in the *Pharmaceutria* of **Maro** <note>Virgil's Eclogues</note>: *God delights in an odd number.* Therefore, everything that first flows outside of God, as soon as it is no longer God itself, becomes "other" than Him and equal to the first multitude. Yet, so that it may nonetheless have being, it turns back again toward divinity, whose emblem is the odd number. For every procession (said **Abucaten** <note>Likely referring to the philosopher Avempace or a similar Arabic source</note>) is effected through the likeness of the secondary things to the first.

The angelic nature, therefore, is to be signified by an odd number: by a number, indeed, because it is a multitude progressed from the One; but by an odd number because it is closest to God and relies on participation in Him. For angels are like a small portion of God, such as the prophetic **Apollo** once answered to one inquiring at Colophon. Here clearly there is no lack of what the philosophers say they have experienced: for where a higher nature touches a lower one, there it reaches the highest point of the lower from its own lowest grade. But soon, when one comes to the summit—that is, to unity—it will necessarily be the opposite: that the highest of the lower aspires to the whole condition of unity, since that which is one and most simple has no grades. Moreover, the whole condition of unity is to be the same...

<summary>This page explores the metaphysical significance of the number one (Unity) as a symbol for God, explaining how multiplicity and the "angelic nature" emerge from this singular source through mathematical and philosophical analogies.</summary>
<keywords>Unity, Monad, Duality, Odd Numbers, Dionysius the Areopagite, Boethius, Aristotle, Angelic Nature, Numerology, Metaphysics</keywords>

# ON THE MIRACULOUS WORD
<meta>catchword: follows</meta>

...and an immutability that is always similar to itself and in agreement, in which only the odd number—as the **Mathematicians** <note>original: *Arithmetici*</note> say—most greatly participates. Indeed, any number whose middle is one is an odd number. But that which has two in its middle receives the quality of evenness and can be divided into two equal halves. Therefore, you see that oddness is more closely related to God, while evenness is more dissimilar; for this reason, the angelic, the ideal, or the entire nature of the archetypal world <note>The "archetypal world" refers to the divine blueprint or the world of ideas in Platonic thought.</note> should more rightly be designated not by an even, but by an odd number. 

And yet, since God alone admits no multitude within Himself, while all other things—however simple—are a kind of heap, as it were, and are constructed from act and potentiality. For this reason, the first stream of the Godhead ought not to be celebrated by some simple mark of oddness, but by one which consists of both an even and an odd number at once. Even, so that it might be something; odd, so that it might be only as much as that which had flowed out and that which would flow back. For "to be" is different from "that which is." 

Therefore, from the first **two** <note>the first even number</note> and the first **three** <note>the first odd number</note>, the **five** is established. This demonstrates the first ray produced from the supreme light into the intellectual world by a single sacred character: that is, the letter **He** <note>The second letter of the Hebrew Tetragrammaton, representing the number 5.</note> itself. The mathematicians have handed down that this is the first "uncomposed" number of counting, and the Hebrew grammarians say it designates the breath of pronunciation and the substance of essence <note>original: *essentiae hypostasim*; the letter He often functions as the definite article "the" in Hebrew, which the author links to defining existence.</note> by its function as an article. 

To my eyes, the most wise **Solomon** seems to have skillfully painted this for the unrefined and unlearned. For he made two **cherubim** above the Ark upon which the name of the Lord of Hosts might sit, so that one wing of a cherub was five cubits, and the other wing of the cherub was five cubits—as if an image of the super-celestial essence and nature just below God.

I wish also to briefly explain the third character of this ineffable and miraculous name, which they declare to be **Vau** <note>The third letter of the Tetragrammaton, representing the number 6.</note>. Although this is a copulative conjunction <note>In Hebrew, the letter *Vau* (or Vav) functions as the word "and."</note>, it nevertheless changes the tense of verbs in continuous speech, and in mathematics, it signifies the number six. Most aptly, therefore, it becomes the little mark of heaven and earth—that is, of all things joined together from matter and form. For **Vau** couples and joins the parts of speech, which is why it is called a conjunction. Furthermore, it even transforms the tenses in words, just as nature does in things. *What is that which has been?* (says **Ecclesiastes**) *The very thing that shall be. What is that which was done? The very thing that is to be done.* <note>A translation of Ecclesiastes 1:9.</note> Therefore, whatever bodily things exist, whether in heaven or on earth or situated in between them, are subject to the change of time. 

But let us pass to the **Pythagorean mysteries** and the secrets of sacred things. I have drawn from the volumes of the ancients that certain most skillful men of the Scythians or other nations were accustomed to carve the nature of the universe in a single image—and for that reason, they were called **hieroglyphs**—which contained an eye, a sword, a shield, and a serpent. According to these, surely after the eye (that is, the point) and the staff or sword (that is, the linear plane), there follows in equal order the hexagonal shield: that is, the solid body which, on account of length, width, and height, is enclosed by six angles. For it has "before" and "behind" in length; "left" and "right" in width; and "up" and "down" in height. For this number six is so established in the nature of solids that it always moves around every body in an inseparable union. Since there are therefore three intervals of solids, of which there are two motions for each, it is no wonder if after **Iod** (that is, one) and **He** (that is, two and three), **Vau** now follows in its own place...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the mystical properties of the Hebrew letters within the Divine Name (Tetragrammaton), specifically focusing on the third letter, Vau, and its numerical value of six.</meta>

# BOOK TWO

...follows, that is, six. This number arises from the multiplication of the preceding numbers, whether you say once two is two and then three times that, or conversely three times two; indeed, it is a number most fitting for individual material things, since it is born from the alternating multiplication of the preceding numbers. Moreover, in the act of multiplying things by one another in turn, matter itself provides the greatest occasion. I shall remain silent on the fact that it obtains its order here from the units of the preceding numbers. For from one, two, and three, the number six <note>original: "fenarius"</note> is gathered, so that by the best right you may recognize that after *Iod* and *He*, the letter *Vau* itself must immediately follow. Added to this is the fact that—just as the nature of the entire corporal universe is asserted to be perfect in every respect, even on the authority of Aristotle, neither abounding in superfluities nor frustrated by necessities—so the number six, providing the seal of the entire corporeal world, is so uniquely perfect in the whole context of numbers from the first unit up to ten, that in comparison to its parts it is neither destitute nor diminished by the poverty of want, nor superfluous by immoderate fullness. For it has a middle part, which is three; a third part, which is two; and a sixth part, which is one; which, when reduced into a single sum, produce the whole body of the number six. Other numbers certainly lack this perfection. For the half of eight is four, the fourth is two, and the eighth is one, the collection of which reduces to a sum smaller than the whole body, that is, seven. Thus we find in the rest that the sums of the parts piled together either abound or fall short of the stem of the number; only the number six is perfect in a totality of this kind. For this reason, not without cause, it alone designates this whole and perfect world.

This opinion of ours must be fortified by a rampart of Holy Scripture, lest you suspect a hollow fabrication or my own invention; and that which few notice, I shall reveal to you, my dearest companions. Truly, although many "generations" <note>referring to the Hebrew word *Toledot*, meaning lineages or histories</note> are accustomed to be recited in the Holy Scriptures, you will nowhere inspect any generations in the entire volume of Sacred Letters to which this character of ours, *Vau*, is added, except only then when we read of the generation of heaven and earth: *These are the generations of heaven and earth*, where *Tholdoth* <note>Hebrew: תולדות (generations)</note> is written with a *Vau* at the end by a singular gift of divinity. Indeed, this letter is subtracted from the same word in all other places, except for the one genealogy of Christ beginning near the end of the Book of Ruth: *These are the generations of Phares*, which lineage was certainly more perfect than any other generation. Therefore, this third letter of the most sacred Name warns us by the number six that the entire universe is perfect, since on the sixth day God saw all things that He had made, and they were very good. Therefore, the heavens and the earth and all the adornment of them were perfected.

It remains for us to discourse briefly on the third progression of things, that is, the fourth letter. For after the "subsistences" truly lacking matter <note>referring to angelic or divine beings</note>, and the "substances" truly possessing matter, there follow—not without justice—things which are at once the forms of bodies within matter, and yet simultaneously are separated from bodies and materials. To this class belongs the rational soul, which has a "fiery vigor," as Virgil says, "and a celestial origin, insofar as harmful bodies do not retard them, nor do earthly limbs and dying members dull them." Although the soul, according to its more excellent essence, walks in equal company with the angelic nature, nevertheless, as far as its actual being...

# ON THE WONDER-WORKING WORD

As for what pertains to the lower realm, that which is immersed in corruptible things and joined to those communicating with the body becomes posterior <note>meaning lower or later in the hierarchy of being</note>. Led by this very thought, the Royal Prophet <note>King David, in the Psalms</note> said: *You have made him a little lower than the angels.* <note>original: "Minuisti eum paulominus ab angelis"</note> I shall do you a great favor, my friends, who are now weary from listening toward evening, for I shall conclude the argument as briefly as possible. In Plato’s book titled *Timaeus*, this doctrine stands forth: That God in the beginning first created fire, then earth. But these two alone, being so opposite, could not fitly cohere without a certain third thing. There remained, therefore, a middle point between both, which would communicate partly with the higher and partly with the lower. Thus, since there are archetypes—like certain fiery lights created first—and then the entire corporeal world embracing the dregs of matter, it was fitting that some substance be made which would neither depend on matter nor exist without matter; so that, as far as the operation of its powers is concerned, it would dwell with the body, and as far as the power of its essence is concerned, it would dwell with the angels, and by either virtue, it would not be absent from either world. The human soul, therefore, is the most direct middle point, which, while being neither, participates in both. Because of this, **Hermes Trismegistus says: O Asclepius, man is a great miracle, a living creature to be adored and honored.** For he passes into the nature of God as if he himself were a god. He knows the race of demons, inasmuch as he recognizes that he originated with them. He despises the part of human nature in himself, trusting in the divinity of the other part. O how much more happily tempered is the nature of man! Joined to the gods by a kindred divinity, he despises that part of himself by which he is earthly. All other things to which he knows himself to be necessary by celestial disposition, he binds to himself with a bond of love, and thus he looks up to heaven. Thus, he is placed in a happier position of "middleness," so that he may love the things below him and be loved by those above him. You hear that the rational soul is the middle between the invisible world and the visible world, yet most closely related to the beings above. For both these reasons, it could not be designated by any mark other than the number five <note>the "quinarius"</note>, which is the last letter called *He* <note>The Hebrew letter ה (He), which has the numerical value of 5</note>, undoubtedly the most just middle of the entire decimal counting system. For if you bind together the numbers around the five on both sides—four above and six below—they come to ten, of which five is the middle. Again, three and seven; again, two and eight; finally, one and nine; and you will always find ten in the same way, and you will experience that only the five is the middle. If we inquire more accurately, the essence of the supernal beings is surely similar and should be shown by a similar character, and nothing but order creates the difference. For the more absolute, pure, and firm the angelic world stands, the more it must be shown to our eyes by the first letter *He* and in a more eminent place. However, the human mind is designated by the second <note>or latter</note> instance of the same character, since it is attached to the body and began to exist simultaneously with the body; but because it will not cease with the death of the body, if it follows its own nature and is not weighed down by foreign allurements, it immediately returns and ascends—both while remaining in the body and flying out of the body—to its corresponding and super-celestial habitation, which I have named the "reduction" <note>the leading back of the soul to its source</note>. Concerning which **Zoroaster** said: **You must ascend to the light itself and to the rays of the Father, from whence the soul was sent to you, clothed in much mind.** And **Pythagoras** handed down these words: **If you, leaving the body, [ascend] into the free**
->**ether**<-

<meta>This page continues the discourse on the mystical properties of divine names, specifically focusing on the letter 'He' and the transition from the discussion of God's essence to the names of angels.</meta>

# SECOND BOOK

->If you transfer a free thing, you will be an immortal god.<- This is the ultimate <term>reduction</term><note>The process of returning or leading back to a source.</note> of man to divinity; this is the end, this is the boundary, this is the goal, this is the limit and perfection of this name so sacred, so adorable, so powerful, so wonderful, which God mentioned in the presence of Moses: *I will show all good to you, and I will call the name Tetragrammaton before you.* <note>original: "Ego ostendã omne bonũ tibi, & uocabo nomen Tetragrammaton coram te." See Exodus 33:19.</note> This is the fourth letter, and in Daniel, the appearance of the fourth is like the son of God. For God gave power to men to become sons of God to those who believe in his name. Its final *He* <note>The fifth letter of the Hebrew alphabet, occurring twice in the four-letter name of God (Y-H-V-H).</note> is like the fourth face of the eagle in the wheels, which in Ezekiel walked continually and did not turn back. By the force and power of this letter, Abraham, at a hundred years old, from Sarah, a ninety-year-old and sterile woman, fathered and grew into nations beyond the laws of nature. And for that reason, he was named **Abraham**, whereas before he was called **Abram**. <note>The addition of the letter 'He' (h) transformed Abram to Abraham.</note> Why say more? All our happiness is expressed by this character of the number five, by which **Alpha** is united with **O** <note>Omega</note>, and the waters under heaven with the waters which are above the heavens. By this we can do what we wish, and we wish what the celestial beings themselves wish. For this reason, **Asher** also obtained the fifth part, since blessedness is most abundantly consummated in the letter *He*. For we interpret *Asher* as happiness or blessedness.

Night falls, best of men; in a too-long speech I have treated those names which seemed to signify the essence of God. Learn the rest yourselves from **Dionysius the Areopagite** and **Jerome**, whether they signify the power of God, or his operation, or our affection toward him. Although there are some explained neither by those men nor by other Greek and Latin authors, but only by our own, such as this name **Isch** <note>Hebrew: Ish (Man)</note>, which suggests both man and God, with whom in the book of Genesis **Jacob** himself is said to have wrestled alone. And it follows: *Your name shall by no means be called Jacob, but Israel, because you have prevailed with God and man.* Likewise there is the word **Na**, which we use in distress and disturbances of the soul. Just as Moses in the book of Numbers: *Forgive, Na, the sin of your people,* which you have translated as, *Forgive, I beseech you.* And in the hundred and seventeenth Psalm: *Ana Lord, save now, Na. Ana Lord, prosper now, Na,* which you translate more elegantly thus: *O Lord, save me; O Lord, grant me good prosperity.* For you also read **Jah** in *Hallelujah*, and **Sabaoth** in the sacrifices, and **Adonai** in Exodus, and **El** in *Immanuel*, and **Ischyros** which is **Shaddai**. And *before the sun Innon is his name*, which you say as, *His name remains.* Also **Elyon** and many others not very common, such as **Makom** and **Kaphu**. 

But there are also certainly many names of angels generically, such as **Chashmalim**, **Chayot**, **Ophanim**, **Tarshishim**, and the rest. Then individually not a few, such as **Uriel**, **Asariel**, **Zuriel**, **Abir**, **Metatron**, and those similar to them. You hold in your memory what I have finally entrusted to you by hearing these sacred names, which is certainly very brief and very small. Although there are some others of that kind, which I would nonetheless set forth and explain however much larger or more moderate they might be than these, but the sun set long ago, and you are tired of listening. I delegate the remaining parts to **Capnion** <note>The Greek nickname for Johannes Reuchlin.</note> for tomorrow. Now let us go inside from here to dinner.

Then **SIDONIUS** said: "Let it be done."
And **CAPNION**: "Let it be done. For it is sumptuously ready."

<meta>This page begins Book Three of Reuchlin's "De Verbo Mirifico" (On the Miracle-Working Word). The dialogue continues between the three protagonists: Sidonius (the Epicurean philosopher), Baruchias (the Jewish scholar), and Capnion (the Christian scholar, representing Reuchlin himself).</meta>

# THE WONDER-WORKING WORD BY JOHANN REUCHLIN OF PFORZHEIM, DOCTOR OF LAWS: CAPNION, OR ON THE MIRACLE-WORKING WORD

<margin>i</margin>Now the third day dawns, venerable Prelate, in which—after the sacred names of the ancient philosophers of the Greek nation and of the Hebrews have been set forth—it is time that the continuous discourse of Capnion <note>The Greek nickname for Reuchlin, meaning "smoky," a play on his German name "Rauch."</note>, a Phorcensian by birth and a Christian by profession, finally be heard. Yet before his beginning, Sidonius, moved again by a great fire for investigating learning, begins thus before the others: "It seemed to me, my dear Capnion," said Sidonius, "that this whole bedchamber moved during the night, and the very walls shook from those portentous words which Baruchias recited to us so learnedly in his speech yesterday—words that were not only most pleasant to hear, but truly most admirable. I believe that from sharp and attentive thinking (as often happens), the unheard-of novelty of such names seized my mind as it neared sleep and pulled it in different directions, so that my imagination fabricated certain gestures of the walls. Or, if what he thinks is true—and I do not strongly reject it—that great chorus of heavenly beings could easily have followed those symbols of divinity. For no god would dare, according to the verses of Homer, to remain in his seat once Jove has moved. But come, Capnion, now is the time for the miracle-working word; do not frustrate our hope." 

To this he replied: "Do you think that anything was left over from yesterday by this speaker Baruchias for me to consume today? He is so full of a variety of names, it would be a wonder if he did not pour out every incentive for stupendous deeds." 

And Sidonius answered, "I truly think so. For he himself testified that so far, none of these words have prevailed by force or power above the common course of nature, nor have any others of his people ever been able to do so." 

Then BARUCHIAS said, "Sidonius reports the truth. Therefore, Capnion, see whether you consider it a matter of your own honor to fulfill what you promised us: to explain both that certain word by which miracles are performed, and at the same time its infallible rite, and hold our minds in suspense no longer." 

Then he said, "Since you strip me so much of the freedom to refuse, I shall do not perhaps as much as you wish, but as much as I am able. Since I think it is now established for you that our ancestors had some name or word by which they were once held to have performed portents, miracles, and wonders, there remain few things which the great friendship between us forbids me to deny you. For if I name the word itself and hand down the rite for using it, I shall have rightly fulfilled the duty you demand. Although all these things will be ready and at hand, nevertheless the matter seems to me to require seeking from a little deeper source. 

Consider within your souls and diligently recall the first day of our disputation, when it was agreed among us according to each man's opinion—and I know not if anything else has sat more deeply in our minds and judgment, or stuck more firmly, or stood more solidly—that whether there be present in us humans a certain wisdom of eternal things, or a science of perpetual things, or a knowledge of momentary things, nevertheless right faith is always superior and more certain than those, inasmuch as it does not rely on our empty..."

<summary>The dialogue opens on the third day as Sidonius describes the supernatural physical effects he felt after hearing the Hebrew names of God. The group now calls upon Capnion (Reuchlin) to reveal the specific "miracle-working word" and the ritual for its use, which he begins to do by emphasizing the supremacy of faith over human knowledge.</summary>

<keywords>Johann Reuchlin, Capnion, Sidonius, Baruchias, Kabbalah, sacred names, miracle-working word, faith vs. reason, Christian Hebraism</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of divine power as the source of all creation, moving from general philosophical principles to the specific authority of the "Word of God" (Logos) as the primary agent of miracles and the ordering of the universe.</meta>

->BOOK THREE<-

It does not rely on human reasonings, but rests entirely upon divine revelation; from this source we must seek the origin of the world and all things that proceed from God into the world. This is supported by the distinguished testimony of Paul of Tarsus <note>St. Paul</note>, who says to the Hebrews: *By faith we understand that the ages were fashioned by the word of God, so that from things invisible, visible things were made.* <note>Hebrews 11:3</note> What the royal prophet <note>King David</note> revealed to mortals through divine revelation in this song: *By the word of the Lord the heavens were created, and by the breath of his mouth all their ornament, power, and host.* <note>Psalm 33:6</note> The Lord is also faithful in all his words, wherefore let no one dare to change the word of God, for it remains forever; all created things must obey it, because he spoke and they were made. For even fire, ice, and the stormy winds carry out his word. It is this which melts the crystal, gives life to man, heals the sick, and slays transgressors; all of this the sacred scriptures have brought forth. Therefore, this Word is set over nature and established as if in a most vast empire—the Word which, according to John the theologian and evangelist, *In the beginning was with God, and all things were made through him,* and what is even greater, *the Word was God.* <note>John 1:1</note> Nothing, therefore, shall be more powerful to us than that Word, which is full of the greatest force, vehement strength, and supreme power. What the power of God is when he wills to perform any work—or rather, the very will of him who wills—we are unable to discern while we dwell in this fragile skin <note>the human body</note>; how great and how spacious the field of its strength is for that will and that Word, concerning which this prophetic oracle exists: *The Lord has done all things whatsoever he willed in heaven and on earth, in the sea and in the depths.* <note>Psalm 135:6</note> You have here the very first thing that one ought especially to use in miracles, which exceed the accustomed habit of nature, the worn path, the established limits, and the order of the universe; for then it will not be a limited and narrow virtue such as is the nature of all subsisting things, but an infinite power with the most ample space of dominion, and the widest field of that divine will which is the Word of God, rightly inscribed in the Apocalypse <note>The Book of Revelation</note> as *King of kings and Lord of lords,* who holds the empire of omnipotence over both those things which are and those which are not. This discourse might be held as foolish by the Peripatetics <note>Followers of Aristotle's school of philosophy</note> and any who profess Natural Philosophy, but to contemplative men it stands as a fitting and true speech. For God, says the Apostle, *calls those things which are not as though they were.* <note>Romans 4:17</note> Dionysius <note>Pseudo-Dionysius the Areopagite, a Christian Neoplatonist</note>, a follower of this discipline, says: "We shall dare to affirm this also by reason, since that which is not participates in the Beautiful and the Good." Whether you call this the "Word" or by any other name, I do not object, provided that the first exit of the Divinity from within its own immanence is understood—like light to light, and the radiance of light back to light, or mind to reason and vice versa in that order. This is indeed called by the Greeks either *rational word* or *verbal reason*, for <term>Logos</term> <note>original: λόγος</note> signifies both "reason" and "word." Indeed, that ancient author Zeno <note>Zeno of Citium, founder of Stoicism</note> calls the maker of the universe and the disposer of nature the *Logos*; and Hermes <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Egyptian sage</note>, the most ancient of philosophers, writes to his son (for I shall interpret speech from speech in so necessary a cause): "For there is," he says, "O son, a secret Word of wisdom, holy, the holy son of the Lord of all and the pre-intellected, which to speak is beyond man." Plato, in the *Epinomis*, wished all things that happen in the world to consist of this Word, when...

<summary>The author argues that the "Word of God" (Logos) is the supreme authority over nature and the primary agent of miracles, citing a synthesis of Biblical scripture, Stoic philosophy, and Hermetic tradition.</summary>
