<meta>This page continues the philosophical dialogue concerning the "Wonder-Working Word" (De Verbo Mirifico), specifically exploring the relationship between the Divine Mind and the Word (Logos), drawing parallels between Platonic philosophy and Hebrew theology.</meta>

# ON THE WONDER-WORKING WORD
-> d <-

When he discussed the fellowship and equal dignity of divine beings in this way, he said, "Let us also render honors, not assigning a year to some, a month to others, and to others no portion at all, nor any time in which he runs his circuit, completing the world itself, which the most divine Word <note>original: *verbum*; here referring to the Logos or Divine Reason</note> of all things ordained." The fortunate man first admires this Word, then conceives a love for learning of it, as much as mortal nature can bear. Indeed, you shall not judge that Word in our common fashion, as if it were a messenger of air broken between the teeth, which is how they describe the voice of mortals. 

But if it is necessary to compare fragile things to eternal ones, earthly to super-celestial, and human to divine, then at least let us consider the internal word of man—which you call the supreme reason <note>original: *rationem*</note> within us proceeding from the mind. It must be allowed that it is neither entirely the same as the mind, nor in every way different from it. For human reason, flowing forth from the mind, is not wrongly judged to be distinct from its source of intellect; yet when it brings the mind out into the open, it cannot be seen as anything other than the mind. Therefore, since the nature of mental substance is the same, it is judged to be diverse based on the functions performed. 

In the likeness of this shadowed image, one may perceive within the inner chambers of the most august Deity the Word or Reason of the paternal mind itself, because it is able to subsist through itself. For this reason, it is distinguished from the Father from whom it simultaneously holds its existence. And yet, since it clearly reveals within itself all things that we contemplate as being in God or around God, we affirm that it is the same and one with the substance of the paternal mind. Nor is there anything anywhere that indicates a distance between God the Speaker and God the Word, except the nature of the prolation <note>The act of bringing forth or speaking</note> alone. For all other things are equal, or rather the same, in both. Each is God, each is eternal; therefore, each exists before all time. This one has always brought forth; this one has always been brought forth—the Word being equal in power to the one bringing it forth, and the Father being the bringer-forth of the Word. 

Plato himself, no idle searcher of divine matters, suggests this in his religious writings in the *Letter to Hermias, Erastus, and Coriscus*, when toward the end he speaks thus: "It is fitting for all of you, being three, to read this letter, especially when gathered together. If not, then at least two together as much as possible; finally, using the covenant and proper law as is right, with an oath interposed—not without elegant zeal and the sister of that zeal, discipline—by witnessing God, the leader of all things both present and future, and the Father and Lord of that leader and cause, whom we shall know if we philosophize rightly." 

Do you not clearly see that what Plato taught in the *Epinomis*—that the Word disposed the order of the world—is in this place called the God, cause, and leader of all things, and is said to have a Father? Therefore, the Word is the Son, whose Father is named Lord. 

I would like us now to recall that discourse which Baruchias <note>A character in the dialogue representing Jewish mystical tradition</note> gave yesterday around noon, both learnedly and copiously, in which he wisely began instructing us concerning the Ineffable Name <note>The Tetragrammaton (YHWH)</note>, which alone most closely suits the Highest God. He maintained that translators placed "Lord" in the stead of the Four-Lettered Name <note>original: *Tetragrammati*</note>, because they could find no equivalent expression for it in any language. Join to this, if you wish, that speech which I myself held the day before yesterday. In it, I remember affirming to you that Thales, Pythagoras, and Plato traveled through Syria to be instructed in Hebrew disciplines, from which not a few...

<summary>The text argues for the divinity and co-eternity of the "Word" (Logos) by comparing it to human reason and citing Plato's letters as evidence of a Father-Son relationship within the Godhead. It also links Greek philosophy to Hebrew tradition, mentioning the Tetragrammaton and the travels of ancient philosophers to Syria.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the theological exploration of the nature of the Son (the Word) and his relationship to the Father, drawing on both Platonic philosophy and Biblical prophecy.</meta>

# BOOK THREE

<note>h ii</note>

...they have compared with their own letters, you see a trace of it here. Plato calls the Lord of all things both the Leader and the Father of the cause. Therefore, that God of all, both Leader and Cause of things present and future—because he ordered the world—who will now doubt that he is that most divine Word <note>original: "verbum"</note> according to Plato, by which anyone who is snatched up into admiration, love, and perception of him shall be happy? The Word, therefore, is the Son of the Lord; for which reason he is both God and Leader and Cause of the world. And so he is named with many titles, variously by many, and differently by others. But among so many names of that most divine Son, one stands out as most chosen for miracles, in which the Supreme Father was most well-pleased. Concerning this matter, he himself made us more certain through prophecy in his Spirit. Hear, I beseech you, the sacred oracle of Isaiah: *A child is born to us and a son is given to us, and the government is placed upon his shoulder, and he called his name Wonderful.* <note>Isaiah 9:6</note> But if his names are so many and so varied, we must seek accurately which among them all is that distinguished name which the Father called "Wonderful." For he is called by John the "Only-begotten Son who is in the bosom of the Father." First, although the title "Son" may apply to other men, yet it fits this most divine Word more properly, while it fits mortals less justly. For he is the same as the Father, and not only that, but also from him. For the Father is he who provided the beginning of being according to a nature similar to himself; the Son, however, is he who took the origin of being by birth from another. Wherefore "Only-begotten" is added, which is most proper, peculiar, and his own to this Son—not only because he alone was begotten of the One, but also because he was begotten uniquely and in a single way, far differently than in a corporeal nature. 

Next, let it be determined what "the bosom of the Father" means for us. And rightly he is first said to be "He who is," so that we may recognize his co-eternity with the Father; he who commanded Moses: *Thus you shall say to the children of Israel: "He Who Is" has sent me,* as if the Son were in him from eternity just as if in a bosom. Therefore he adds: *Who is in the bosom of the Father.* By this speech, he declares the intimate and most familiar nativity of the Son from the Father. By another name, David calls him the "Hand" when he speaks to the Father thus: *Why do you turn away your hand, and your right hand from the midst of your bosom?* Therefore, the Hand in the bosom of the Father is the Son, and the Son in the bosom is the Hand of the Father, through whom he made all things; he who says: *I myself, I am the first and I am the last. My hand also founded the earth, and my right hand measured the heavens.* 

Nor shall I omit to show you the certainty of the cause. For that this Hand took its beginning from the Father, the truth is ready in the 108th Psalm. For thus the prophet prays: *Help me, O Lord my God, save me according to your mercy; and they shall know that this is your hand, and you, Lord, have made it.* But that the Father and the Hand are the same and one substance, and one God and the same Lord, will be easy to see in the 43rd Psalm, if the verse is read according to the Hebrew custom. For it says: *You are your hand, you have scattered the nations and planted them.* For what follows a little later there concerning the "illumination of the face" is no small secret of the divine Trinity. Let us return again to the Son, called by the Latins the "Word" <note>original: "verbum"</note>, and by the Greeks much more significantly and perhaps more correctly called the <term>Logos</term> <note>original: "logon". A Greek term meaning word, reason, or divine logic.</note>. For <term>λόγος</term> <note>Logos</note> is both reason, and definition, and word, and speech. And so, having obtained pardon, let us bring our fragile things into matters divine.

<summary>The author argues that the "Word" (Logos) described by Plato is identical to the Biblical Son of God, using various titles like "Hand of the Father" and "Only-begotten" to explain the Trinity's unity. He emphasizes that the Greek term Logos more accurately captures the Son's role as both divine reason and the creative power of God.</summary>

<keywords>Logos, Trinity, Plato, Isaiah, David, Only-begotten, Hand of God, Christology, Biblical exegesis, Divine Names</keywords>

--- Page 93 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical and theological exploration of the "Wonder-Working Word," focusing on the relationship between the Father and the Son through the lens of Aristotelian logic and Neoplatonic thought.</meta>

-># ON THE WONDER-WORKING WORD<-

For just as human reason relates to the mind, so the subsisting Reason <note>original: "ratio," here meaning both "reason" and "the Word/Logos"</note> of God relates to the Father, not only because of the impassible <note>incapable of suffering or changing</note> generation of both, but also because of their cohesion. For there will be no reason without a mind, nor any mind without reason. Since the office of the Word is to announce the interior things of the mind—as the Stagirite <note>Aristotle, who was born in Stagira</note> judges in his book *On Interpretation*—and to bring forth secrets from the innermost chambers of thoughts and to spread open the will of the speaker, for that reason this Son is most aptly judged to be the "Word" of the Father. Because "no one has ever seen God," says John, "but the only-begotten Son who is in the bosom of the Father, he has announced him to us." He is the Messenger of Great Counsel, and for that reason is named an "angel" <note>from the Greek 'angelos' meaning messenger</note> who has revealed the super-celestial meditations of God to mortals. 

Regarding the definition, it is no secret to you that it must be of such a condition that it seems to eviscerate <note>to lay bare the internal essence</note> the entire nature of the thing being defined. Now, the "Reason" which the name signifies, according to Aristotle’s edition of the *Metaphysics*, is the definition itself. In this manner, therefore, this most divine and substantive Word—indeed, this super-substantial Reason—may rightly be called a certain evident demonstration of the paternal nature; his whole adequate and universal generation was a silent and certain description of the Generator, through which we immediately know him with a clearer intuition, whom a moment before we did not know. 

"Therefore, he who sees me," he says, "sees also my Father," calling a sight of this kind the knowledge of the intellect. For the mind itself is the eye of the soul. Therefore, the vision of the mind will be the notice of things, which become known especially when the whole substance is opened in a brief speech, which the logicians <term>dialecticians: scholars of formal logic and debate</term> call a "term." For if this is what it means to be "begotten"—namely, to be produced from the substance of the generator as a likeness according to nature—then the Father will no longer be hidden to whom the Son is shown, who is the perfect image of the Father. Paul expressed this in his letters, urging the Colossians to give thanks to God and the Father, who "transferred us into the kingdom of the Son of his love, who is the image of the invisible God." 

But I ask you, wise men, in what way or how shall any image of an invisible thing consist? By what outlines, what features, what likeness, or what color shall it imitate that example which no one has ever seen, which no one knows except the Son? But consider what follows: "No one knows the Father except the Son, and he to whom the Son wishes to reveal him." Therefore, the Son himself is a living and willing image; not one assimilated with colors by Apelles <note>the most famous painter of ancient Greece</note>, not engraved by Pyrgoteles <note>a renowned gem-engraver</note>, not cast in bronze by Polyclitus <note>a famous Greek sculptor</note>, nor fused by the cement of any craftsmanship, carved with a chisel, painted with a needle, or fashioned from clay or wax. He is not like Dido’s longed-for and imaginary "little Aeneas" <note>a reference to Virgil's Aeneid, where Dido wishes for a child who resembles his father</note> who would reflect his father only in his face; nor like Seth, begotten in the image of Adam; nor like the other sons of our propagation whom mothers desire to be as similar as possible to their husbands in appearance. 

For "Fatherhood" is spoken of first and more truly regarding divine generation, and regarding us only later and in the manner of a certain shadow. For the Apostle writes to the Ephesians: "For this reason I bow my knees to the Father of our Lord Jesus Christ, from whom all fatherhood in heaven and on earth is named." Truly, in these mortal things, that which imitates its exemplar either is not of one substance with it (even if it is of equal shape), or if by chance it is of one substance, it will not be entirely equal in power in all other respects...

<summary>The author argues that the Son is the "Word" and "Reason" of God, acting as a perfect, living definition that reveals the otherwise invisible Father. He distinguishes this divine "image" from man-made art and physical biological reproduction, asserting that divine fatherhood is the true original of which human fatherhood is a mere shadow.</summary>
<keywords>Logos, Aristotle, Christology, Divine Image, Metaphysics, Paternity, Revelation, Neoplatonism</keywords>

--- Page 94 ---
<meta>This page continues the theological discourse from Book Two, focusing on the nature of the Word (Logos) and the transition from the eternal Word to the Incarnation.</meta>

->BOOK THREE<-

->h iii<-

The same, similar in counsel, equal in power in its effect, and equal in motion. Indeed, that divine image is so close to the Father—as close as anything can possibly be—that it should more rightly be called "the same" rather than an "image." Where the image is of an identical <term>consubstantial essence</term><note>original: "homousios"; the theological concept that the Father and Son are of the exact same substance.</note>, there the thing itself is discerned entirely in the likeness, even if in its own nature it can neither be seen nor touched. They also sometimes call this image, which is of the same substance as the highest Father, a "seal," using the same interpretation as above. For what a ring effects in the form of a seal linearly within time, here the Father expresses in His Word substantially within eternity, and in a far more excellent manner. 

But concerning the "character"—since the Son is also honored by this name—I shall hold another opinion, which I will soon set forth to you. My own boldness—or to speak more truly, perhaps my excessive confidence in your kindness toward me—has not only cast me into this long-windedness but has handed me over to it; nevertheless, however it happens, I will gladly explain. First, however, I must remind you that all the names of the most divine and wondrous Word ought to be understood in a threefold way, according to the different conditions to which the names belong. For that the Word is both the cause of the universe, and God, and the disposer of nature, and that in the beginning He was with God like a hand in the bosom of the Father, has, I believe, become clearly known to you from the previous discourse, brief though it was. 

Now it remains for us not to be ignorant of that which will be most useful of all for our task of performing miracles: that this same God, the Word of God the Father, by His own Spirit proceeding from the Father, was finally incarnated from an incorrupt Virgin in recent years, just as the prophecies of the prophets many centuries before had predicted would happen. Let us read the oracle of Jeremiah: *Because the Lord <term>Tetragrammaton</term><note>The four-letter Hebrew name of God (YHWH), here used to emphasize the divine authority of the creator.</note> has created a new thing on earth: a woman shall encompass a man.* This is stupendous, this is new, this is admirable: that the female sex, without cohabitation, without the embraces of a man, but by God alone—not merely making but creating—carried in her womb and encompassed in her womb not an embryo, but a perfect man. Because of this, Isaiah says: *The Lord himself shall give you a sign; Behold, a virgin shall conceive and bear a son, and shall call his name "God with us."* 

For what else did the prophet wish to express by that name "Immanuel" except this alone: namely, the incarnate Word? So that because He is the Word, He is God; and because He dwells among us, He appears incarnate. Otherwise, it would be a sign of no value for a King to seek so earnestly, nor would it be believed that he had not tempted God in vain, if God had promised to dwell with us only according to the nature of His divinity, by which He fills heaven and earth. For no place is empty of God. But there were three things in that surpassing sign to be weighed, not without great wonder from men: first, that a virgin should conceive; second, that a virgin should give birth; and lastly, that He who was born from her should be both God and man at the same time. These individual points are found to be signified even to the malicious and unwilling Hebrews. The universal Church of our ancestors will be the witness, who were present for such a great event and followed that <term>God-man</term><note>original: "theanthropum"; from the Greek 'theos' (God) and 'anthropos' (man).</note> with their own feet. Wherefore John, His disciple, made it known through his Epistle: *That which was from the beginning, which we have heard, which we have seen with our eyes, which we have looked upon, and our hands have handled, concerning the Word of life.*

<summary>The author discusses the consubstantial nature of the Son as the "seal" of the Father and transitions into the mystery of the Incarnation, citing Jeremiah and Isaiah to prove that the Word became the "God-man" (Theanthropos).</summary>
<keywords>Consubstantiality, Holy Trinity, Incarnation, Virgin Birth, Immanuel, Jeremiah, Isaiah, Tetragrammaton, Theanthropos, Word of Life</keywords>

--- Page 95 ---
<meta>This page continues the theological exploration of the Word (Logos), moving into the third book of the treatise. It focuses on the mystery of the Incarnation and the nature of divine names.</meta>

### BOOK THREE

And the life was made manifest, and we have seen and bear witness and announce to you the eternal life which was with the Father and appeared to us. <note>A translation of 1 John 1:2.</note> And it is not only for that reason that this sign given by God is esteemed so surpassing and so wonderful—because he appeared to us by bodily sight and even in a human body—as if the angels had not also once shown themselves to the fathers, with whom they lived and spoke in a human manner, and even the Lord God himself at times. Rather, it is because by his coming he "dyed" <note>original: "tinxit" — likely a metaphor for the transformation or permeation of humanity by the divine.</note> the entire mass of human substance, just as a superior leaven is said to permeate the whole dough and to perfume <note>original: "suffire" — to fumigate or scent; here meaning to sanctify or influence.</note> any destiny. This is that which excels every miracle, which we rightly value most highly, and which we pursue with unceasing proclamation. For the Deity of this divine man—that is, of the incarnate Word—united to the whole leaven (that is, to our nature), has so sublimated us by his "perfuming" that, the barrier of our ancestors' and grandfathers' condemnation having been removed, we are able through his name <insert>to become divine</insert> and be counted among the gods.

You have, therefore, three states of the Word: He is God, he is the Son, and he is Incarnate. For each of these, we find most religious titles in the sacred scriptures, either denying or affirming. For we express God by nothing more certainly than by a privative particle or a negative word <note>A reference to "Apophatic Theology," which describes God by what He is not (e.g., infinite/not-finite).</note>, although even that is not certain, since as far as his substance is concerned, he is subject to no name. For whatever affirming symbols assert about God, all of that is neither his essence nor his nature, but that which is believed to be most closely around it. Whence that saying of Hermes <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Hellenistic figure associated with Hermetic philosophy.</note>: "Because his name cannot be spoken by a human mouth, for to speak it is beyond man." And also that in the Revelation of our theologian <note>Referring to John the Evangelist and Revelation 19:12-13.</note>: "And on his head were many diadems, having a name written which no one knows but himself, and he was clothed in a garment sprinkled with blood, and his name was called the Word of God."

In this place, the most sacred divination of that exile <note>John the Evangelist, exiled on Patmos.</note> can clearly instruct us concerning this Son of God: both when he has a proper name and when he has none. Here I hope there comes to our memory the prayer of Baruchia <note>Likely a reference to a Kabbalistic or apocryphal source associated with the name Baruch.</note> which asserted the first emanation from God, which according to the philosophy of the Hebrews is called *Kether* <term>Kether: The "Crown," the highest Sephirah or emanation in the Hebrew Kabbalah.</term>, that is, the diadem and royal majesty. For because it is the highest and most ample, it therefore contains in itself many diadems which are on the head—that is, in his multiform wisdom. Therefore, this first exit of such great majesty from the Father of Lights, because it is ineffable, cannot obtain a peculiar name. For in the spirit Isaiah exclaims: "Who shall declare his generation?" <note>Isaiah 53:8.</note> For if the prophet will not declare it, who will declare it, since we perceive nothing of divine matters except by the prophetic spirit—that is, through a revelation falling upon us from above? Wherefore a little before, he says: "To whom has the arm of the Lord been revealed?" Now it is clearly established from the foregoing what the holy scriptures mean by "hand" or "arm," as being nothing else in God than substance, whose nature, subsisting through itself, always depends with and from another. For just as the Father is always whole and never crippled, so he was never sterile but always a parent, because he was always a Father. But he is a parent without time; therefore, a perpetual Father of a perpetual Son. Such a distinction we conceive in the mind under the dark cloud of thought.

<meta>This page continues the dialogue on the mystical power of words, specifically focusing on the hidden names of God and the Trinity as encoded in Hebrew scripture. The previous page likely discussed the limitations of human language in describing the divine.</meta>

# ON THE WONDER-WORKING WORD
-> h iiii <-

But those things which we see through a glass darkly <note>original: "per speculum in ænigmate" - a reference to 1 Corinthians 13:12</note> and only in part, we certainly cannot express with any proportionate, clear, or complete name. For this reason, the one wearing crowns upon his head has a name in the Apocalypse <note>The Book of Revelation</note> which no one knows except himself; this was rightly said concerning a proper, adequate name that encompasses the entire definition of the thing. For concerning the whole Trinity, no one knows this—not even the organs—except insofar as the Divinity itself has brought it forth freely according to our measure; not as great as it is, but as much as we can grasp. Do you not see clearly enough that the one wearing the crowns lacks an equal name among us? Yet, do you see how, when clothed in a blood-stained garment, he begins to be called the Son of God by men? For concerning the Son, we held nothing stable, nothing firm, and nothing known before the coming of the Incarnate Word and his garment sprinkled with blood. Whatever we were taught by prophets, philosophers, or poets, we judged it all to be full of smoke and darkness. "The Lord said to my Lord, sit at my right hand," "You are my son, today I have begotten you." And "his going forth is from the beginning, from the days of eternity." And "the stone which the builders rejected has become the head of the corner; this was made by the Lord," or more truly, "this was from the Lord; it is marvelous in our eyes." Indeed, these are shadows in our eyes, these are riddles, these are doubtful oracles. For who in that age would have shown me the Father and the Son in the "stone," which the Holy Scripture brought forth in Hebrew as <term>Aben</term> <note>Hebrew: אבן (Eben)</note>? It is well established that in that language, *Ab* signifies the Father, and *Ben* the Son; these three letters, joined into two syllables, form <term>Aben</term>, that is, "stone." This is a mystery, this is a secret, which I think I would not have perceived then; I would have been a Davus, not an Oedipus <note>A proverbial expression: Davus was a simple slave in Roman comedy, while Oedipus was the famous solver of riddles.</note>. Similarly, in the word <term>Bra</term> <note>Hebrew: ברא (Bara) - "He created"</note>, which means "he created" and with which the book of Moses begins, I would not have sought a knot in this bulrush <note>A Latin idiom meaning to look for a difficulty where there is none, or to find hidden complexity</note> so as to scrutinize those letters in which I might detect "A" for the Father, "B" for the Son, and "R" for the Spirit. Or I might have conjectured that the word <term>Bra</term> itself is the first syllable "in the beginning," by which argument it would be clear either that the most Holy Trinity was the beginning of the world, or that the Father created heaven and earth in the Son, which happens in this manner: If anyone joins the last of the three letters of <term>Bra</term> to the first, <term>Ab</term> immediately remains, which we showed above to be the Father. Then, doubling the "B" and adding the remaining "R," it will sound as <term>Bebar</term>, that is, "in the Son." For as you know, vowels among the Hebrews are not placed in the line of letters as such. Finally, if one takes the whole expression, he will surely perceive that this entire truth lies hidden in a single syllable: namely, "The Father created in the Son." O wonderful thing, but one in need of a prophetic spirit or divine inspiration! In this manner, infinite things are encompassed in the most sacred characters—not only the fulfillment of past events but also certain omens of the future, provided someone is present who does not so much interpret them as prophesy them. For thus the spirits of the prophets of a past age become subject to future prophets; wherefore the Apostle says: "I wish that you all spoke in tongues, but even more that you prophesied. For he who prophesies is greater than he who speaks in tongues." And some are seers, as Plato says in the *Timaeus*, while others are the interpreters of seers. Because indeed Paul, the teacher of the Gentiles, even deigns to use the title of "poet"...

<meta>This page continues the dialogue on the "Wonder-Working Word," specifically exploring how ancient pagan poetry prefigures Christian mysteries. It follows a discussion on the hidden names of God.</meta>

# ON THE WONDER-WORKING WORD

<note>original: "DE VERBO MIRIFICO"</note>

Regarding the prophets, let us also say this of the poets. Who, before the Word was made flesh, would have dared to apply the mystery of Orpheus <note>Orpheus was a legendary Greek poet and musician; Reuchlin views his "Orphic Hymns" as containing hidden divine truths</note> concerning Night, Heaven, and Ether—which you also, Sidonius, recently mentioned—to the most simple Trinity of the highest God? Who would have dared to say of the Father, alongside Orpheus: *Night is the generation of all things*, and therefore, Night is the Father? Listen to Malachi: *Have we not all one father? Has not one God created us?* <note>Malachi 2:10</note> This is He who, as the Psalms testify, *made darkness his secret place; his pavilion round about him.* <note>Psalm 18:11</note> Nor indeed would that suffice concerning the birth of the only Son, unless in that same Orphic hymn to Night there followed: *Night, you who send forth the light.* Therefore, the prophet directs his prayers to God the Father thus: *Send out thy light and thy truth: let them lead me; let them bring me unto thy holy hill, and to thy tabernacles* <note>Psalm 43:3</note>—that is, to you, the Father, whose tabernacles (as appeared before) are darkness and a secret place. Both of these led me—both your Son, who spoke in the treasury, saying *I am the light of the world*, and the Spirit of Truth who proceeds from you, the Father (as it is written in John)—and they brought me into a sublime kind of contemplation. For in this way we speak of the "mountain" which Moses ascended to reach God. Do you wish to hear the Apostle teaching this far more clearly? *For God, who commanded the light to shine out of darkness, hath shined in our hearts, to give the light of the knowledge of the glory of God in the person of Jesus Christ.* <note>2 Corinthians 4:6</note> For you should know that the Word made flesh is hereafter called after the manner of the Apostle, to which he added *prosopon* <note>Greek: πρόσωπον</note>, that is, "person," which he intended to be the seal of the threefold distinction <note>The Trinity</note>.

Proceed now to hear what Orpheus, the ancient poet, sings also concerning Heaven, whom he likewise calls the parent of all. If Night brought forth all things, as the previous hymn demonstrates, she also brought forth Heaven; therefore, Night will be the parent of Heaven. But she is not like a common parent of all things, but rather as one sending forth celestial light from herself. Hence Olympus is called "Heaven," as if it were *ololampes* <note>Greek: ὁλολαμπής, "all-shining"</note>, because it shines entirely; and this Orpheus soon calls *Pangenetora* <note>Greek: πανγενέτωρ</note>, that is, the "begetter of all." In this most ample goodness, he agrees with Night, the maker of all. For just as Night is said to be the generation of the universe, so also Heaven is proclaimed in those same poems as the begetter of all, so that what David sang is true: *The darkness hideth not from thee; but the night shineth as the day: the darkness and the light are both alike to thee.* <note>Psalm 139:12</note> Consider now the consubstantial <term>consubstantial: of the same substance or essence</term> equal power of Night and Light. For *as is his darkness, so is his light*. Each is the creator of all, each is God, each is light—the Father in the Son, and the Son from the Father, as light from light and the brightness of eternal light. Concerning this, our Ennius <note>An early Roman poet</note> says: *Behold this sublime glowing thing, whom all invoke as Jove.* He spoke not of the Stygian <note>Underworld</note> Jove, but of the celestial Jove, just as Euripides does in these verses: *You see the high expanse, which embraces the infinite ether and the earth everywhere with moist arms; believe this to be Jove, consider this to be God.* This is that sublime brightness of which Ennius taught us, whom the ancients named *Diespiter* <note>Father of Day</note> and *Lucecius* <note>Light-bringer</note> because of the light of day. From this, the Sun was also named by our people, as Malachi says: *But unto you that fear my name shall the Sun of righteousness arise.* <note>Malachi 4:2</note> This is He whom John saw through revelation on Patmos: *And his countenance was as the sun shineth in his strength*, and he said, *I am the first...*

<summary>The author argues that ancient Greek poets like Orpheus and Roman poets like Ennius were "pagan prophets" who used metaphors of Night, Heaven, and Light to describe the Christian Trinity. He aligns these classical verses with Biblical passages from Malachi, the Psalms, and the Gospel of John to show a universal divine truth.</summary>

# BOOK THREE

and the last. Therefore, concerning our Heaven, whom we consider to be the Son in the Orphic mystery, a similar thing is sung in the second verse of his hymn: *Old in birth, the beginning of all things, and the end of all things.* <note>original: "Nativitate senex, principium omnium, omniumque finis"</note> Nay, indeed, he says the same likewise of Jove: *Jupiter is the first-born, and the last, and the prince of lightnings.* The cause of this you yourselves know, since indeed God is the Son, and although he is God, he was nonetheless born as a man. Concerning whom it is written to the Corinthians: *The first man is of the earth, earthy; the second man is from heaven, heavenly.* <note>1 Corinthians 15:47</note> It is easy to make a conjecture regarding the ether, which Orpheus calls *Pyripnon*, that is, a fiery breath. <term>Pyripnon: from the Greek 'pyr' (fire) and 'pnoe' (breath)</term> I interpret the first verse of his hymn in this way, for he supplicates the ether, exclaiming in this manner: *O thou who possessest the august power of Jove, forever invincible.* Do you not now perceive the equality of power? You perceive the Jovial union, that is, the fellowship with heaven. For according to Hermes Trismegistus <note>A legendary Hellenistic figure associated with a syncretic blend of Greek and Egyptian philosophy</note>, Jupiter is the *Ousia* of heaven, that is, the essence; through heaven, Jupiter offers life to all. Wherefore the Greeks call him *Zina* by a just name. For we say *Zin* means "to live." <note>original: "Zin enim vivere dicimus"</note> *In him* (says our theologian) *was life.* <note>John 1:4</note> Or also *Dia*, just as in Plato’s *Cratylus*, which signifies the preposition "through" <note>Greek 'dia' means 'through'</note>. For all things were made *Dia*, that is, through him. He embraces in his moist arms both the spirit and the corporeal nature, that is, the ether and the earth, according to the sentiment of Euripides which you heard before. For since the only-begotten Son of God is the water of life, as the sacred eloquence relates, *descending like rain upon the fleece, and like the drops that water the earth* <note>Psalm 72:6</note>, it is clear that he embraces in his moist arms—in his shining river, in that outflowing of his, streaming from the paternal substance—the ether, that is, that spirit nourishing all things within and the "fiery vigor," as Virgil says, and the heavenly origin proceeding not from that heaven which we discern with corporeal eyes, but from the invisible, that is, the divine Son and from the Heaven of Heavens itself. On account of this embrace, the Hebrews call heaven *Schamaim*, as if it were *Eschumaim*, that is, fire and water. <note>A traditional mystical etymology: 'Esh' (fire) and 'Mayim' (water)</note> For Ether is so called as if it were *aeth-aer* from *aetho*, that is, "I burn," and *aer*, that is, "spirit," a name which, according to Greek grammar, signifies a burning spirit. From Night, therefore, according to Orpheus, Heaven is born; from Heaven proceeds the Ether. *And there was evening and morning, one day.* <note>Genesis 1:5</note> A thing most like a miracle and scarcely credible, or rather incredible, unless the Word of God had been incarnated, which has instructed us in such great mysteries. It was one thing that was simultaneously evening and morning and day, three days before the sun and moon and stars were born. There was one light, divided into morning and evening and day, which was the same with that same Disposer who said, *Let there be light.* Consider the words, weigh the mysteries. These are the words: *In the beginning God created the heaven and the earth.* Behold the briefest summary of the entire creation. But what was it that was made, and what was in the beginning before anything was made, and in what manner was that which was in the beginning, and who or in what condition was he already who begat the beginning? Thence it follows: *Because the earth was a vast deformity.* Finally, heaven was like a garment which surrounded and concealed the earth, and cloaked it like a nearby mantle. And it was called the abyss, as in the hundred and third Psalm: *Who laid the foundations of the earth upon its stability, it shall not be moved forever and ever. The abyss like a garment is its clothing...*

...of him. Above the heavens there was darkness; after the darkness, water; and above the water, the spirit of the Lord. And so, God, by speaking, made the Word flash forth and there was light. And he divided the light from the darkness, and called the light Day and the darkness Night, and there was evening and there was morning, one day. Indeed, "darkness" and "evening" carry the sense of the Orphic Night <note>A reference to Orphic cosmogony where Night is a primordial deity</note>. "Light" and "Day" refer to the heavens, while "Morning" refers to the ether. For the most ancient of the Latins used the word *manum* to mean "clear" or "bright," just as the Greeks use *aethrion* <note>original: "æthrion"</note>. From this, the word for morning (*mane*) is derived. Day indeed proceeds out of night, and morning depends upon both—not only before the creation of heaven and earth, but even before all eternity. These things have perhaps fallen from our lips too rashly, yet for the sake of our discussion, they seem to me to appear quite opportunely; you should not think there was any time in the memory of our fathers when our ancestors could have extricated these matters (and many others of this kind) so accurately, so properly, and with such firmness of opinion <unclear>before the incarnate fate</unclear>. But after that Word of God came into this flesh of ours to enlighten mankind, then the eyes of our mind were opened, so that we now recognize many things previously proclaimed by divine will almost more clearly than their own authors did—especially whatever pertains to the divinity of the Word. 

But let us return again to his names, from which it is necessary to select the one "Wonder-working" <term>Mirificum: The "Wonder-working" or "Miraculous" name, a central concept in Reuchlin's thought regarding the power of sacred language</term> name, in which alone we mortals, though established in nature, may rule over nature and over fate, and be able to perform portents, wonders, miracles, and the signs of divinity. And so that you may understand that various names have been attributed to that Word, I have decided that those which he holds in common with the Father and the Spirit should be commemorated. Then, those which are considered proper to his own divine subsistence; thirdly, those of the flesh; and finally, those of the Incarnate one. 

At this point, Sidonius, you cannot help but admire with me how much convenience Baruchias <note>Baruchias is the Jewish representative in this dialogue, providing Kabbalistic insight</note> brought us in his speech yesterday. I know this one thing: you will certainly congratulate me when you see me relieved of such a vast enumeration of names. For all things signifying divine ineffability, essence, abstraction, and relation—besides those signifying distinct power, operation, and likeness, and whatever follows or adheres to the supreme nature—are predicated of the Son of God (whose name is Wonder-working) and simultaneously of the entire Trinity. These we have decided to separate in this way: some of them do not indicate what the nature of divinity *is* (for that would be impossible according to human institutions), but rather what seems to be *around* it. These pertain to the "Essence" <note>original: "essentiam"</note>, which you, my Baruchias, have in my opinion already discharged copiously and abundantly. Let us refer the others, however, to the various management of the "Dispensation" <note>The divine plan or economy of salvation</note>. This latter root rises into two branches: into the arrangement of things to be done without a body, and into the state of the assumed flesh, that is, of the man. The former are attributed to the Word in common; the latter, properly. For "Being" <note>original: "Usias" / Greek: Ousia</note> and all the terms that fit him before the body was united to him are common to the supersubstantial and triune Trinity. Therefore, leaving aside those things which belong to Essence—as we were sufficiently instructed concerning them in yesterday's disputation—let us now attempt to recite in summary those which are believed to represent dignity and power. For "He Who Is," and "I Myself," and "Fire"...

# BOOK THREE

...you will consume, and the *Tetragrammaton* <note>The four-letter Hebrew name of God, YHWH, often considered too sacred to pronounce.</note> which signifies ineffability, seem to me to have been sufficiently explained by you, Baruchia, and with a sharper studious rigor than I possess, regarding what you considered to be of "substance." We cannot deny that "Lord," "King," and "God" signify imperial majesty, if we follow John of Damascus <note>An 8th-century Syrian monk and Church Father known for his systematic theology.</note>. Certainly, that famous Gregory of Nazianzus <note>A 4th-century Archbishop of Constantinople and influential theologian.</note> places these among the terms that are relative <note>original: "ad aliquid referuntur" — terms that require a relationship to something else to make sense.</note>. For we read "Lord of lords," "King of kings," "Holy of holies," and "God of gods." He considers only *On* <note>Greek: Ὁ Ὤν, meaning "The One Who Is" or "Being."</note>, that is "He who is," to be the most powerful name of God in and of itself. And although grammarians teach that "God" <note>original: "deus"</note> is a word changed from *theos*, which is believed to have descended from *Aethin* according to Plato—that is, "to burn"—since God is a consuming fire, from which it might seem to be a mark of substance; nevertheless, it is not of such a kind that it explains something according to itself without comparison to another. For the Lord said to Moses: "Thus shall you say to the children of Israel: The Lord God of our fathers, the God of Abraham, the God of Isaac, the God of Jacob has sent me to you; this is my name forever." Therefore, that voice awaits certain others of whom He is the God, just as a lord is a lord of subjects, and a king is set over inferiors; so He alone is the Most High and Exalted. "I said, you are gods and all sons of the Most High," and "the Sublime One inhabiting eternity." "Cause," "Prince," "Leader," "First and Last," "Alpha and Omega," "Beginning and End," "Head," and "Eye of Man"—each of these is understood as being relative to something. 

Truly, by His own power, God is "Wonderful," "Omnipotent," "Great," "Terrible," "Living," "Seeing," "Eternal," "Ancient of Days," "Abiding," "Strong," "Everlasting Life," and "Lord of Virtues." Likewise, God is judged by some to display "Energy" <note>In a theological sense, the "activities" or "operations" of God as distinct from His essence.</note>, those who are accustomed to derive *theos* from *thein*, that is, from "running through," "moving," or "penetrating." For this reason, the Lord speaks in Exodus: "Do not worship a foreign god; the Lord, 'Zealous' is his name, he is a jealous God." But also from other works—which Dionysius <note>Pseudo-Dionysius the Areopagite, a Christian Neoplatonist.</note> calls "effects" <note>original: "causata"</note> and Nazianzus calls "attributes" <note>original: "quae adsunt"</note>—God himself possesses surnames. Indeed, from the magnitude and beauty of the things made, it happens reasonably that the Craftsman is estimated. For the supreme Creator is not easily made manifest to us from His own substance, which has no form, but from those things which are present and from the very things effected. In this way He is called: "Worker of Wonders," "Man of War," "Crusher of Battles," "Protector," "Giver of Life," "Father of All," "Creator," "Redeemer," "Savior," and others of that kind. 

A "Likeness" follows which spreads widely, and which arrives from several comparisons, as when all good things—nutritive, corporeal, rational, legal, mental, and angelic—are cast together into one Author, He is called the "Sea of Goodness"; and from all conditions of living forced into one origin, He is called the "Fountain of Life"; and from all brightness, splendors, lights, and universal radiance thrust into one clarity, He is called the "Sun." For the divine philosopher wrote that what the sun is to sensible things <note>Things perceived by the senses.</note>, God is to intellectual things. Another kind of likeness is taken by the comparison of one to one and equal to equal, just as if I wished to compare the most divine Trinity to the clarity of the sun, I then compare any light of the former to any "subsistence" <note>original: "subsistentiam"</note> of the latter. For thus I have been accustomed to call the *hypostasis* <note>The distinct persons of the Trinity (Father, Son, Holy Spirit) as individual realities.</note>, as you will hear more clearly after this, most...

It draws on Platonic philosophy and Church Fathers to explain how God is known through His "energies" and effects rather than His formless substance.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical dialogue regarding the nature of the Divine Word. The speaker is refining technical definitions of "substance" versus "subsistence" to avoid theological confusion.</meta>

-># ON THE WONDER-WORKING WORD<-

<note>original: "DE VERBO MIRIFICO"</note>

In divine matters, I have chosen to use the term "subsistence" <note>Latin: *subsistentia*</note> rather than "substance" as Boethius <note>An influential 6th-century philosopher</note> did, because it seemed more suitable to the subject. I do this so I do not fall into the confusion of *Ousia* <note>Greek: "Essence" or "Being"</note>, which almost every translator customarily renders as "substance." I have decided not to be a mere craftsman of words, but an imitator of character; there will be no great danger in this business as long as the reality itself becomes clear through usage. You have certainly heard God called "the Sun" by the philosophers. Just as there is the globe of the sun, its ray, and its light—and all are one sun—so in God there is the Father, the Son, and the Spirit, and all are one God. Let the same be judged concerning the soul. For "God is a soul to us," as the poems say. There is also the soul of man, which consists of mind, reason, and sense. The mind illuminates the reason, and the reason flows into the sense; all are one soul. It is the center of nature, the middle of all things, the sequence of the world, the face of all, the measure and the bond of the world; by this, even if under an obscure image, it may be compared to God in whatever way possible. Furthermore, many attributes accompany the divine nature from which divinity receives its names, such as Unity, Truth, and Goodness. God is also said to be alone, always, and everywhere, understanding infinite things, understanding himself first and then individual things, acting upon all through his own being. He is named Love and Charity, immortal Providence; he is proclaimed as Wisdom, Beauty, Justice, Peace, and Perfection. Finally, there are a vast number of things which describe God much more truly through deprivation or negation <note>This refers to "Apophatic theology," defining God by what He is not (e.g., infinite/not-finite)</note>—not because of a defect in the subject, but because of his super-excellence, for God exceeds all speech. This entire enumeration tends toward this point: that you should consider all these titles to belong not only to God the Father and the Spirit, but also to the Word, who is the most beloved Son of God, the best and greatest, the everlasting God, ineffable, incomprehensible, the immovable eternity who is one in all, and the origin of all things that exist. For he himself is the creator of all, and through him the Father created all things; he is the same as the consuming fire, and the Four-Lettered Name <note>original: "Tetragrammatus," referring to the Hebrew name of God, YHWH</note>, the light, the day, Olympus, King of kings and Lord of lords, God of gods, Holy of holies, the God of Abraham, the God of Isaac, the God of Jacob. The only Most High, exalted, sublime, inhabiting eternity, the prince, the leader, the first and the last, Alpha and Omega <note>original: "Alpha & o"</note>, the beginning, the middle, and the end. The head and eye of man, perfection, peace, justice, beauty, wisdom, immortal providence, charity and love, understanding infinite things, understanding himself first and then individual things, acting upon all through his own being, always and everywhere. Unity and truth and goodness, soul, reason, sun, ray, the fountain of life, the sea of goodness, the cause, the father of all, creator, protector, savior, redeemer, a man of war, doing wonders, crushing wars. The life-giving Lord, the Zealous One, and the jealous God, God Almighty, admirable, great, terrible, living and seeing me, the Ancient of Days, remaining, strong, the Lord of virtues, and eternal life. With these and similar praises, the Word is celebrated in common with the other Persons, but he is specifically celebrated by this: that he is the Son, begotten without beginning. For he is said to have a Father, but not a beginning, and to be begotten from the "Always-Father" always and without suffering; one from one and uniquely, whom the whole shows as whole, and adequately demonstrates his nature. Whatever words aspire to this, whatever words suggest such a distinction, will be of the kind that investigate the most divine Word—which we, in the present discourse, treat as worthy of the highest praise.

<summary>Reuchlin explores the various names and attributes of God, arguing that titles like "Light," "Truth," and the "Tetragrammaton" apply specifically to the Word (the Son) as the mediator of creation. He emphasizes that God is best understood through "negation" because His excellence exceeds human language.</summary>
<keywords>Trinity, Substance vs Subsistence, Tetragrammaton, Apophatic Theology, Christology, Boethius, Neoplatonism, Divine Names</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the theological discussion from Book Two regarding the divine nature, specifically transitioning from the concept of the "Only-begotten in the bosom of the Father" to the "mark of his person."</meta>

# BOOK THREE

...will explain with a peculiar ornament, just as the only-begotten in the bosom of the Father, of whom we spoke before, and the mark of his person <note>original: "character hypostaseos"; the author is discussing the Greek term for a distinct individual existence within the Trinity</note> of which we promised to speak. For that very term, we read today in Paul's writings: "Who, being the brightness of his glory and the figure of his substance" <note>Hebrews 1:3</note>. This translation perhaps follows the custom by which *hypostasis* is translated as "substance" <note>original: "substantia"</note> from "standing under," rather than "subsistence" <note>original: "subsistentia"</note> from "standing by itself," even by certain learned men. We are instructed that it should be interpreted this way in the book *On the Two Natures*, which is attributed to Boethius <note>An influential 6th-century philosopher and theologian</note>. And yet, when he eventually translated the volumes of Aristotle into Latin, he similarly named *Ousia* <note>Greek: οὐσία, meaning being or essence</note> as "substance," thereby transgressing his own precepts. This instability pours no small cloud over the eyes of many, so that almost no students of Latin theology are able to bear this variety with a calm mind. It is full of weariness, or rather hatred, to interpret diverse conditions with a single-voiced explanation, especially in a matter that should not be adorned with oratorical dye and the little flowers of speech, but rather with the strength of truth and the firmness of discipline.

I beseech you to listen attentively and diligently to what the business is. The senate and the faithful people of the Christian commonwealth have decreed that it must be so felt and so professed: that there is only one true God—eternal, immense, unchangeable, incomprehensible, almighty, and ineffable—Father, Son, and Holy Spirit; three persons indeed, but one essence, substance, and nature, entirely simple. These are the very words of the decree; this is the law, this the edict, this the true rule of just religion by which we are taught that substance, essence, and nature are inwardly one and the same thing. 

However, "Hypostasis" in the highest divinity is not a little different; for there is but one and only one essence, nature, and substance. There are three *hypostases*, which even that same Boethius translated into Latin as "persons," and in one single *hypostasis* two substances consist <note>referring to the two natures of Christ, human and divine, in one person</note>. Therefore, *hypostasis* and substance shall not be the same in saying or in the same manner, just as person and substance are not. Now this is the Catholic faith: that we worship one God in Trinity, and Trinity in Unity, neither confounding the persons nor dividing the substance; in which we recognize that "substance" signifies one thing, and "hypostasis" another. For the Father, by generating the Son from eternity, gave him his own substance and nonetheless retained it; and the Son, by being born, received the substance of the Father. But neither did the former transfer his own *hypostasis* or proper person into the latter, nor did the latter assume it from the former. For they are both of two *hypostases* and two persons, yet truly of one and the same substance.

Wherefore, I could easily believe that Jerome <note>The 4th-century translator of the Vulgate Bible</note> in that passage translated *hypostasis* not as "substance" but rather as "subsistence," especially in a matter of such weight, where he perceived that no small difference lies hidden between *hypostasis* and *Ousia*. In the first volume of Luke and the fifteenth chapter of that book, he is found to have translated it equally and correctly as "substance." For according to our custom, *Ousia* is to be named "substance," but *hypostasis* "subsistence," so that when the Apostle says "the mark of his *hypostasis*," we should understand "the figure of his subsistence." For in what way would the word "mark," "seal," or "figure" refer to the paternal substance, since it is not merely similar but is truly that entire substance of the Father itself, and nothing else besides? Therefore, if the "mark" is different from that whose form it expresses—as Chrysostom <note>John Chrysostom, an early Church Father known for his eloquence</note> bears witness—it was necessary...

<summary>This page examines the linguistic and theological distinctions between "substance," "essence," and "hypostasis" (personhood) within the Trinity. The author critiques previous translations by Boethius and Jerome, arguing for a precise vocabulary to distinguish the shared divine nature from the distinct persons of the Father and Son.</summary>
<keywords>Trinity, Hypostasis, Substance, Essence, Boethius, Jerome, Christology, Latin theology, Greek terminology, Subsistence</keywords>

--- Page 103 ---
<meta>This page continues the theological exploration of the nature of the Son (the Word) and his relationship to the Father, specifically focusing on the titles and attributes ascribed to him in Scripture.</meta>

# BOOK THREE

The Son is said to be the character of the Father’s *hypostasis* <note>original: "hypostaseos"; here meaning the distinct person or individual existence</note>, that is, of his subsistence; he is not, however, the seal of the *Ousia* <note>original: "Vsias"; meaning the essence or substance shared by the Trinity</note>, that is, of the substance. For the Father, by generating, marked the person of the Son, just as the shape of a signet ring expresses the form of the seal, even as he himself taught concerning himself: "Work," he said, "not for the food which perishes, but for that which endures unto eternal life, which the Son of Man will give to you; for God the Father has sealed him." <note>John 6:27</note> He is named, furthermore, the brightness of clarity or glory, because he is light from light, as if indicating a communion of one essence, by which two subsistences <note>distinct persons</note> may more truly become known to us. Moreover, the Image or *Icon* <note>Greek: "eikon"</note>, although it is the total manifestation of the Father (as we revealed a moment ago), must by no means be understood as a separate thing from the reality, but as existing with the reality; indeed, it is not artificial but natural, born without time, the cause of eternity.

There follows: "The power of God and the wisdom of God," by which names our sun, Paul, writing to the Corinthians, designates him. "We," he says, "preach to those called, both Jews and Greeks, Christ the power of God and the wisdom of God." <note>1 Corinthians 1:24</note> For he possesses all power within himself in a supreme way, and possesses it beforehand as the author of all power, producing all things through a most firm and unlimited power. When the prophet had contemplated this with the eyes of his mind, he exclaimed: "Be exalted, O Lord, in your power." What shall we say of his wisdom, whose "foolishness" is wiser than men? For he is full of all wisdom and understanding, and of his wisdom there is no number. But he is also placed super-eminently above all reason, mind, and wisdom, being the giver of all wisdom and the cause of all intelligence, having proceeded from the head of God. For in him all the treasures of wisdom and knowledge are hidden.

Furthermore, since the Son of God names himself the Way, the Truth, and the Life, it will certainly befit us to venerate with religious worship those names which are the closest signs of the Almighty Creator, urging us toward the highest blessedness of our soul. "Blessed," says the prophet, "are the undefiled in the way." <note>Psalm 119:1</note> For they obtain the vision of God in a pure heart, which is the sole blessedness of men, the only happiness, which is granted to us through this one way. Let us hear from his own mouth: "No one," he says, "comes to the Father except through me." <note>John 14:6</note> Therefore, the way by which one goes to the Father is the Son; and for that reason he is the Life, since this gate, this path, this door is our science and knowledge of the Father. He is therefore the Truth, for otherwise it would be error, not science, not knowledge. He is also the Truth because he is One; for falsehood is many, not one. Perhaps these last two <note>Truth and Life</note> are common to the whole Trinity. But "the Way" seems to be a unique and proper name for the Son.

Many such forms of naming are contained in the sacred writings, diverse in appearance but noble in kind, which all harmonize toward this goal: that the Word of God, that is the Son, was alone begotten from the substance of the Father without beginning—the Only-Begotten. The character and figure of the Father's subsistence, the brightness of glory, the filial *hypostasis* and person of the Word, the seal of the Father, light from light, the Image or Icon, the power of God and the wisdom of God, the way, the gate, and the door. Now it remains to commemorate in a very brief discourse those things which follow his human nature. Therefore, although this Word is the eternal Son of God, begotten from the substance of the Father before the ages, he was nonetheless born in time from the substance of the Virgin, perfect God and perfect man, consisting of a rational soul and human flesh...

<summary>This page explains the divine titles of Christ (the Son), such as the "Image of God," "Wisdom," and "the Way," emphasizing his shared essence with the Father while maintaining his distinct personhood. It concludes by transitioning from his eternal divine nature to his temporal incarnation as a human.</summary>

<keywords>Christology, Hypostasis, Trinity, Wisdom of God, Only-Begotten, Image of God, Incarnation, Substance and Essence</keywords>

-># THIRD BOOK<-

->i ii<-

existing. Therefore, since he is man, you should think that nothing human is absent from him except sin; indeed, having been made all things for all people, he is whatever we are, except for sin. For this reason, he does not shrink from, nor disdain, nor refuse the manners and titles of a man. He is called the Son of Man, shepherd, high priest, obedient, servant, messenger, one sent, speaking nothing of himself, judging no one, not granting seats at his right or left, not doing his own will, ignorant of the day of completion <note>original: "consummationis diem," referring to the End Times</note>, subject to the one who subjects all things, staying awake all night in prayer, praying on the mountain, petitioning the Father, asking where they had laid Lazarus, advancing in wisdom, age, and grace before God and men, sleeping in a little boat, hungering after fasts, weary from travel, weeping, anxious, comforted by an angel, thirsting, carrying the cross, crucified, dead. Now I pass to his flesh, which was as a worm and not a man <note>A reference to Psalm 22:6, often interpreted as a prophecy of Christ's humiliation</note>, as the serpent in the desert, as a grain falling into the earth, as a vine, as a sheep, as a lamb, as a sacrifice, wrapped in a linen shroud, buried. You, therefore, who have such anxiety and care, and who are greedy for the performing of miracles, choose from this assembly, from this army, from this vast legion of sacred names, which one is most powerful to be used in operations of this kind.

Then **SIDONIUS** said: I willingly and gladly escape this duty of the priests and transfer it to you alone, who preside over today's disputation by order; or, if he desires it, it should be granted to this **BARUCHIAS** at the pulpit. For I, from my earliest years and from my "tender little nails" <note>A Latin idiom meaning from earliest childhood</note>, have been initiated into other sacred rites. I have known no majesty, no god, nothing excellent, except these brightest lights of the world which we see: the sun, the maker of the day, and the moon, the ornament of the night, or perhaps those other little lights which you call stars and constellations. And later, whenever I wished to be most deeply religious, there occurred to me those names of invisible spirits which Ennius <note>An early Roman poet</note> duly encompassed in these two verses: *Juno, Vesta, Minerva, Ceres, Diana, Venus, Mars, Mercury, Jove, Neptune, Vulcan, Apollo.* Or those which the most ancient Orpheus, writing to Musaeus, enumerates at greater length: Earth, Proserpina, Liber, the Foam-born <note>Venus/Aphrodite</note>, Hebe, Hercules, Nymphs, Pan, Memory, the Muses, the Graces, the Seasons, the Year, Latona, Dione, the Curetes, the Corybantes, Castor, Pollux, Themis, Night, Day, Faith, Justice, Rhea, Saturn, Tethys, Ocean, Eternity, Time, Styx, Foresight, spirits of the heavens, of the air, of the water, of the earth, of the underworld, of the fire, Semele, Bacchus, Ino, Leucothea, Palaemon, Victory, Adrastia, Aesculapius, Pallas, the Winds, Thunders, the Month, Adonis, the Furies, Hecate, and others whom that same grave author mentions in his hymns. However, while reading, at times when other matters were not pressing, I happened to slip into foreign things and crept like a scout into an enemy camp. Thus I read the Gospels and the books of the Christians.

And I also, said **BARUCHIAS**. For although it was with horror and trembling, Sidonius, I have also sometimes read of those demons of yours; yet I always judged that they were not so much to be read as to be neglected, because they were far removed from the tradition of our fathers. It did not agree with reason that something made by another should be called God. Thus, I more easily accepted that the Word of God is God, since he is one with God and without beginning, born together with the Father and from the Father before all eternity, and before all things.

<meta>This page continues a dialogue concerning the power of sacred names and the philosophical basis for miracles, specifically discussing the transition from natural philosophy to divine intervention.</meta>

-># ON THE WONDER-WORKING WORD<-
->## Book 1, Page 5<-

ages. For that which was born of the Virgin was not so much a matter of essence as of dispensation <note>The "dispensation" refers to God's plan or arrangement for human salvation through the Incarnation</note>. For He willed it so, and therefore it was so; He could have assumed another nature had He willed it. For the Lord has done all things whatsoever He willed in heaven and on earth. But since you give me the choice among so many names, as to which might seem most effective of all in the performance of miracles—though I have previously confessed that I know next to nothing of these things, since the power of the sacred names has not seen fit to accompany my ancestors down to this present age—how is it that in the memory of our fathers we read of great works performed above nature and above fate in the name of the **Tetragrammaton** <note>The four-letter Hebrew name of God, YHWH, considered too sacred to pronounce</note>? Unless it be that there are found among the Hebrews, as elsewhere in the world, sacrilegious men, sorcerers, and superstitious exorcists, whose minds are blocked by evil spirits in every nation. Therefore we pray you, **Capnion** <note>The Greek-style nickname for the scholar Johannes Reuchlin, meaning "smoky"</note>, proceed so that we may not remain ignorant of which among all the most sacred names is more powerful for miracles, which is more prompt, and which is most ready at hand. For **Sidonius** desires this along with me. Indeed, he says, I desire nothing else, and I pray you again and again to proceed.

At this, **CAPNION** said: It is manifest that neither the words of our voice, nor the signs of written characters, have any force, power, or command—not only over those effects which occur above nature, but not even over those whose mother is nature—unless they are joined by the virtue of a nobler operation. For **Aristotle**, in the book he published *On the Soul*, left it sufficiently proven to the Peripatetics <note>Followers of Aristotle's philosophy</note> that neither sound nor scent exerts any dominion over a body or works any effect in substances, except through some accidental quality. For we are composed of primary, not tertiary, qualities. Therefore, the nobler virtue by which words bring force to things beyond—indeed, above—nature and the perpetual course of the world, is not some temporary natural thing, but is itself above nature; for anything that acts under the guidance of nature produces something either similar to or inferior to itself, but does not work above its own species. 

Thus, since we agreed yesterday without controversy that the omnipotence of God alone is the creator of miracles—for "Blessed be the Lord God of Israel," as the King-Prophet <note>King David</note> prophesied, "who alone does great wonders"—we shall rightly perceive that God Himself has instilled into every wonder-working word a singular ray of His own omnipotence. This is the operative power of a miraculous effect brought down to us through the ministry of angels, just as we have found concerning certain figures or secret preparations attested by Holy Writ: *Make a bronze serpent and set it up as a sign; whoever is struck and looks upon it shall live.* <note>A reference to Numbers 21:8</note> Likewise: *If the spirit of jealousy stirs you, after the offering is made, the priest shall take holy water in an earthen vessel, and shall cast a little dust from the floor of the tabernacle into it. And when the woman stands in the sight of the Lord, he shall uncover her head and place upon her hands the sacrifice of remembrance and the offering of jealousy. He himself shall hold the most bitter waters in which he has gathered curses with execration, and he shall adjure her and say: "If no other man has slept with you, and if you are not defiled by deserting your husband's bed, these most bitter waters into which I have gathered curses shall not harm you. But if you have turned away from your husband and are defiled, and have lain with another, you shall be subject to these curses: May the Lord make you..."* <note>A translation of the "Ordeal of the Bitter Water" from Numbers 5</note>

<summary>The speaker Capnion argues that words and symbols have no inherent power over nature unless infused with a "ray of omnipotence" from God. He cites biblical examples, such as the bronze serpent and the ritual for a jealous husband, to show how physical objects can channel divine power.</summary>

<note>The following text describes the "Ordeal of the Bitter Water" from the biblical Book of Numbers, used to determine a wife's faithfulness.</note>

...into a curse, and an example for all among his people; may he make your thigh rot and your swelling womb burst. May these cursed waters enter into your belly, and as your womb swells, may your thigh rot. And the woman shall respond, Amen, amen. Then the priest shall write these curses in a small book and shall wash them out with the most bitter waters into which he has gathered the curses, and he shall give them to her to drink. When she has drained them, the priest shall take from her hand the <term>sacrifice of jealousy</term> <note>original: "sacrificium zelotypiæ"; an offering made by a husband who suspects his wife of adultery</note>, and he shall elevate it before the Lord and place it upon the altar; and thus he gives the woman the most bitter waters to drink. When she has drunk them, if she is defiled and, having despised her husband, is guilty of adultery, the waters of the curse shall pass through her, and with her belly inflated, her thigh shall rot, and the woman shall be a curse and an example to all the people. But if she has not been defiled, she shall be unharmed and shall bear children.

Let us also hear the divine teaching a third time: "Thus shall you bless the children of Israel and say to them: May the Lord bless you and keep you. May the Lord show his face to you and have mercy upon you. May the Lord turn his countenance toward you and give you peace. And they shall put my name upon the children of Israel, and I will bless them."

By such force of words, by that power of figures, by the virtue of such preparations, and by these songs <note>or "incantations"</note> of either curses or blessings and composed words, the thing itself is fashioned with marvelous artistry—not by us, but through us. For indeed, if it is necessary for a divine virtue to be enclosed within these names, and for an infallible divinity to hasten to help, and for a power that both surpasses and penetrates all laws of things to lie hidden within, then it will be necessary not to be ignorant. 

First: what is that name which is superior to all others—indeed, the only one remaining out of all of them—by whose secret energy and hidden assistance the whole business of wonder is completed? 

Second: how is it known that it is that very name, and no other besides it? 

Lastly: by what way of use may we approach what we desire? 

And this is indeed a great matter, and I know not if it be the greatest; yet it is easy enough, by degrees and with prudent and mature investigation, to thin out the cause of names like plucking wool, until you comprehend that which lies hidden and immediately prove it to be that which you desire. For from the vast heap of sacred words, you have received some that are <term>ineffable</term> <note>original: "ineffabilia"; names that cannot or must not be spoken aloud</note> and some that are <term>utterable</term> <note>original: "fabilia"</note>. It is a hard thing, if not impossible. But it is difficult for a voice to be made for us out of the ineffable which we may use obediently, or which might be ready and at hand for us in any case, by any affection, or in whatever dangers. Therefore, it remains that, led by necessity, we turn to scrutinizing the named voices with sharp judgment, if we might first investigate as briefly as possible the cause of such vast power and such great virtue of names through a slightly deeper study.

Wherefore it has seemed best to act more openly now. For just as the clemency of the sky, the rays of the planets, and the eternal fires of the stars are believed to effect something in <term>sublunary things</term> <note>original: "sublunaria"; the earthly realm below the moon, subject to change and decay</note> by their downward flow:

*Hence we are able to predict storms from a doubtful sky,*
*Hence the day of harvest and the time for sowing,*
*And when it is fitting to strike the treacherous sea with oars,*
*When to launch the armed fleets,*
*Or to fell the pine tree in the woods at the proper season.*
*Nor do we watch in vain the settings and risings of the signs,*
*And the year made equal by four diverse seasons.*
<note>The author is quoting Virgil's "Georgics," illustrating how celestial influence governs earthly timing.</note>

This is the work of nature, this is the <term>influence</term> <note>original: "influentia"; literally a "flowing in" of celestial power</note> of the rays; this is the descent of light, motion, and heat, penetrating all things with a subtle liveliness. Hence...

It transitions into a natural-philosophical explanation of "influence," comparing the power of sacred names to the way celestial bodies affect the earthly seasons and agriculture.</summary>
